PREFACE

OM NAMO BHAGAVATE SRI RAMANAYA

Lord Dakshinamurti is the Primal Guru in the long and
great teacher-student lineage guiding all earnest seekers. This
lineagecontinuing unbroken to the present day. The Guru’s
Grace and Compassion are bestowed not only on human
beings but also on animals, birds and plants; instances of
which are found in Ramayana and Bhagavatha Purana.

 In the first half of the last century also, the devotees
and the visitors who came to seek the blessings of Bhagavan
Sri Ramana Maharshi were witnesses to such happenings and
these have been recorded in various books. This book in
which a humble effort is made to compile all those recorded
incidents will show its readers how Guru Ramana all through
his life bestowed his Grace not only on human beings but
also equally on birds, animals and plants. The supreme
symbol of Non-dual Consciousness of this age, Bhagavan
Guru Ramana, knew no duality and never discriminated as
higher or lower form of life. His compassion for animals,
birds, and plants was of the highest order. Souls, inhabiting
forms other than human, could get the Grace of the Guru,
because of their devotion and diligent spiritual practices in
their previous births. Such evolved souls were spiritually
uplifted and liberated by Guru Ramana. Samadhis have been
built for them in Sri Ramanasramam.



iv

Both on the holy Hill of Arunachala and later at its
foothills, the animals found in him a great companion,
benefactor and guide. They received his love and Grace. As
for the Cow ‘Lakshmi’, the monkey ‘Nondipaiyan’, the white
peacock ‘Madhava’, and the dog ‘Jackie’, their devotion to
Bhagavan Ramana was unsurpassed.

May this book serve to inspire us all to surrender totally
to Sadguru Bhagavan Ramana and receive his Divine Grace.

Tiruvannamalai President
July 14, 2008 Sri Ramanasramam

CHAPTER – ONE

LIFE SKETCH OF BHAGAVAN
SRI RAMANA MAHARSHI

Bhagavan Sri Ramana Maharshi was born in Tiruchuzhi
on 30th December 1879, at 1: 00 a.m., on the auspicious day
of Ardra Darshanam. He was the second son of Sriman
Sundaram Ayyar, a local pleader, and his devout wife Srimathi
Alagammal. The child was named Venkataraman. He received
his early education in Tiruchuzhi and later in Dindigal.

Venkataraman was twelve, when his father passed away,
leaving his wife, three sons and a daughter. The family broke
up. Mother Alagammal with her youngest son and daughter
went to her brother-in-law’s house in Manamadurai.
Venkataraman moved to his uncle’s house in Madurai with
his elder brother, Nagaswamy. He pursued his studies at the
Scott Middle School and then at the American Missions High
School. Venkataraman a robust and healthy boy had an
excellent memory, and a clear and sharp intellect. Though
he did not seem particularly interested in his studies, but he
preferred sports and excelled in them.

The first noteworthy event in his early life occurred in
November 1895. One morning on his way to school he met
a relative and asked him where he was coming from. The
relative replied “From Arunachala”. This reply had a magical
effect on Venkataraman. He had heard the name before,
but on this occasion it thrilled him to the core.



2 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

The second noteworthy event was when he came across
a copy of the Periapuranam, containing the life stories of the
sixty-three Nayanmars, the Tamil Saivite saints. Young
Venkataraman was overwhelmed with bliss as he read the
lives of these devout Nayanmars, who lived only to praise
and serve Siva, and looked upon themselves as mere specks
of dust at the feet of the Lord.

Despite these two incidents Venkataraman was leading
a normal life until the middle of July 1896. At that time he
underwent a sudden transformation. One day he was sitting
alone in the first floor room of his uncle’s house in Madurai.
All of a sudden he was seized with a fright that he would die.
He felt that he was going to die, though he was in usual
robust health. He did not care to consult anyone and he
decided then and there to solve the problem himself.

To quote his own words:
“The shock made me at once introspective. I said to

myself mentally: ‘now death has come. What does it mean?
What is it that is dying? This body dies.’ I at once
dramatised the scene of death. I extended my limbs and
held them rigid as though ‘rigor mortis’ had set in. ‘Well
then’ I said to me, ‘this body is dead. It will be carried to
the burial ground, and reduced to ashes. But with the
death of the body, am ‘I’ dead? Is this body I? This body is
silent and inert, but I feel the full force of my personality
and even the sound of ‘I’ within myself – apart from the
body. So I am the deathless spirit, a thing transcending
the body’. All this was not a mere intellectual process. It
flashed before me vividly as the living Truth”1



Life Sketch of Bhagavan Sri Ramana Maharshi 3

Bhagavan narrated the above experience many years
later. He emphasised that though he described it step by step
the whole experience took place in a flash and that the
awareness of ‘I’was direct and Self luminous and not the
product of any thought.

After the ‘Death Experience’, Venkataraman lost all
interest in his studies, relationships, friends and sports. He
developed greater humility and meekness. Furthermore, he
gave up all likes and dislikes regarding food and would eat
with the utmost indifference.

Venkataraman would also go to the Meenakshi Temple
almost every evening. He would stand motionless for a long
time before the images of Siva, Meenakshi, Nataraja and the
sixty-three Saivite saints. Waves of emotion overwhelmed him
and tears would roll down from his eyes unawares.

Another six weeks passed in this way. His relatives
observed this change in the boy with resentment. The climax
came on 29th August 1896. Venkataraman had been given a
chapter from Bain’s Grammar to copy three times as an
imposition for not studying his lesson. He copied the chapter
twice and when he was copying it for the third time, a sudden
revolt rose in him. He bundled up the books and sat in a
meditation posture with his eyes closed.

Venkataraman’s elder brother, Nagaswamy, who was
watching all the while, rebuked him saying, “For one who
behaves in this way, what is the need of all this?” “Yes,”
thought Venkataraman, “What businesses have I here?”
Immediately the presence of Arunachala pervaded him. He
rose to his feet to leave his house renouncing everything then



4 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

and there. He told his brother that he had to go back to his
school to attend a special class, whereupon Nagaswamy told
him to take five rupees from the box below and pay his college
fees on the way.

Venkataraman considered this as a call from his father,
Arunachala to go to him. He scanned the atlas, noted the
railway route and surmising that three rupees would be
enough; he left the balance two rupees with a letter that
would be easily noticed and left for the railway station.

The letter read as below:
        in search of my father and
“I have ̂  in obedience to his command started from

here.
This is only embarking on a virtuous enterprise.
Therefore, none need to grieve over this affair. To

trace this out
no money need be spent.

Thus
______________

Your college fee has not
 yet been paid. Rupees two
enclosed herewith.” 2

It is interesting to note that, the letter begins with the
personal pronoun ‘I’ but soon passes on to the impersonal
pronoun ‘this’ – and finally the letter is left unsigned.

Venkataraman walked to the train station. He bought a
ticket to Tindivanam, which he thought was the nearest
railway station to Tiruvannamalai. He was not aware that



Life Sketch of Bhagavan Sri Ramana Maharshi 5

there was a train to Tiruvannamalai, as he had referred to an
old atlas. Luckily for him, the train, which was to depart at
12 noon, came late that day. He entered the compartment,
sat down and was immersed in meditation. A Muslim moulvi
told him that there was a train to Tiruvannamalai and for
that one had to change the train at Villupuram.
Venkataraman got down at Villupuram. He bought a ticket
to Mambalapattu with the money he was left with, and
decided to walk the rest of the distance to Tiruvannamalai.
When he reached Mambalapattu, he got down and walked
for 10 miles (16 km) in the hot sun and arrived at the
Arayaninallur Temple by evening. He sat in the pillared hall
of the temple immersed in meditation. He experienced a
vision of dazzling light pervading the whole place. When it
was time for the temple to close, he asked the pujari (the
temple priest) for food. The priest told him that he could get
food at the Kilur Temple, which was about six furlongs
(1 km) away. He accompanied the pujari to the Kilur temple.
But after puja, he did not get any food either there. He was
very tired and hungry. A temple drummer gave
Venkataraman his share of food, and someone led him to a
pandit’s house for water. While waiting for water,
Venkataraman fainted, dropping the food and scattering the
same on the ground. When he regained consciousness, he
picked up the scattered rice, ate some of it, and then slept.

Next day was 31st August, Gokulashtami. It was a
Monday. A pious couple of Kilur, Muthukrishna Bhagavatar
and his wife took pity on Venkataraman and took him to
their home. They gave him not only the food to eat but also



6 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

a packet of sweets. Since he had no money, Venkataraman
pledged his ruby ear studs for four rupees with
Muthukrishna Bhagavatar so that he could reach
Tiruvannamalai. Since there was no train until the next
morning, Venkataraman spent the night in the railway
station. On 1st September 1896, three days after leaving
Madurai, Venkataraman boarded the train in the early hours
of the morning and reached Thiruvannamalai, his father’s
abode, before noon.

With quick steps and a pounding heart, Venkataraman
went straight to the Arunachaleswara temple. As if the father
was welcoming his son, all the gates, and doors of the temple
were open. Not a soul was present. Without any hindrance,
he entered the innermost shrine. And in the bliss of union
between father and son, the quest was achieved and the
journey ended.

Leaving the temple, Venkataraman walked about
aimlessly and reached Ayyan Kulam tank to the east of
Arunachala. He noticed that he still had the packet of sweets.
“Does this need the sweets too?” he thought and threw it into
the tank. As he was returning from the tank somebody asked
him whether he would be interested in having the head
tonsured. He assented and the stranger took him to the house
of a barber and his head was tonsured. Standing on the steps
of a tank, he tore a small piece of cloth from his dhoti to
make a kaupinam (loin cloth) and threw the remaining cloth
into the water along with the sacred thread (symbol of a
Brahmin) and his remaining money. While returning to the
temple, there was a short but intense shower of rain that



Life Sketch of Bhagavan Sri Ramana Maharshi 7

gave him a bath and thereby completed the ritual of
becoming a Sannyasi.

Venkataraman then took up residence in the Thousand
Pillared Mantapam in the Arunachaleswara temple. When
urchins started troubling him, he moved into a deep pit in
the mantapam known as the ‘Pathala Lingam’ where few had
courage to enter since the place was infested with vermin
and insects. Brahmana Swami as he was called now, sat there
completely absorbed in the Self. He was in deep meditation
and oblivious of his body and of the world around him. The
vermin and insects dwelling in the Pathala Lingam found
that Brahmana Swami was a convenient prey and fed upon
the lower parts of his body. Yet, Brahmana Swami continued
to sit there without moving, absorbed in meditation.

Seshadri Swami, a saint who had come earlier to
Tiruvannamalai, recognised the greatness of Brahmana
Swami, and often tried to ward off mischievous boys, but
mostly failed. One day some local people descended into the
Pathala Lingam at Seshadri Swami’s request. They were all
amazed when they discovered a young boy deep in samadhi
with his thighs full of blood and pus from the insect bites.
They bodily lifted him out of the pit and placed him by the
Subramania shrine, and the young Swami appeared to be
oblivious of what was happening. The intensity of the young
Swami’s apparent tapas earned him the respect of the local
sadhus who began to look after his needs. The Swami stayed
for three months in several places in the temple.

His first attendant, Uddandi Nayinar, came to the Swami
while he was staying in the Mangai Pillayar temple. When



8 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

the number of visitors increased, it disturbed the Swami, so
he moved to Gurumurtham, a hall in a field just outside the
town and stayed there for eighteen months. There he had a
regular attendant called Palani Swami. Devotees started
coming and offered food to young Swami. All this time, ever
since he came to Thiruvannamalai, he was in mauna, as he
had no need to talk. It was at Gurumurtham that the young
Swami, for the first time revealed his identity to one of his
devotees who was insistent, by writing on the wall in English,
with a piece of charcoal: “Venkataraman, Tiruchuzhi.”

Brahmana Swami and Palani Swami moved to a
neighbouring mango grove and stayed there for about six
months. In the mango grove he acquired his knowledge of
scriptures to help Palani Swami, who had borrowed some
books and was struggling to understand them. The Swami
easily committed these works to memory and summarised
their content for the astonished attendant. Later on he learnt
Sanskrit, Telugu, and Malayalam while explaining spiritual
texts written in these languages.

Brahmana Swami’s uncle Nelliappier came to know that
his nephew was a revered saint in Tiruvannamalai. He came
to the mango grove with a friend and at first they were not
allowed to enter because the owner wanted to strictly limit
the number of visitors. Nelliappier sent a chit to his nephew
and gained admittance. Nelliappier was surprised to see an
unkempt, unwashed youth sitting aloof, with matted hair
and long nails. His uncle pleaded with the Swami to return
with him, so that his needs could be attend to. But he had to
return disappointed.



Life Sketch of Bhagavan Sri Ramana Maharshi 9

Brahmana Swami’s mother Alagammal3 visited him when
he was in Pavalakunru, a temple on one of the eastern spurs of
Arunachala. The mother cajoled her son, wept and pleaded
with him to return with her. But the son remained unmoved.
When pressed for an answer, the Swami wrote on a paper:

“The Ordainer controls the fate of souls in
accordance with their past deed – their Prarabdha karma.
Whatever is destined not to happen, will not happen –
try how hard you may. Whatever is destined to happen
will happen, do what you may to stop it. This is certain.
The best course therefore is for one to be silent.” 4

Mother Alagammal returned to Manamadurai with a
heavy heart.

In 1900, Brahmana Swami moved to Virupaksha Cave
located on the eastern slope of Arunachala. Here in 1907,
the famous Sanskrit scholar Kavyakanta Ganapathi Muni
climbed the hill in the hot sun, and fell flat on his face before
the Swami who was sitting alone on the veranda of the cave.
The Muni clasped both the feet of Swami in his hands and
quivering with emotion he cried:

“All that has to be read, I have read. Even Vedanta
Sastra I have fully understood. I have performed japa to
my heart’s content. Yet still up to this time I have not
understood what tapas is. Hence I have sought refuge at
thy feet. Pray enlighten me about the nature of tapas.” 5

The young Brahmana Swami gazed at the Muni for some
time and broke his silence by saying:

“If one watches whence the notion ‘I’ arises, the
mind is absorbed into that; that is ‘Tapas’. When a



10 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Mantra is repeated, if one watches the Source from which
the mantra sound is produced, the mind is absorbed in
That, that is Tapas” 6

The answer not only satisfied the Muni, but also filled
him with joy. He declared henceforth that the young
Brahmana Swami, who had been named ‘Venkataraman’ by
his elders, should be known as Bhagavan Sri Ramana
Maharshi. The devotees started addressing the young Swami
as BHAGAVAN, which means ‘The Divine’ or simply ‘God’.

In 1911, the first western devotee Mr. F.H. Humphreys
came to Bhagavan and wrote about the teachings of
Bhagavan in a journal called The International Psychic Gazette,
published in England.

In 1916, Bhagavan’s mother Alagammal came and stayed
permanently with Bhagavan who was then living in Virupaksha
cave. She had lost her eldest son Nagaswami and her daughter-
in-law, the wife of her youngest son Nagasundaram. As she
had no one else to look after her in her old age, Alagammal
went to Bhagavan, who did take care of her but showed no
special privilege as his mother. Nagasundaram soon joined
Bhagavan and his mother and later became a sannyasi under
the name Niranjanananda Swami.

Soon after the arrival of his mother and younger brother,
Bhagavan moved from Virupaksha cave to Skandasramam,
a little higher up the hill, and directly above Virupaksha cave.
There was a spacious cave, where a devotee called
Kandaswami himself built a small hermitage for Bhagavan.
Earlier Bhagavan had discovered a perennial spring next to
the cave. Kandaswami planted mango and coconut plants in



Life Sketch of Bhagavan Sri Ramana Maharshi 11

the hermitage. In recognition of Kandaswami’s labour,
Bhagavan insisted that the Ashram be called Skandasramam.

Bhagavan watched and guided the mother, weaned her
away from worldly attachment and moulded her for the
spiritual path so that she could attain the final destination in
life- liberation from the cycle of birth and death.

Alagammal attained her Maha Samadhi in May 1922.
Bhagavan was with her till the end. The body of the mother
was buried at the foot of the Arunachala hill, on the southern
slope by the devotees between Palithirtham and the temple
of Dakshinamurthi. A large crowd had gathered to pay her
their last respects. A sacred Lingam was installed over her
Samadhi. Later, a temple was built called ‘Mathrubhuteswara’,
the Lord manifested as Mother.

After the event, when somebody remarked that mother
had passed away, immediately Bhagavan corrected, “No.
Mother has merged. She has become one with the total.” And
still on another occasion Bhagavan observed, “Where has
mother gone? She is still here.”

Bhagavan’s brother Niranjanananda Swami, also known
as Chinna Swami, stayed near his mother’s samadhi and took
care of the daily pooja. Bhagavan often came down from
Skandasramam to visit his mother’s samadhi. One evening
in December that year, he came down from Skandasramam
and stayed permanently near mother’s samadhi. The devotees
followed him and then buildings came up. Thus was founded
the present ‘Sri Ramanasramam’.

In 1930, Mr. Paul Brunton, a western seeker visited
Bhagavan. He wrote in his book A Search in Secret India



12 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

about the experience he had in India and also about
Bhagavan. People all over the world were introduced to
Bhagavan and his teachings, through this book. Devotees
from different parts of the world started coming to
Thiruvannamalai to have darshan of Bhagavan.

Bhagavan taught vichara, self-enquiry – He asked
people to enquire into the source from which the notion of
‘I’ arises. He said, “Self enquiry is the one infallible means,
the only direct one, to realize the unconditioned, absolute Being
that you really are.” 7

In Bhagavan’s presence devotees got peace of mind and
they discovered that most of their doubts were resolved
without even having to put any questions to Bhagavan.
Though Bhagavan’s teaching was mainly through the
tremendous power of Spiritual Silence, he also answered
verbally too when devotees solicited.

The literary works of Bhagavan were mostly written
when requested to do so by his devotees. Some of his works
were spontaneous outpourings of devotion for his father
Arunachala like the Saivite Saints (bhakti) and other works
were more in line with the non-duality of Advaita Vedanta
(jnana). But in his presence Bhakti (the path of devotion)
and Jnana (the path of knowledge) became one and the same.
Bhagavan also translated some of Adi Shankara’s works into
Tamil. Bhagavan’s Collected Works were published in Tamil,
and translated into many different languages.

Besides Bhagavan’s biographies, the happenings in the
hall in his presence have been written as diaries and
reminiscences by several of his devotees. In all these books



Life Sketch of Bhagavan Sri Ramana Maharshi 13

one can see his compassion, love, care and equality of
treatment for all beings whether they were humans, animals,
birds, insects, or plants. Bhagavan never led a secluded life;
he was accessible to all, even to animals and birds, at any
time of the day or night. There was no discrimination between
the higher or the lower forms, or the rich and poor. He had
Samadarshan, equal vision towards all beings.

Early in 1949, a small nodule appeared below the elbow
of Bhagavan’s left arm. In the beginning, it was not considered
serious. But when operated on later, it was found to be
sarcoma. He remained calm and poised, submitting his body
to the treatment as if the body was not his. He acted purely
as a witness to the body. Bowing to the wishes of his devotees,
he allowed himself to be treated with different systems of
medicines but none succeeded.

Till the last day, although weak, Bhagavan gave darshan
to his devotees, who filed past the open door of the room
where he was staying. Due to the mammoth congregation of
people, a person could not have had more than a second or
two of his darshan. But during these silent darshans devotees
felt Bhagavan’s ever-flowing Grace and compassion.

Arthur Osborne describes Bhagavan’s Mahanirvana
on 14th April 1950 in his book Ramana Maharshi and
the Path of Self Knowledge:

“After darshan in the evening the devotees did not
disperse to their homes as usual. Apprehension held them
there. At about sunset Sri Bhagavan told the attendants
to sit him up. They knew already that every movement,
every touch was painful, but he told them not to worry



14 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

about that. He sat with one of the attendants supporting
his head. A doctor began to give him oxygen, but with a
wave of his right hand he motioned him away. ….
Unexpectedly, a group of devotees sitting on the veranda
outside the hall began singing ‘Arunachala Shiva’
(Aksharamana Malai). On hearing it, Sri Bhagavan’s eyes
opened and shone. He gave a brief smile of indescribable
tenderness. From the outer edges of his eyes tears of bliss
rolled down. There was one more deep breath and no
more. There was no struggle, no spasm, and no other sign
of death. Only the next breath did not come.

It was 8:47 pm. and exactly at that moment an
enormous star trailed slowly towards the peak of Arunachala
in the Northeast direction. Many witnessed it in many places,
as far as Delhi and Sri Lanka.

Bhagavan had earlier told a grieving devotee who
asked what would become of them without Bhagavan,
“You attach too much importance to the body …They say I
am going away. Where could I go? I am here.”8 This clearly
implied that the end of his body would not interrupt his
Grace and guidance. So the very air of Sri Ramanasramam
will always be permeated by his presence. The peace that
abides at the Ashram is not a passive peace but a vibrant
exhilaration. His presence is not confined only to
Tiruvannamalai now as then. Wherever the devotees may
be, they find Bhagavan, their Guru, his Grace, support,
inner peace and inner presence as potent as ever. Even
now, as before, the solace of a visit to Sri Ramanasramam
sinks deep into the soul. Visiting and staying in the Ashram



Life Sketch of Bhagavan Sri Ramana Maharshi 15

confers a bliss that can only be experienced; it can hardly
be described in words.

Bhagavan Sri Ramana Maharshi is ever present as our
inner Guru. He is eternally residing as the timeless, spaceless
inner Atma in the hearts of his devotees.

CHAPTER – TWO

BHAGAVAN AND THE ANIMAL DEVOTEES

“In a body, a dull cowherd, a monkey or dog, a
knave, a scholar or a devotee, everywhere he beholds the
same being and is without the least partiality” 1

(Ramana Gita, Chapter 18, Verse 22)

A devotee asked a Mahatma “Swami! How do we know
a Jnani?”

The Mahatma answered,“One who has a balanced
tranquil mind in any kind of situation, one who has universal
vision of equality towards all beings; one who has love, care,
compassion and solicitude for all beings; irrespective of whether
it is a plant, animal, bird, insect or human being, he is a Jnani.”

Devotees and visitors to Sri Ramanasramam experienced
all these qualities in Bhagavan on various occasions in his
presence. Not only human beings, but also animals and birds
would be totally free, fearless and peaceful when Bhagavan
was around.

Lakshmi the cow, Jackie the dog, the blessed crow and
Valli the deer were all liberated by Bhagavan’s blessings. At
Sri Ramanasramam, the animals that were liberated by
Bhagavan’s abundant Grace were properly buried and
samadhis were erected over them. Bhagavan even wrote a
Tamil poem (Venba) on Lakshmi the cow in which he said
that she attained Mukti.



Bhagavan and the Animal Devotees 17

It is said that if a human being dies without realising the
Self, he will undergo the cycle of birth and death according
to his karma, that is, the good or bad deeds that he has
performed in his earthly life. For human beings there are
three types of karma. Prarabdha karma or one’s destiny,
agamya karma, the new karma that has accumulated during
earthly life according to his deeds, and sanchita karma, which
is karma accumulated from previous births. Whether one
will go to a higher or lower birth is decided by one’s prarabdha
karma i.e. part of sanchita karma.

It is said that except human beings, other forms of life like
animals, birds, plants etc. do not accumulate new karma because
they do not have reasoning power like human beings. They only
exhaust their prarabdha karma and do not accumulate agamya
karma. Their sanchita karma remains as it is.

It is generally believed in Hinduism that in order to get
liberated from the cycle of birth and death, one must acquire
a human birth. But Bhagavan has indicated that a fully
ripened soul may take the body of an animal, plant, bird or
any creature to exhaust the remaining karma. We have read
in the Puranas and the Epics about the liberation of animals
from Samsara by the Lord. In Ramayana, Jatayu the bird
received liberation in the hands of Lord Rama, Gajendra
the elephant by Lord Vishnu in Bhagavatam and it is said
that even a thorny plant was liberated in Chidambaram by
the saint Umapathi Sivam.

Bhagavan had equal and unbiased consideration for all
beings, whose destiny had brought them in contact with him.
His vision was universal. He treated animals and birds with



18 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

the same love and compassion as he had for human beings
and, in turn, they had the same love and devotion towards
him. They were attracted to him just as human beings were.
He would not brook the least hurt inflicted on them and
even upbraided devotees for plucking flowers or for beating
tree branches to make the fruits fall.

Bhagavan, prior to moving on to the Arunachala hill, lived
in Gurumurtham for eighteen months. Already by that time
squirrels and birds were drawn to him. Birds built their nests
near and around him. In those days Bhagavan seemed oblivious
of the world around him as he sat still and silent all the time,
almost like a statue. Nevertheless, he was keenly observant of the
happenings around him. He had a deep love for nature.

In later years when Bhagavan moved to Sri
Ramanasramam, he used to recall how a bird had built a
nest in the mud next to where he sat in Gurumurtham. It
laid its eggs in the nest and hatched them. When the fledglings
grew, they flew away with the mother bird. Later, a squirrel
occupied the same nest. Bhagavan also remembered the life
cycle of hornets which he had keenly observed and used his
observation to explain certain Vedantic concepts.

When Bhagavan moved on to the Arunachala hill,
Palaniswami became his companion, devotee and attendant.
Gradually devotees and visitors started coming to him. A great,
divine power in Bhagavan attracted not only human devotees
but also animals and birds as well, just as a magnet attracts iron.
When he was in Virupaksha cave all types of birds and beasts
were visiting him. Squirrels, parrots and sparrows perched on
nearby trees, tried to converse with him in their own languages.



Bhagavan and the Animal Devotees 19

Monkeys came in groups and camped near Virupaksha cave.
Peacocks spread their feather in a fan shape and danced before
him. Dogs lounged in the sun near him; wild animals came to a
water spring near the cave, drank water, looked at Bhagavan
announcing their coming and going with a roar.

Not only did animals and birds came to Bhagavan but
even snakes, scorpions, lizards and spiders were drawn to him.
Bhagavan was in close association with all creatures, as he
understood their languages, gestures and emotions. He was
sarvajna, the Omniscient, the all knower. All creatures, in turn,
understood his language, gestures and glances. They obeyed
him accordingly. That was how a pleasant companionship and
friendship developed between Bhagavan and the animals. This
relationship was not an ordinary one. There was divinity in it.
Animals and birds knew that Bhagavan was their benefactor.
They were not scared of him. As he was fearless they too were
fearless. They knew he loved and cared for them.

The devotee Suri Nagamma, the author of Letters from
Sri Ramanasramam, once told Mrs. and Mr. Greenblatt, how
even animals lost body consciousness in Bhagavan’s presence:

“Once, in the hall in Ramanasramam, a lady was
playing the veena and singing along with it beautifully.
The devotees and visitors were completely immersed in
the music. Then Suri Nagamma noticed a wonderful sight.
The cow Lakshmi was completely stretched out in front
of Bhagavan in a state of absorption. Madhava, the white
peacock had spread his feather like a fan and danced with
joy, while the Ashram dogs sat motionless. It was a scene
of ancient days of a Rishi Ashram.”



20 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Suri Nagamma felt she was lucky to be there to witness
such a scene.2

Bhagavan never referred to or addressed animals as
‘it’ in neuter gender as commonly done. He referred to
them as ‘he’ or ‘she’ and never treated them as inferior to
human beings. Bhagavan said:

“Though the living beings are of different shapes, sizes,
and colours outwardly, they are simply the expressions of
the supreme intelligence which is one without the second
and never divided. That is, they are all one and the same
without difference, the all-pervading Brahman to the core.”

When Bhagavan asked, “Have the boys been fed?” a new
visitor would think he was enquiring about young boys. But
actually he was referring to the Ashram dogs that would
answer the whistle and come to take their meal.3

“Lakshmi has come. Give her some iddlies,” he would say,
looking through the window and it would not be a young
girl, he was talking about, but the Ashram cow Lakshmi,
who would come and stand in front of him.4

If Bhagavan said, “She has come with her baby. Give her
some fruits”, it would be a scene where a mother monkey,
holding her little one to her bosom, would be standing at
the window near Bhagavan’s couch looking at Bhagavan with
all her love and devotion.5

He would say, “Kamala take this newcomer devotee for
giripradakshina” and the newcomer would be expecting a
girl as a guide, but she would be Kamala the dog, who would
take him round the hill stopping at the places of interest and
bring him back to the Ashram.6



Bhagavan and the Animal Devotees 21

Since the time the Ashram was started, to the present
day, the sadhus who come as Narayana are first fed, then the
animals and birds and finally the devotees and visitors inside
the dining hall. Whatever food there is equally distributed
among all and shared by all alike.

There were no superior or inferior beings, including
animals that got their equal share. Devotee Arthur Osborne
writes that he was surprised to see Bhagavan tasting a mango
fruit between meals. As the mango season had just started,
he had tasted it to see if the fruit was sweet or sour, before he
gave it to the white peacock.

Pascaline Mallet, a French woman who came to seek
Bhagavan’s blessings in 1936 wrote in her book, Turn Eastward,
Glimpses into Indian Life, how Bhagavan had equality for all
beings:

“In Maharshi’s eyes all are equal. Moreover, this is not
limited to human beings but includes the animal kingdom
as well. There are many beautiful and touching stories
testifying to his love and compassion for our younger
brothers. In his presence wild animals forget to fight or kill
each other and do no harm to man. A cobra used to come
into the hall without ever attacking anyone and even stayed
peacefully beside its most bitter enemy, a peacock, and
another inmate of the Ashram. Maharshi forbids ill
treatment or killing of any living thing, and all unconsciously
feel that here they are perfectly safe. The little black-striped
squirrels run in and out, birds build their nests on the rafters,
and monkeys peep in occasionally to steal some food. When
an animal inmate of the Ashram is about to die, Maharshi



22 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

will attend to it in the same manner he does to a human
being, sitting beside it till the end with a hand on its head.
He sees the same life in all, although he recognizes
differences in the outer forms. His vision of equality is based
on the realization of the one behind the many.”7

Animals had complete faith in Bhagavan. They felt his
Grace. Wild animals, birds and monkeys would bring their
young ones and leave them in his care. Usually if human
beings caressed a wild animal, its own kin would boycott it
on its return to them. But if they were touched by Bhagavan
and returned back to their clan, they did not boycott them;
rather they seemed to honour them. They felt that Bhagavan
had blessed their young ones.

Animals knew Bhagavan loved them, as he was always
calm and poised. There was no room for anger at all. They did
not fear him nor did they feel threatened by him. They thought
of him as one of them. They identified themselves with him.

On the last day, before Bhagavan left his physical body,
the doctors thought the pain of sarcoma wound must be
terrible. But still Bhagavan was so concerned about animals
that when two peacocks screeched on a nearby tree, he asked
whether they had received their food. The pathetic cry of
the white peacock sitting on the roof of the room where
Bhagavan was lying down would melt anybody’s heart. Our
Bhagavan was the dearest friend of all beings.

To the peacocks, dogs, squirrels, cows, birds, monkeys
and all other animals including wild animals, he was a teacher,
a father, a friend, a relative, a well-wisher and everything on
this earth.



Bhagavan and the Animal Devotees 23

Sri Morarji Desai, then the Prime Minister of India,
presiding over the 99th Jayanthi celebration of Bhagavan at
Sri Ramana Kendra, New Delhi on 13th January 1979, said:

“ The Maharshi seemed to know everything. He
knew the language of animals. He listened to their
complaints. He treated every being with love, whether it
was a cow, a dog, a crow or a monkey. All were equal in his
eyes, the beggar or a millionaire. He never went out of
Tiruvannamalai. He refused to go out and preach. He
said, ‘If I am a Jnani, I consider everybody else as Jnani
too. What is there to teach?’ He regarded every being as
himself.” 8

Sri Morarji Desai visited Bhagavan and sat in front of
him in 1935. The visit left an abiding impression on him
about Bhagavan.

The devotees and distinguished visitors who came to
seek Bhagavan’s blessings were dumbfounded by his universal
vision of equality. They have exclaimed with wonder that they
had not seen the like of this anywhere else.

We are fortunate to have Bhagavan as our inner Guru
and to be blessed by him.



24 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

CHAPTER – THREE

BHAGAVAN’S SATSANG

T.R.A. Narayana relates how he witnessed, quite by
chance, Bhagavan’s satsang with some animals and how
deeply this encounter impressed him:

“The year was 1948. I was then in my thirty-ninth year.
I lived in Madras with my wife and four children. I was the
branch manager of a large British firm, and being in happy
circumstances, I did not find the need for any religious
practices or spiritual enquiries; I was content with enjoying
the good things in life.

“I was on a tour of small towns, with one of the
inspectors under me, Sri Parthasarathi. It was a hot April
day. As Sri Parthasarathi and I were boarding the train at
Villupuram to go to Tiruvannamalai, we noticed a young man
of about twenty-five trying to enter the first class
compartment by the next door. The man was so fat that he
heaved his bulky body this way and that, while another man
on the platform, obviously his servant, pushed him in through
the door. He was also ashamed by the curious way the people
on the platform, including Sri Parthasarathi and myself,
watched his predicament. He got in somehow and occupied
the cubicle next to ours.

“When the train had run for some minutes, the man
came to our cabin, introduced himself as Ratilal Premchand
Shah and started talking. Sri Ratilal, a Saurashtra Vaishya,



26 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

the only son of his father, a rich merchant of the place was
born and brought up in Gondal. He had married six years
ago. Cursed with so much fat in his body from his tenth year,
now at twenty-five, he was a huge mass of flesh and misery.
How he wished to get rid of his fat and be a man!

“In the last week of March, Sri Ratilal had a vision while
he was asleep at night. He saw an ascetic smiling and
beckoning him. The smile and the beckoning persisted for a
long time and stood clearly before Sri Ratilal’s mental eye
when he awoke. He did not speak to anyone about the vision.
Two days later, his wife was reading a Gujarati magazine.
Looking over her shoulders he saw the picture of the ascetic
he had seen in his vision. He came to know that the ascetic
was Bhagavan Sri Ramana Maharshi. He at once went to his
father and arranged for his journey to Tiruvannamalai with
the trusted family servant. All he knew about Bhagavan was
what that Gujarati article had said. But he felt sure that his
suffering would end as soon as he reached Bhagavan; the
smile and the beckoning of his vision of Bhagavan had given
him that firm faith. Sri Parthasarathi had seen Bhagavan many
times before and had also read a good deal about him. He
and Sri Ratilal talked about Bhagavan during the entire two-
hour journey. I was apparently reading an English novel,
but heard their conversation with interest and attention.

“At Tiruvannamalai station, a local merchant with whom
his father had arranged for his stay received Sri Ratilal. Sri
Parthasarathi and I proceeded to the travellers’ bungalow.

“It was four when we had our bath and tiffin. Sri
Parthasarathi knew that I was very businesslike and would



Bhagavan’s Satsang 27

not waste a single minute. He said we could visit the market.
I said: ‘No, Parthasarathi! We shall go and have darshan of
Maharshi first. Then, if time permits, we shall go to the temple.
“Let business wait!’ He was very surprised at my reply.

“It was about five when Sri Parthasarathi and I entered
the Ashram. Going round Bhagavan’s Mother’s samadhi, we
came to the veranda by its side. About fifty people were sitting
there, Sri Ratilal, his host and his servant included. Bhagavan
was not on his couch as usual. The visitors talked in whispers,
trying to find out where he was.

“After waiting for some ten minutes and finding that
Bhagavan had not come to his seat, Sri Parthasarathi suggested
to me that we could in the meantime go around and see the
goshala and other places.

“Finishing our inspection we were returning to the
veranda by another side, when we heard a childish voice say
‘Chee, asatthe!’ (Fie, you creature)!. We could see no children
around, and, therefore, peeped to find out the source of the
voice. We observed movement among the leaves of the brinjal,
lady’s-finger and other plants in the kitchen garden near the
veranda. Looking more intently, we saw a small goat, a little
monkey and a squirrel and Bhagavan Ramana Maharshi!
Bhagavan was sitting on his haunches with his legs folded up
to his breast. The goat nestled between his knees; the monkey
had its head resting on his right knee; the squirrel was perched
on his left knee. Holding a paper packet in his left palm,
Bhagavan picked groundnuts from it with his right-hand
fingers, one by one, and fed the goat, the monkey and the
squirrel, and himself, by turns. His remarks appeared to have



28 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

been addressed to the monkey, which had tried to snatch the
nut he was going to place between the squirrel’s lips. As we
watched, the four companions went on enjoying the eating.
All four seemed to be equally happy; the way they looked at
one another and keeping close together was touching. The
goat, the monkey, the squirrel, and Bhagavan, had obviously
forgotten their differences! And we too, looking on, saw all
the four only as good friends despite the differences in their
forms. No words could describe the feelings that passed through
my being at the sight. The vision of the Transcendent appeared
as a flash of lightning, and revealed to me the essence of being,
awareness and bliss, sat-chit-ananda.

“The nuts were over. Bhagavan threw the paper away
and said: ‘Pongoda!’ (Go away, you fellows!), just as any old
man would speak to his grandchildren. The goat, the monkey,
and the squirrel left. Bhagavan made to get up. Sri
Parthasarathi and I hurried away, feeling guilty of trespassing
into the Divine, but not sorry.

“Soon after Sri Parthasarathi and I had resumed our
seats in the veranda, Bhagavan came to his couch. I cannot
say he looked at us. He stood facing us, his eyes fixed on
something far above and beyond anything on earth. They
were like screens, which shut the material world off from the
light, which was burning behind them. Sparks of light shot
out through the fibres of the screen at times, sparks which
cooled the eyes on which they fell, pierced the gross coverings,
and lighted the wick inside them.

“Bhagavan reclined on the pillows in the couch,
supporting his head on his left palm. We all sat down to look



Bhagavan’s Satsang 29

at his face. We sat and looked around. No one spoke or made
any noise. But the confrontation was not a dead silence; it was
a very live experience in which the innermost being of each
one of us communed with the Supreme Consciousness, which
was Bhagavan. I was numb with the appalling realisation that
the Glory was the same that dwelt in the simplicity which a
few minutes ago I had seen eating groundnuts in the intimate
company of the goat, the monkey and the squirrel. My mind
kept recalling that scene: how the goat had snuggled to
Bhagavan’s breast in perfect confidence in his love for it; how
the monkey had grinned in joy and how Bhagavan had
returned the grin as both bit the nut; how the squirrel had
peered with its pinhead eyes into Bhagavan’s dream-laden ones
and scratched his nose tenderly with its tiny left paw. The vision
of the Supreme Spirit underlying and overlaying the sense
perception was spiced with the lovely sight of the groundnut
party in the kitchen garden.

“Bhagavan got up from the couch. We got up. It seemed
tacitly understood that we were to leave. We left. I felt a hitherto
unknown peace and joy inside me; the faces of the others also
showed a similar condition. I saw Sri Ratilal, his host and his
servant get into their bullock cart at the Ashram gate. There was
a new spring in Sri Ratilal’s movements. Bhagavan’s promise in
the lad’s vision appeared to be starting a fulfilment.

“Many things have happened since that day in my life.
My material circumstances underwent changes for the worse,
but my inner life has always been happy since that day, for I
very often got a vision of Bhagavan, particularly when I was
most depressed in spirits.



30 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

“In 1953, I was in Rajkot staying alone in a lodge. One
day, while in the dining hall, a man of about thirty accosted
me, ‘Don’t you recognise me, Sir?’ ‘No, I’m sorry’, I replied,
truthfully. The man continued: ‘I am Ratilal of Gondal, Sir!
You remember the darshan of Bhagavan Ramana Maharshi,
five years ago?’ I looked at the man again. He was thin and
wiry, his face aglow with health and happiness. I shook his
hands heartily. He spoke again: ‘Sir, Bhagavan fulfilled his
promise wonderfully well. You see me. I am now managing our
family business, my father taking complete rest. I have a son two
years old and expect my wife to give me another child in a month
or two.’

“My mind immediately went back to the goat, the
monkey and the squirrel – and Bhagavan Ramana Maharshi.
I could never think of Bhagavan alone! So it has been all
these years. The scene comes to my mind’s eye often: the
kitchen garden with the four friends at the groundnut party.

“And, I thank Sri Ratilal and Sri Parthasarathi for
guiding me to the Vision Beautiful!” 1

S o u r c e
