CHAPTER – FIVE

BHAGAVAN’S COMPANIONS – THE MONKEYS

Between 1899 and 1922, Bhagavan lived in various
caves on the Arunachala hill. The hill abounded in monkeys.
Bhagavan would refer to the hill as their kingdom and
humans as the intruders. Bhagavan was very intimate with
the monkeys. He watched them closely, with love and
sympathy. He was a jnani, a sarvajna (the Omniscient).
Therefore, the love he had for all beings was natural. He
was a keen observer too. He moved closely with monkeys,
understood their cries, their conduct and knew the life story
of most of them. He observed that they had their own code
of conduct, morals and principles of governance. He found
that each tribe had its own recognized district, and if
another tribe infringed on this, there would be war. But
before starting a war, in order to make peace, an ambassador
would be sent from one tribe to the other. Monkeys, as a
rule would boycott the one that had been cared for by the
humans. However, they made an exception in the case of
Bhagavan. He would tell the visitors that the monkeys
recognized him as one of their community, and was
accepted as an arbitrator in their disputes. Whenever there
was a misunderstanding or a quarrel, the monkeys would
come to Bhagavan and he would find a solution to pacify
them and stop their quarrelling. He knew about their way
of life, attitudes, priorities, and their pranks too!



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 69

The people of the town called Bhagavan “Korangusami”
(Master of the monkeys) because, they were aware how much
the monkeys respected and accepted him as one of their own.
Bhagavan showed great love and compassion towards the
monkeys living onthe Hill of Arunachala.

We have a tendency to look down on monkeys. When we
refer to the mind we often compare it to a restless and
mischievous monkey. However, Bhagavan would always speak
positively about monkeys and praise their intelligence and agility.

Bhagavan once remarked:
 “I have known something about their organisation,

their kings, laws, regulations. Everything is so perfect and
well-organised. So much intelligence behind it all. I even
know that tapas is not unknown to monkeys. A monkey
whom we used to call ‘Mottaipaiyan’ was once oppressed
and ill-treated by a gang. He went away into the forest
for a few days, did tapas, acquired strength, and returned.
When he came back he sat on a bough and shook it. All
the rest of the monkeys, who had previously ill-treated him
and of whom he was earlier mortally afraid, were now
quaking before him. Yes. I am clear that tapas is well
known to monkeys.’’ 1

Here are some of the incidents that are reported by the
Ashramites and devotees, which show how lovingly Bhagavan
interacted with animals, especially with monkeys that lived
on the Arunachala hill and in the Ashram.

1. MONKEYS’ MEDITATION
When Bhagavan was living in Virupaksha cave, a

monkey clan regularly visited him and would sit under a big



70 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

shady tree in front of the cave. They would groom one
another for long hours in front of him. At other times, they
would sit before him without moving, silently absorbed as
though they were in deep meditation.

Bhagavan would often have light-hearted interaction
with his beloved companions – the monkeys and he would
even poke fun at them at times.

Once there was a clan of monkeys sitting silently and
serenely in front of Bhagavan as if they were meditating.
Bhagavan was thoroughly enjoying the spectacle and admired
their concentration; however he could not miss the
opportunity of teasing them.

Bhagavan addressed the monkeys, “Aye! Are you sitting
before me in samadhi? Very well done! But let me see how long
you will be able to sit like this?”

Just as he spoke, a monkey began to fidget. He opened
his eyes, unable to sit still any longer. Bhagavan looked at
him and said, “You fellow! Where are you going? What kingdom
have you to conquer? Sit quietly. Let me see!”

At Bhagavan’s words, the monkey became quiet and
completely peaceful. He sat, absorbed again, without even
the slightest movement!1

2. THE MONKEYS’ GRATITUDE
There is an incident involving the monkeys that

happened when Bhagavan and his devotees went for
giripradakshina (auspicious circuit around Arunachala, a
14-kilometre walk keeping the hill to the right). Bhagavan’s
samatva (treating all alike) was not merely theoretical, but very
real and it even extended to animals. Bhagavan and the



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 71

monkeys were true companions. He looked after them with
great care and compassion. This is how the monkeys of
Arunachala showed their gratitude to Bhagavan on a hot
summer day. It is a remarkable incident and very heart warming
indeed. The incident took place while Bhagavan was living in
Virupaksha cave. One hot summer day, Bhagavan and his
devotees set out for giripradakshina. Bhagavan would walk
leisurely even in the hot summer and would take several hours
or sometimes even a full day, on a few occasions, even more.

While they were going round the hill, the devotees
noticed that a troop of monkeys was following them, jumping
from one tree to another. At around l: 00 pm. Bhagavan
and his devotees reached the Pachaiamman Koil, an ancient
temple situated on the north-eastern side of the mountain.
The sun was intensely hot and the devotees were quite hungry
and thirsty. Although there was no water nearby, Bhagavan,
noticing the plight of his devotees decided that they should
stop, take rest for awhile. Suddenly the monkeys, who were
coming along with the group, climbed a nearby jambulina
tree, which had ripe, purple fruits. The monkeys shook the
branches and the fruits rained down. Then the monkeys came
down the tree but did not touch a single fruit. The devotees
realised that the fruits were meant for them. They picked up
the offering and ate them with great relish and appeased
their hunger. At the same time a group of women came up
with earthen jars of water to quench their thirst.1

The devotees who were with Bhagavan, remarked that
the monkeys who had received Bhagavan’s hospitality on the
Hill, were showing their gratitude.



72 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

It was heart warming to see how the monkeys
understood the plight of Bhagavan’s party, and the way they
rendered this timely service even without waiting for Bhagavan
having to say anything. They had understood the situation
and promptly came to the aid of the devotees. One does not
know how this came to be, unless it was still another instance
of Lord Arunachaleswara taking special care to look after his
son, our Bhagavan. Did not Bhagavan sing, “O Arunachala,
did you not bid me to come here? Come now, provide for me.”
(Aksharamana Malai, Sloka: 94).2

3. THE “MIRACULOUS VIBHUTI”
The following incident illustrates the fact that implicit

faith in Bhagavan has produced miraculous cures. Bhagavan
was so humble. He could make a joke about his own inability
to cure either himself or others. Even when miracles did
happen on account of the devotees’ faith in Bhagavan, he
never accepted the credit for the miracle. For him all these
things happened automatically and were part of the natural
process of the Self.

Chhaganlal V. Yogi one of the staunch devotees of
Bhagavan has recorded an incident about a miracle narrated
by an old devotee, who had known Bhagavan from his earliest
days on the hill:

“In 1908, when the town was afflicted with plague,
Bhagavan was staying in Pachiamman Temple. There
were many tamarind trees in the surrounding area. The
municipality gave the right to collect the tamarind to
the highest bidder, and during the period of Bhagavan’s
residence, the rights belonged to a local Muslim. In order



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 73

to protect his interests, the Muslim contractor had to
guard the trees from the monkeys, and he used to drive
them away by firing stones at them from a sling. As he
only wanted to scare them away, he took care to see that
his stones were not thrown hard enough to injure the
monkeys. Unfortunately, one stone hit a monkey on its
head and it died immediately. The rest of the monkey
tribe gathered up the dead body, and screeching with
both anger and sorrow, took the body to Bhagavan. Their
screeches grew louder in Bhagavan’s presence. Their
sorrow caused tears to trickle down Bhagavan’s face. His
presence finally calmed the monkeys down, and when
they were quiet, he turned to them and said, ‘For everyone
that is born, death is inevitable. He at whose hands this
monkey died will certainly meet death one day, so there is
no need to grieve.’

“The monkeys seemed pacified by these words and
they went away carrying the corpse with them. Two or
three days later the Muslim contractor became seriously
ill, and since he had heard what Bhagavan had said to the
monkeys, he interpreted the words to mean that Bhagavan
had put a curse on him. He told his family about the
incident, and since he was unable to travel himself, they
went up to the temple to beg Bhagavan to forgive the
contractor and to withdraw the curse from him. One of
them said, ‘It is certain that your curse has hit him, so we
beg you to be gracious enough to save him from death.
Please give us some vibhuti (sacred ash) so that we can
apply it to his head and then he will certainly recover.’



74 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

“Bhagavan smiled at them and said. ‘You are mistaken.
I never curse or bless anyone. I sent away the monkeys that
came here by telling them the simple truth that death
inevitably occurs to all those who are born. Moreover, I never
give vibhuti to anyone. Please go home and nurse the patient
whom you have left all alone.’

“The Muslim family was not convinced and they
refused to move unless Bhagavan gave them some
‘vibhuti’. Finally, in order to get rid of them, Bhagavan
took some ashes from the fire and gave it to the family.
The family was overjoyed and they rushed home to apply
the ash to their ailing relative. The ailing contractor was
back on his feet again within a few days, whether cured
naturally or due to the vibhuti 1

“The family of the contractor had great faith in
this vibhuti. Soon after it was applied to the ailing man,
he began to recover because the vibhuti offered by
Bhagavan carried sublime humility with it. Within a few
days he rose from his bed, fully recovered. Following the
‘miracle’ of his recovery the contractor was more tolerant
towards the monkeys. It is said that the contractor
remained a devotee of Bhagavan until his last days.”1

4. THE OLD MONKEY KING’S SAMADHI
Once, a monkey king took the bold step of outlawing

two turbulent males in his tribe. Early one morning it was
reported, that a monkey lay dying near Virupaksha cave.
Bhagavan went to see and it was this king. It was brought to
the Ashram and it lay with Bhagavan supporting it on his
lap. The two exiled males were sitting on a tree nearby,



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 75

watching. Bhagavan moved to shift his weight and the dying
monkey instinctively bit his leg. “I have four such marks of
favour from monkey kings,” he said once, pointing to his leg.
Then the monkey king uttered a last groan as he expired.
The two watching monkeys jumped up and down and cried
out with grief. The body was buried with the honours given
to a sannyasin. It was bathed in milk and then in water and
smeared with sacred ash; a new cloth was placed over it
leaving the face uncovered and camphor burnt before it. It
was given a burial near Virupaksha cave and over the Samadhi
a stone was raised.1

5. NONDIPAIYAN – THE LAME MONKEY
In 1916, Bhagavan’s mother Alagammal and her

youngest son Nagasundaram came to live with Bhagavan at
Virupaksha cave. A little later in the same year it was decided
to move into Skandasramam. Bhagavan moved there along
with a number of devotees and his animal companions. He
would make Skandasramam his abode for the next six years.

In the days just prior to Bhagavan moving from
Virupaksha cave to Skandasramam, there was a monkey troop
near Virupaksha cave. There was a young monkey in the
troop who had started to show some promise, and was gaining
popularity within the tribe. The leader of the troop became
envious and was very much displeased. Out of jealousy he
attacked the young monkey, making him fall from a high
tree. The fall badly injured a leg. The leader and the other
monkeys abandoned him to his fate near the Virupaksha cave.

Barely conscious, the monkey eventually limped into
Virupaksha cave. The all-compassionate Bhagavan bandaged



76 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

his leg and nursed him back to health. In due course injuries
were healed but he was left permanently crippled. Bhagavan
named him ‘Nondipaiyan’ the little Hobbler. He was called
‘Nondi’ as a pet name.

Nondi would always sit on Bhagavan’s lap or next to
him while he ate his food. He would never waste food. He
was a scrupulously clean eater. He followed Bhagavan
wherever Bhagavan went, limping along. Even when
Bhagavan told him not to follow him, he would follow with
great effort. He developed a fondness for Bhagavan and was
devoted to him, looking up to him as his master. Under
Bhagavan’s loving care, Nondi’s leg improved, and he
regained his strength. Nondi became a friend not only to
Bhagavan but also to other ashramites. They appreciated his
charming personality.

But whatever has a beginning has an end too. Nothing
is eternal except the Self, the Atman. Five days later, the
monkeys from Nondi’s former troop came to Virupaksha
cave. The ashramites saw them coming and were scared that
they may hurt Bhagavan. Even mother Alagammal felt the
same and told Bhagavan to leave Nondi. But Bhagavan
assured them that nothing would happen. The monkeys
would not hurt anybody at Virupaksha cave.

As soon as Nondi saw his troop members, he went and
climbed on to Bhagavan’s lap. One of the members of the
troop came near them and looked at Nondi’s bandaged leg.
Bhagavan told the monkeys that he had applied medicine and
bandaged to cure the injured leg. The monkeys were sorry
that they had hurt Nondi so badly. They gave a friendly growl



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 77

and one of them came and pulled Nondi asking him to rejoin
the troop. But Nondi did not want to leave Bhagavan. He
looked at Bhagavan. Bhagavan told Nondi, “Your troop has
come to reclaim you. Go with them like a good boy. Do not forget
us when you become a king.” So saying, Bhagavan patted Nondi
and sent him back with his relatives. Like other animals the
monkeys too have love and attachment to one other.

We have seen that as a rule, monkeys banish members
of their group who have had contact with human beings.
But in Nondi’s case, they did not outlaw him. They made an
exception of Bhagavan. He was very happy that everything
had ended well, and the devotees were amazed at the
companionship Bhagavan shared with monkeys.

 When Bhagavan shifted his abode to Skandasramam,
Nondi and his troop visited him often. The monkey troop
made themselves at home at Skandasramam and they would
get their share of food. After eating, they would climb the
trees, sit and watch the devotees. But Nondi would linger on
near Bhagavan. He had become one of the ashramites.
Mother Alagammal had a separate plate filled with food for
Nondi at meal times. But still Nondi preferred to sit with
Bhagavan, share food by helping himself from his leaf.
Bhagavan never minded Nondi eating from his plate. Thus
Nondi enjoyed the rare privilege of being fed by Bhagavan’s
own hand. It was a divine relationship.

One day when Nondi and his friends were given food,
Nondi did not touch his food while his friends ate their
share. Bhagavan had gone for a stroll on the Hill. When
Bhagavan came back, he saw that Nondi had not touched his



78 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

food. He realised the reason. Nondi wanted to sit on Bhagavan’s
lap and eat. Bhagavan gently told him that he had now grown
up enough to sit and eat on his own. Nondi understood. He
sat next to Bhagavan and ate his food. Nondi and his friends
spilled some rice on the ground, and Bhagavan scolded him.
Then Nondi cleaned the place by picking up each grain.
Bhagavan was very happy and admired Nondi’s smartness. 1

Desur Akhilandammal was Bhagavan’s staunch devotee.
She often brought food from her home and fed Bhagavan.
Once at Skandasramam when Akhilandammal served food
to Bhagavan, Nondi who was sitting next to Bhagavan
snatched the food from his leaf. When she placed another
helping on Bhagavan’s leaf, Nondi growled at her as if it was
his turn to be served by her. The pious lady got frightened.
Bhagavan told Nondi, “Adei, Adei (hey, hey) she is our own,
do not threaten her.” Nondi became quiet and sat down to
eat. Akhilandammal felt blessed hearing the words “our own”
from Bhagavan’s lips.2

Bhagavan in later years used to reminisce about Nondi
and his friends. One day in the Old Hall in Sri Ramanasramam,
he said:

“Nondi was a scrupulously clean eater. When a leaf-
plate of rice was set before him he would not spill a single
grain of rice outside it. If he ever did spill any he would
pick it up and eat it before going on with what was on
the plate. He was very sensitive though. Once, for some
reason, he threw out some food and I scolded him....
‘What! Why are you wasting food?’ He at once hit me
over the eye and hurt me slightly. As a punishment he was



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 79

not allowed to come to me and climb on to my lap for
some days, but the little fellow cringed and begged hard
and regained his happy seat. That was his second offence.
On the first occasion I put his cup of hot milk to my lips to
blow it, so as to cool it for him, and he was annoyed and
hit me over the eye, but there was no serious hurt and he
at once came back to my lap and cringed as though to say,
‘Forgive and forget’, so he was excused.”3

6. VENKITOO AND NONDI
Bhagavan’s nephew, Sri. T.N.Venkataraman, was the

former President of Sri Ramanasramam. He later became a
sannyasi with the name of Sri Ramanananda and was staying
in the Ashram till he was Absorbed in Bhagavan on 26
December, 2007. He was the son of Nagasundaram, the
younger brother of Bhagavan. Nagasundaram later became
a sannyasi, with the name of Niranjanananda Swami and was
the Sarvadhikari of the Ashram. Venkataraman was
affectionately called Venkitoo. As Venkitoo’s mother had died
very early, Bhagavan’s sister Alamelu and her husband Pichu
Iyer brought him up. Sometimes they would leave him in
the care of his grandmother, Alagammal for a few days. In
this way the little boy had the opportunity to be a part of the
life at Skandasramam with Bhagavan. Venkitoo also enjoyed
the company of Nondi and his clan. When devotees brought
offerings of fruits and sweets or other snacks, invariably
Venkitoo and Nondi were the principal beneficiaries. They
would get the lion’s share of the offerings.

The following incident took place when Venkitoo was
about four and a half years old:



80 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

One day, some devotees brought an offering of snacks
for everyone at Skandasramam. After distributing the prasad,
mother Alagammal set aside a special plate for Nondi who
had gone out and placed it inside. Venkitoo had his share,
and ate it with relish. During this time Venkitoo’s father, along
with some of the devotees, had gone to town for alms.
Bhagavan had gone up the hill for a stroll. After finishing
the morning chores Alagammal went inside to take a nap.
After mother Alagammal fell asleep, Venkitoo started eyeing
the snacks reserved for Nondi. Unable to resist any longer,
he thought that he would just sample Nondi’s snacks. But as
he started eating it he could not refrain from taking more
from Nondi’s plate. Suddenly Nondi burst in. He growled
at Venkitoo, snatched the food from the child’s hand, and
slapped him on the cheek. The boy started to cry in horror.

Hearing Venkitoo’s loud cry, mother Alagammal woke
up. Venkitoo complained that Nondi had slapped him, and
the mother asked Nondi why he had struck the child. About
that time Bhagavan returned from his walk, He had
overheard Alagammal’s words and saw Venkitoo rubbing his
cheek. He understood that Nondi had slapped Venkitoo for
eating the snacks that mother had put aside for him.

Bhagavan said smilingly to Venkitoo, “So you got the blow
Venkitoo? It serves you right. That is Nondi’s portion of snacks.
We should not take what belongs to others, should we? It was
Nondi ’s share.”

Petting her grandson and consoling him, Alagammal told
Bhagavan, “Poor child! What does the child Venkitoo know
about his own share or that of another? He is really scared.”



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 81

Bhagavan called Venkitoo and put him on his lap, patted
the child affectionately and said, “Do not worry, Nondi is a
nice fellow. He will not beat you anymore. Because you took
Nondi’s share of food, he was upset and slapped you on the cheek.
Look how very gentle he is with grandma and me.”

From that day onwards Venkitoo was no longer tempted
by Nondi’s plate.1

7. NONDI’S CORONATION
Nondi often came with his clan to visit Bhagavan at

Skandasramam. Bhagavan observed that Nondi had
become very powerful within his group. He appeared
to be the bravest of all the monkeys. At the same time
the current leader of the clan had grown old and had
turned into a bully. When there was a shortage of food
the old king would take everything for himself not even
caring for his queens or for his offspring. Although the
old king abused the members of the clan, Nondi still
remained loyal to him. Bhagavan was watching all this
silently. He wanted to see what would happen. He had
already witnessed one confrontation between Nondi and
the corrupt old king.

One day Bhagavan went for giripradakshina with his
devotees. During Bhagavan’s absence, Nondi and a host of
other monkeys came to Skandasramam. When they didn’t
see Bhagavan there, they went on a rampage in the garden
and then went on to break the twigs and branches on the
trees around Skandasramam. When Bhagavan returned from
giripradakshina, he saw the destruction and wondered at the
monkeys’ behaviour.



82 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

The following day Nondi came back to Skandasramam
with his clan. He climbed the highest branch of the tallest
tree, and began to shake the branch as he cried out loudly.
Bhagavan knew that this was a privilege enjoyed only by a
monkey chieftain. After two or three minutes, Nondi came
down, hopped onto Bhagavan’s lap and embraced him.
Bhagavan stroked Nondi’s head. There were nearly a hundred
monkeys assembled. In their excitement the monkeys climbed
the trees just as Nondi had done and began to jump wildly.
Suddenly a large, old monkey approached Bhagavan and
Nondi and knelt before them. It was the old king
surrendering to Nondi.

When Bhagavan offered food as usual to Nondi, he did
not take it but led the attendant to where three other monkeys
were sitting, and there he shared the food with them. The
three female monkeys were the queens of the deposed king
and they were now Nondi’s queens according to monkeys’
custom and usage. Seeing all this Bhagavan knew that Nondi
had become king. Bhagavan explained this sequence of events
to the bewildered onlookers.

Nondi had won the throne. While Bhagavan and his
devotees had gone for giripradakshina, Nondi had come with
his retinue to have his coronation in Bhagavan’s presence, the
previous day. Finding Bhagavan’s absence in Skandasramam,
the monkeys had expressed their chagrin by breaking branches
and ravaging the garden. Nondi had once been banished,
rejected and left for dead by his own clan, unable even to
walk! But with Bhagavan’s Grace, he grew up and became a
great warrior and even the king of his clan.1



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 83

Nondi begot children from his three queens. Even after
Bhagavan moved down from Skandasramam to Sri
Ramanasramam, Nondi came searching for Bhagavan and
continued to visit him very often. He would bring his
children also and they would also get a morsel of food from
Bhagavan, when he took meals. Other monkey friends from
the Hill would also visit Bhagavan for they all felt Bhagavan’s
absence and journeyed long distance just to see him.
Bhagavan was always able to recognise their faces, even of
those whom he had not seen for a long time.2

King Nondi grew in wisdom and by Bhagavan’s Grace
earned the respect of his clan. He brought prosperity to his
clan by virtue of his wisdom, valour and strength of character.
Perhaps his greatest gift to the tribe was the special place he
held in Bhagavan’s heart, for it was through Nondi that
Bhagavan’s blessing came to them.3

But in spite of the justness of Nondi’s rule, there was
infighting in the clan. Nondi became old and weak. In a
war, he was gravely injured and succumbed to his injuries.
Nondi’s mortal remains were returned to the five elements
on Arunachala.4

8. ETIQUETTE OF THE MONKEYS
Just as human beings have their own customs and

regulations, the monkeys too have their own etiquette.
When Bhagavan was on the hill, the following incident

took place. One day a monkey king came to Skandasramam.
He was the king of a large tribe. Bhagavan had just sat down
to have his meal and mother Alagammal was serving food.
The monkey king came and sat next to Bhagavan. Before



84 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

eating, Bhagavan gave the first mouthful to the monkey king.
But instead of eating, the monkey king growled and gave
Bhagavan a slap on the cheek.

Bhagavan asked the monkey, “Why you are angry, I fed
you first, didn’t I?”

But even as he was saying this, Bhagavan suddenly
realised his mistake. He then asked that the food be
served on a separate leaf. The monkey king was pleased;
he ate the food with relish and left happily after finishing
his meal. Afterwards Bhagavan explained to the devotees
that this monkey king had to be treated in accordance
with his royal status. Since he had not been given a
separate leaf, he was angry with Bhagavan and had
slapped him.1

9.RIVALRY OVER SITTING ON BHAGAVAN’S LAP
When Bhagavan was living on the Hill, this incident

took place. Bhagavan loved children and was charmed by
their innocence. He admired their lack of hypocrisy.
Vajreswari, the four year old daughter of Kavyakanta
Ganapathi Muni, a staunch devotee of Bhagavan and a well
known Sanskrit scholar, had full liberty with Bhagavan and
would insist on sitting on his lap whenever she came.
Bhagavan would often call her near him, and show her
affection by putting her on his lap and talking to her.

Once Vajreswari came to the Skandasramam and as
usual sat on Bhagavan’s lap. As Bhagavan was holding her
and talking to her lovingly, a young monkey came up to them.
The young monkey apparently got jealous of Vajreswari,
pushed her off Bhagavan’s lap and took her place.



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 85

Vajreswari, started to cry, and she pleaded with
Bhagavan,“Send this monkey out; I want to sit on your lap.”

But the young monkey would not give up his
privileged place and continued to sit there. This rivalry
between the child and the young monkey thoroughly
amused Bhagavan but at the same time he wanted to
appease both of them.

He turned to the young monkey and said, “Look,
Vajreswari is your sister. Is she not? Let her also sit on my lap
with you. Give her some room.”  Then he looked at Vajreswari
who was standing near him and said, “Is he not your young
brother? Come on both of you sit on my lap.”

Finally both the child and the monkey sat together on
Bhagavan’s lap fully satisfied and enjoyed each other’s
company. Is this not a practical demonstration of Bhagavan’s
great love for all beings irrespective of their forms? It is a
lesson to all devotees!1

MONKEYS IN SRI RAMANASRAMAM
After Bhagavan moved down from Skandasramam and

settled in Sri Ramanasramam at the base of Arunachala.
More and more devotees and visitors came for Bhagavan’s
darshan and sought his blessings since it was no longer
necessary for people to climb the Hill. But with this change,
he was now more accessible to human devotees than to his
animal companions. Bhagavan often lamented the fact that
in Ramanasramam he was indoors, where animals did not
have free access to him. He insisted that no one regardless of
what species they may belong to should be deprived of his
darshan. Slowly, the ashramites and devotees began to



86 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

understand that Bhagavan’s animal companions were also
his devotees and as dear to him as any of them.

10. VISIT OF THE OLD MONKEY KING
After coming down from Skandasramam to the present

Ramanasramam, Bhagavan, as always, was surrounded by
his devotees. One day, Bhagavan unusually got up abruptly,
after listening to some peculiar noise and went eastward to
the farthest fence of the Ashram. An old monkey with a
retinue of female monkeys was shouting and crying aloud.
Bhagavan alone had heard it, perhaps because the cries of
the monkey were directed at him!

He went near the crying monkey and listened to him
and then addressed him: “Thatha (Grandpa)! What to do? I
have been retained here. I could not come there. I also miss you
all very much. You have come to see me with your queens, all the
way from there, risking attacks from other herds of monkeys
staying in these parts. How are you? How is your family? Is
everything all right? I am quite well here. Please go back and
also take back these queens safely. It is very good of you to have
come to see me.” Bhagavan’s voice was choked and tears were
rolling down from his eyes.1

Bhagavan returned to the hall and told the devotees
about the incident.

11. PERILOUS JOURNEY – BLESSINGS TO THE NEWBORN
When Bhagavan was living in Skandasramam if a

monkey had a baby, the mother would bring the newborn
to Bhagavan in order to seek his blessings. On such
occasions Bhagavan would take the baby and stroke it



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 87

affectionately, and give his blessings before giving it back
to the mother.

Once Kunjuswami, who was a witness to such an
incident, told the following incident to Sundaram Anna
(V.S.Ramanan):

“Just after moving down the hill from Skandasramam,
Bhagavan was sitting at the back of the present
Ramanasramam and talking to a mother monkey and her
baby. The mother monkey was apparently complaining
to Bhagavan for having left Skandasramam and shifting
all the way down to the foot of the mountain. In order to
come for Bhagavan’s blessings, she had to travel quite some
distance, trespassing the territory of other monkey clans.

“Bhagavan listened patiently to her protests and
then with genuine regret said, ‘Amma, I am sorry. There
was nothing I could do. We had to come here.’

“Meanwhile, Bhagavan took the young one in his hands,
caressed and fondled him, and then handing the baby back
to the mother, urged her to leave quickly because monkeys
of the other tribes whose terrain she had encroached might
attack her and the baby.”1

12. PERILOUS JOURNEY OF GRANDPA
On one occasion Bhagavan and Kunjuswami came back

from giripradakshina and were resting on a bench under a
tree at the back of Sri Ramanasramam. They suddenly heard
cries from a distant banyan tree where Bhagavan would often
go and give fruits to monkeys. Bhagavan hastened over to
the tree. Kunjuswami had never seen Bhagavan go
somewhere in such a hurry and with such a sense of urgency.



88 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

He also followed. Bhagavan and Kunjuswami arrived at the
banyan tree and they found about twenty monkeys and in
the middle of the group, an old monkey holding a baby
monkey. Bhagavan approached them and listened to the old
monkey with utmost attention.

Bhagavan looked concerned and said to the old monkey:
“Grandpa! What ill fortune has befallen you? At this advanced age
you have to care for this young one! I am sorry! But look after him
with care. You will get Punya”. Kunjuswami did not understand
anything. Perplexed, he asked Bhagavan what was happening.
Bhagavan explained, “Do you see his plight? The mother of this
baby monkey died recently. According to the monkeys’ custom, the
oldest member of the group has to look after the orphan. Now the
responsibility of bringing up this baby rests on our grandpa.”

 So saying Bhagavan looked sympathetically at the old
monkey who was muttering plaintively. Kunjuswami asked
Bhagavan, “What is it that the old monkey is saying?”

Bhagavan after listening to the old monkey with full
attention and with a lot of compassion, turned to Kunjuswami
and said: “To tell their woeful story they have come to us very
cautiously, as this is not their territory. When we were at
Skandasramam this same grandpa and his clan used to come
and visit us. Just like human beings, they have kingdoms with
boundaries and laws. Each kingdom has its boundaries. Our
grandpa was the king of the entire hill, from foot of the hill to
Sonathirtham (The tank next to Ganesha temple which is on
the South-west of Arunachala). Beyond that is another kingdom.
One should not trespass into another’s kingdom. To do so is a
crime. That is why these monkeys came stealthily today, so as



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 89

not to attract any attention. See how much trouble we are
causing to these monkeys! ”

So saying, Bhagavan asked Kunjuswami to get some
plantains from the Ashram stores. Bhagavan distributed the
plantains to the monkeys and then the troop departed one
by one. The old grandpa monkey cast glances at Bhagavan,
reluctant to leave his company and go off with the troop.
Bhagavan assured the old monkey by saying, “Grandpa! Don’t
be afraid! Take care of the little one. For all of us Lord
Arunachala is here. He saves us all! Please take care of your
safety! Go! We will meet again.” 1

Bhagavan, who was compassionate and caring towards
all beings, thus consoled the old monkey.

13. BHAGAVAN’S EMPATHY
The companionship of Bhagavan and the monkeys

continued even after his coming down to the present Sri
Ramanasramam from Skandasramam. Everyday Bhagavan
would take a stroll up the Hill, at 7 o’clock in the morning
and at 5 o’clock in the evening. The monkeys would be sitting
along the path.

Bhagavan would talk to them and then come down to the
Ashram. Whenever an opportune moment came, the monkeys
would come even to the hall in the Ashram, get some prasad
and return. Suri Nagamma relates the following incident in one
of her letters that shows Bhagavan’s empathy for the monkeys:

“At 3 o’clock this afternoon while we were discussing
something in Bhagavan’s presence, a stranger came to the
Ashram with a platter full of fruits. It seems that on the
way to the hall some monkey came, snatched some of the



90 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

fruits, and escaped. Hearing the noise outside and realising
what had happened, Bhagavan laughingly said that the
monkey took away his portion of the fruit, as he was afraid
we would not otherwise give it. We all laughed.

“While this was going on, a female monkey with a
baby at her breast approached the fruit basket. People
near the basket shouted her away. Bhagavan said, ‘She
is a mother with a child. Why not give her something and
send her away?’ But he was not sufficiently audible, and
so the monkey got frightened, went off, and hid herself
in a tree. Bhagavan, full of pity and kindness, said:

‘Is this fair? We call ourselves sannyasins; but when a
real Sannyasi comes we drive him away without giving
him anything. How unfair! We want to eat for years and
live. We store things in a room, lock it and keep the keys
with us. Have the monkeys got house? Can they put
anything by for the tomorrow? They eat whatever they
can get and sleep on whichever tree available. The mother
carries the child under her belly, wherever she goes, until
the child is able to walk about when she leaves the child to
itself. Who is a real sannyasi, the monkey or we? That is
why the male monkey took his share on the way itself.
That was a male monkey. He could do it with impunity.
This is a female. What can she do?’

“So saying Bhagavan began calling that monkey
cajolingly. The monkey came on to the side of the couch
and stood there. In an endearing manner, Bhagavan gave
her all the fruits she wanted and sent her away.” 1

Once, a monkey with her baby stood at the window by



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 91

the side of Bhagavan’s sofa. As he was reading something, he
did not notice her. After a while, the monkey screeched and
attendants tried to drive her away by shouting, but she would
not go.

Bhagavan then looked up and said, “Wait! She has
come here to show us her baby. Don’t people bring their
children to show Bhagavan? For her, her child is equally dear.
Look how tender the child is?” So saying Bhagavan turned
towards her and said in an endearing tone. “Hello! So you
have brought your child? That is good! Come in. I will see
that nobody harms you.” Bhagavan then urged her to come
in. The mother monkey came with the baby and gave her
child to Bhagavan. After fondling the baby, Bhagavan gave
the baby back to its mother. He gave some fruits to the
mother and sent her away. The monkey was full of
contentment and joy.2

14. MONKEYS’ ONE - POINTEDNESS
Suri Nagamma tells another story of how Bhagavan cited

the example of monkeys to teach his attendants about
concentration:

“Sometimes these great monkey-warriors knock the
fruit out of the hands of newcomers, while on their way
to Bhagavan, and at times they would even snatch away
the fruit kept by their side, which people get back as
Bhagavan’s prasadam from his attendant,. Noticing these
things Bhagavan would say, ‘They take their share of the
fruit, why be angry with them? There is the concentrated
look, the ‘lakshya drishti’. Somehow they find out where the
fruit is kept and in the twinkling of an eye, all of them come



92 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

and take away their share. Their attention is always on the
fruit. That is why, in Vedantic parlance, the monkey’s look is
given as an illustration of the concentrated look, ‘lakshya
drishti’. The moment the Guru makes a sign with his eye,
the disciple should understand; otherwise the disciple cannot
achieve his aim”.1

Kanakammal, a staunch devotee from the days of Bhagavan
and who merged with Bhagavan on his Jayanti day (1-1-2010),
told us that the monkeys were able to get away with many pranks
because of the special privileges they enjoyed with Bhagavan.
Often, when unwary visitors to the Ashram would bring
sweets and fruits or other article of food as offerings to
Bhagavan, monkeys would pounce on them and rob them
of the delicacies. Even as the devotees and the attendants
watched helplessly, Bhagavan would smile mischievously, as
if to say, “You are no match for my monkey friends.” 2

15. ASYLUM
Once, a group of young monkeys entered the hall.

When Bhagavan’s attendants tried to chase them away, the
young ones ran straight to Bhagavan and hid under his sofa,
as they knew that nobody would touch them as long as they
were near Bhagavan. The attendants indeed stopped
pursuing them.

The little ones made the most of their strategic advantage
and lost no time in taunting the attendants, going as far as
making faces at them. Bhagavan chuckled, fully enjoying the
situation. He made it clear that he was on the monkeys’ side
by rewarding the cheeky little rascals with fruits and nuts. 1



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 93

16. BHAGAVAN’S WARNING
One day in the hall, a devotee, was reading the

newspaper to Bhagavan. There was an article in it about a
team of veterinarians from Madras (Chennai) coming to
Thiruvannamalai to catch monkeys for taking them to various
research laboratories. Upon hearing this news, Bhagavan
turned to the monkey clan gathered at the window and
warned them of the approaching danger, advising them to
go and hide for three days. Evidently they understood his
words and heeded them as not one monkey was seen for
several days thereafter. 1

17. BHAGAVAN’S COMMAND
Ramani Ammal of Rajapalayam, a devotee, was a

permanent resident of Ramananagar. She shared with
V. Ganesan the following anecdote:

“Once, while I was seated in front of Bhagavan in
the old hall, slices of fruits were brought by a devotee and
placed before Bhagavan. A monkey walked in at that time
and wanted the fruit. Though the attendant was not very
keen to oblige, upon Bhagavan’s behest, he gave the
monkey a piece of fruit. Receiving it, the monkey bounded
away, but another monkey soon came in evidently
informed by the former. A piece was given to the second
monkey also and he soon went away. A third came in for a
share! Bhagavan gave him a piece, saying, ‘All right! Now
don’t go and inform any others, or else these people here will
be angry with me!’ This third fellow left with his share and
obviously followed Bhagavan’s command to the letter, as
there was no further claimant from his group.”1



94 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

18. SUFFERING IS MINE
Krishnaswami was Bhagavan’s attendant for almost

twelve years. As he was dominating the other attendants, he
was nicknamed ‘Hall Sarvadhikari’. Krishnaswami was a
veritable enemy of the monkeys. He would chase them, shout
at them, and beat them when they were doing mischief in
the hall or when they tried to steal fruits. Once when
Krishnaswami began beating the monkeys, Bhagavan was
deeply pained.1

In a cracked voice full of pain, Bhagavan stunned his
attendants and all the others gathered in the hall when he
said, “You are not beating the monkeys. You are beating me.
The pain is mine.” 2 What compassion and understanding
Bhagavan had! He could feel the pain of others even if it was
an animal or a bird.

When some devotees complained to Bhagavan that the
monkeys were troubling them, He would say, “Not many
years back it was deep jungle here, the monkeys’ home. They
roamed freely. We came, cleared the ground, built houses and
drove the monkeys away. Who is to be blamed? They or we? If
they give us a little trouble, can we not bear it quietly?” 3

Bhagavan would always come to their defence. Devotees were
ashamed of their complaint.

19. TRANSGRESSING OFFICIAL ORDERS
In his book ‘Moments Remembered ’ V. Ganesan

relates the following incident involving attendant
Krishnaswami  :

“Once, the monkeys played havoc in the garden
and took great liberties with the visiting devotees by



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 95

relieving them of the offerings brought by them for
Bhagavan! Many complaints reached the ears of the
Sarvadhikari Sri Niranjanananda Swami, and he took
immediate action by asking Krishnaswami not to feed the
monkeys anymore. That day a monkey came and sat on
the window sill looking expectantly at Bhagavan for the
usual prasad. Bhagavan directed his attendants to help the
visitor. Krishnaswami said that the Sarvadhikari had given
orders, due to the intolerable mischief wrought by the
monkeys. Meanwhile the monkey still waited on the
window sill.

“It was too much for Bhagavan to see his companion
wait so long for prasad. With great emotion he said:

 “‘ You see! When Anjaneya found mother Sita safe
in Sri Lanka, his joy knew no bounds. In sheer joy, he
destroyed the Ashoka grove and set fire to Sri Lanka and
returned happily to convey the good news to Lord Rama!’’

“Bhagavan continued:
“‘The monkeys, out of intense joy, sometimes turn

uncontrollable, pulling down branches and leaves from
trees and teasing the visitors. We should not treat this
as being a ‘nuisance’ and deprive them of their rightful
share of food. This is their birthplace and it is we who
are outsiders. We have no right to chase them away.’

“Bhagavan was so moved that he could not control
his emotion. The loving concern of Bhagavan for animals
moved Krishnaswami so much that he transgressed the
official orders and gave a bunch of bananas to the waiting
monkey. Bhagavan beamed with a glorious smile.”1



96 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

20. THE EMPTY COUCH
Bhagavan used to walk on the hill every morning and

evening. One evening, instead of his usual short walk he went
up to Skandasramam. When he did not return at the usual
time, some devotees went up the hill side in search of him
and some others gathered in small clusters discussing where
he might have gone, what it meant and what to do about it.
Others sat in the hall, waiting.

Meanwhile, a band of monkeys approached the darshan
hall. A number of them sat outside while the other monkeys
remained at their usual place and peeped in through the
bars of the window near Bhagavan’s couch. But two of them,
eager to see Bhagavan, fearlessly stepped inside and looked
anxiously at the empty couch. When the monkeys came to
know about the empty couch, the whole clan began to jump
and scream. 1

21. WHAT CAN YOU DO ABOUT THIS?
In her book Cherished Memories, Kanakammal relates

the following story:
“Once, a Westerner who was on a visit to the Ashram,

brought a folding canvas chair to the meditation hall.
Since he was not accustomed to sitting on the floor, he
had brought this low chair to meditate in Bhagavan’s
presence. When the visitor was unfolding the chair and
setting it up, an Ashram attendant noticed it. The
attendant approached the visitor and told him that it was
not the practice to sit on a raised seat in Bhagavan’s
presence. The visitor felt that it was impossible for him to
sit on the floor, so he picked up his chair and left the hall.



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 97

“Bhagavan was watching this and told the attendant,
‘So! You have done a great service to your Master, have you
not? That man was planning to use a chair because he could
not sit on the floor. You very cleverly prevented him from
acting in such a disrespectful fashion. Now what are you
planning to do about this other person who is sitting right
above my head?’ All of us looked up, and saw a big monkey
perched on the roof of the hall, directly above Bhagavan’s
head. Until Bhagavan pointed out the monkey, nobody
had noticed him. Now all our eyes were upon him; but
the monkey was totally unconcerned about the attention
he was receiving from the people below.

“Bhagavan said, ‘We try to impose our will upon
others if it is possible to do so. If the other person
happens to be stronger, we let him have his way. That
is human nature. Why should we concern ourselves with
such trivial matters? What difference can it make,
whether a person sits on the floor or upon a chair? What
is high and what is low? Are these not mere concepts?
Should we not learn to look beyond these things?’” 1

22. BHAGAVAN’S PATRONAGE
In the book The Power of the Presence - Part III’ there is an

incident narrated by Krishna Bhikshu who witnessed how
Bhagavan supported the monkeys:

“One never knew how Bhagavan would react to
human devotees but one would always know that he would
take the side of animal visitor if he got into trouble with
any of the people present in the hall. On a sultry summer
afternoon Bhagavan was resting on a couch near the wall.



98 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

“A visitor brought some fruits that were kept on a brass
tray near the couch. A gathering of monkeys looked at
the fruit from the distance and Bhagavan’s attendant
Madhava Swami with a long stick in hand was on the alert.
Others were in meditation with their eyes closed.
Bhagavan was reclining quietly and gazing into space.
Suddenly he started clapping his hands and laughing like
a child.

“‘He got it, He got it!’ He shouted with glee. ‘He
got the fruit!’

“A monkey, managing to outwit Madhava Swami, had
snatched some fruit from the tray and was sitting on the
wall holding the fruit. The people sitting in meditation
opened their eyes in wonder. Bhagavan’s merriment
continued as long as the monkey was eating the fruit. It
looked as if Bhagavan was extremely pleased that Madhava
Swami was defeated by the monkey.

“Bhagavan always sided with the monkeys. It seemed
that he would rather have the monkeys eat the fruit than
the visitors. He would give away the monkeys any fruit or
food that was kept nearby. When the attendant was on
guard with the stick, Bhagavan would send him away on
some errand. The monkeys would then have the best time
of the day. When chased they would hide behind
Bhagavan’s sofa – between the sofa and the wall – where it
was difficult to get at them without disturbing Bhagavan.
They knew very well who was on their side, and once behind
the sofa the monkeys would show the attendants how
little they cared for them.



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 99

“Bhagavan loved monkeys dearly and used to say that
they were teaching us a spiritual lesson. ‘They may seem
to gambol or sit quiet, but their minds are always on the
fruits, and whatever they may be doing, they go for them
as soon as there is a chance.’”1

23. MONKEY - MANGOES
Suri Nagamma writes in one of her letters about how

the monkeys take only what they need:
“This morning at 10 o’clock Dr. Anantanarayana Rao

and his wife Ramabai brought some good mangoes from
their garden and while giving them to Bhagavan, said,
‘The monkeys are taking away all the mangoes. So we
hurriedly plucked these and have brought them here.’
Bhagavan said smilingly, ‘Oh, is that so. So the monkeys are
going there also?’ Then looking at all the others there,
he said, ‘Yes, monkeys take the fruits one by one while
people take them all in one lot. If asked why, they say it is
their right. If what the monkeys do is petty theft, what
people do is regular looting. Without realisizg that, they
drive away the monkeys.’” 1

24. THE OBEDIENT MONKEY
Devotee Kanakammal was in a reminiscent mood and

told the following story:
“One day a devotee offered Bhagavan a cluster of

plantain fruits that he had grown at his house. Afterwards,
Bhagavan and that same devotee started discussing some
spiritual matters. At that time a big male monkey walked
into the hall and took away the whole cluster of fruits that



100 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

were offered. The monkey sat outside the hall with the
fruits. Seeing this the devotee shouted, ‘Ayyo! I had brought
those fruits for Bhagavan, that monkey has taken them away.’

“Bhagavan turned to the monkey and said, ‘Aye! How
can you take away the whole bunch of fruits? Like a good boy
take one fruit, bring back the remaining fruits, and keep
them here.’

“Hearing Bhagavan’s words the monkey took his share
of fruit and brought back the remaining to Bhagavan like
an obedient boy.

“Bhagavan turned to the devotee and said, ‘When
you entered the Ashram, if you had given a fruit, when this
fellow asked for it, he would not have taken all the fruits.’

“The devotee was astonished by what Bhagavan told
him. How did Bhagavan come to know this? In fact when
the devotee had entered the Ashram with fruits, this money
had asked for some by extending his hand. The devotee
had not obliged the monkey.”1

Was there anything that omniscient Bhagavan did
not know? It was impossible to hide anything from him.

25. THE MONKEY’S WORK
There are two incidents recorded that describe how the

monkey ‘did the work’ of Bhagavan’s attendants and how
Bhagavan supported them.

Once, a devotee brought some fruits to the hall. He placed
the fruits on the stool, prostrated to Bhagavan and went and sat
down with the other devotees. The attendant was not in the
room at the time and the fruits remained upon the stool. A small
monkey, standing near the screen behind Bhagavan’s sofa,



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 101

was gazing at the fruits with longing eyes. He must have been a
rather timid monkey, for he hesitated to come forward and
snatch the fruits. Bhagavan saw the little fellow and gave an
encouraging nod. As though he had been waiting just for this
encouragement, the monkey darted forward, grabbed some
fruits and scrambled up to the thatched roof of the hall. Perched
there, safely out of human reach, the monkey was happily feasting
on the fruits when the attendant returned to the hall. As soon as
the attendant entered the hall, Bhagavan said, “You must thank
him for doing you a favour.” The attendant was puzzled by
Bhagavan’s remark. Bhagavan explained: “Somebody brought some
fruits. You were not here at the time. Seeing that your job had to be
done, he decided to take care of it himself.” Saying this, Bhagavan
pointed to the monkey on the roof. Then he added, “There is
small differences in the way the two of you go about the job, however!
Whereas you would have put the fruits in a basket, he chose to put
them in his stomach! Anyway, he has disposed of the fruits and for
that, you should thank him” 1

Once, while Bhagavan was in the old hall, one of the
attendants was sitting with a basket to receive fruits offered by
devotees. The attendant was sitting with closed eyes. Listening
to the radio he dozed away. Finding a suitable opportunity,
some of the monkeys came and grabbed the fruits. When the
people in the hall tried to scare them away, Bhagavan said:“When
these attendants are immersed in deep meditation (dhyana
samadhi), the monkeys come and see to the work of the
attendants. Someone has to look after the work! The attendants
put the fruits into the basket; the monkeys put the fruits into their
stomachs; that is all the difference. While people forget themselves



102 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

listening to the music over the radio, the monkeys busy themselves
in enjoying the sweet juice of the fruits. That is good, isn’t it?” If
the monkeys come while none of the attendants was there,
Bhagavan would say,  “See, not one of you was here and so the
monkeys have been looking after your work. They are actually
helping you. So you can take some rest. When I was on the hill,
they were my constant companions. You now drive them away,
but in those days, theirs was the empire”. 2

26.INDEPENDENCE DAY CELEBRATIONS
The monkeys enjoyed Bhagavan’s affection, compassion,

and Grace. But often Bhagavan’s human devotees were not
as enthusiastic about monkeys as he was. In fact they were
indifferent to his monkey companions. This was especially
true of the jubilee hall attendants who had to maintain order
and protect visitors who brought fruit offerings. They often
clashed with the simian visitors especially during festivals.

Once on August 15th, the Indian Independence Day,
Krishnaswami, Bhagavan’s attendant, was putting up the national
flag. There were many devotees gathered there, and they were
looking on. Some of the devotees were busy helping
Krishnaswami. He was in high spirits and was using a long
bamboo pole to hoist the Indian flag that would mark the day’s
event. Suddenly a monkey appeared and approached
Krishnaswami. For no clear reason Krishnaswami began to chase
the poor animal away. He was shouting wildly and beating the
monkey wildly with the very pole that bore the national flag at
its end. Bhagavan was observing all this from a distance. The
irony of the situation was not lost on Bhagavan who called out,
“Oh, Krishnaswami, this is Independence Day celebration.



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 103

Moreover, until recently, this place was the kingdom of monkeys.
When we came here and settled, they kindly withdrew to the hill.
Today this monkey has come to participate in our Independence
Day celebration out of joy for the event. But you are unreasonably
driving him away with the very centre piece of the day’s celebration!
Has it not occurred to you, dear fellow, how silly that looks?” 1

A few days before Independence Day while Bhagavan
was seated in the jubilee hall, an army of monkeys came
clamouring for fruits. Krishnaswami, tried to drive them away
by shouting, when Bhagavan said, “Remember 15th of August
is an Independence Day for them as well. You must give them a
feast on that day instead of driving them away.”

On August 14th, the attendant tried to drive the
monkeys away again. Seeing this, Bhagavan said with a laugh,
“Do not drive them away please. You must give them Bengal-
gram, lentils, parched rice, and feast them. Is it proper to drive
them away?”

“But tomorrow is Independence Day, Bhagavan” said
the attendant. Bhagavan laughed, “So that’s it, is it? But when
we are making the arrangements for the celebration, should they
not make their own arrangements? That is why they are busy,
going here and there. Don’t you see?” 2

In this way the monkeys were given a feast on
Independence Day.

27. ONE - CONSCIOUSNESS
Once in the hall, the devotee Mercedes D’Acosta asked

Bhagavan:
Devotee: Does one who has realised the Self lose the

sense of ‘I’?



104 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Bhagavan: Absolutely.
Devotee: Then there is no difference between yourself

and myself, that man over there, my servant. Are they all the
same?

Bhagavan: All are the same, including those monkeys
sitting over there.

Devotee: But the monkeys are not people. Are they not
different?

Bhagavan: They are exactly the same as people. All are
same in Consciousness. 1

When Mercedes D’Acosta was sitting in front of
Bhagavan, she saw that several small monkeys had climbed
onto his couch and had broken the silence that prevailed in
the hall with their gay chattering. She writes:

“Bhagavan loved animals and they were always
welcomed and respected. They were treated as equals to
humans and were addressed by names by Bhagavan.
When sick, animals were brought to Bhagavan. He would
keep them on his couch or on the floor beside him until
they were well. Many animals died in his arms. He loved
to tell stories about the goodness of animals. It was
remarkable, that around him none of the animals ever
fought or attacked each other. “2

28. THE MONKEY AND THE CHILD
Mrs. M. A. Piggot, an English lady, who had read

the book A Search in Secret India, came to see the Bhagavan.
The services of a disciple as interpreter were provided. There
were many visitors at that time in the hall, including some
ladies with their infants. The place resounded with noise.



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 105

At length silence prevailed. Suddenly Maharshi, who
seemed to be looking at infinite space, was heard to say
softly, “Korangu!” A little baby was then discovered in the
doorway (unobserved by the mother who was seated on
the other side of the door) with a large monkey standing
on his hind legs, who with both hands was fondling the
child with the tenderness of a mother, not hurting the
child in the least, both being at peace with each other in
Maharshi’s presence. When Maharshi’s voice was heard
the monkey jumped out nimbly and disappeared in a
trice.1

Mrs. Piggot who witnessed this was very greatly
impressed and felt blessed to be there. What a wonderful
scene of equality between the child and the animal! Was this
not due to Bhagavan’s presence!

29. BHIKSHA FOR THE MONKEYS
The monkeys were Bhagavan’s companions throughout

his stay on the hill. Stories about monkeys abound in every
book on Bhagavan. During his stay in Virupaksha cave and
Skandasramam, Bhagavan would divide the alms collected
equally among those present including the monkeys. After
eating their portion, the monkeys would make loud noises
expressing gratification. When Bhagavan came to the present
Sri Ramanasramam the same monkeys visited him. Soon they
settled in the Ashram groves. They were fed in the Ashram
presently also.1

Year by year the number of human devotees steadily
increased. Gradually, for monkeys the intimate access to
Bhagavan was reduced. However, as the visitors came in large



106 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

numbers so did their fruit offerings, which Bhagavan was
always keen to share with his four-legged devotees. Streams
of visitors came for festivals. On such days Bhagavan saw to
it that his monkey companions were also included in the
festivities.2

On festive occasions like Bhagavan’s Jayanthi and
other festival days, Bhagavan would see to it that food
was taken out separately, made into balls, placed in a
basket and then taken into Palakotthu. Bhagavan used
to sit and personally hand over the balls one by one with
great joy to the monkeys. The monkeys were much
disciplined and would express their joy with a big cry in
unison. A photo has been taken of this event, at the time
of Bhagavan’s Shashtipurthi (60th birthday) festivities in
1939. One can see the radiant smile on Bhagavan’s face
in the photograph. This  photo is  there in
Ramanasramam.3

Bhagavan used to refer Sri Rama Navami days as
‘monkeys’ day’. He was insisting that they must be fed
sumptuously especially on that day. On a Sri Rama
Navami day one of the attendants Vaikuntavasar, was sent
to Palakotthu with a lot of tasty food to feed the monkeys.
Bhagavan fol lowed it  up and enquired from
Vaikuntavasar as to what happened. He replied, “When
I went there, there were only two or three monkeys. But
after a time, all of them came. They were well fed. They
did not quarrel or bite each other.”

Bhagavan said, “They don’t fight when there is
enough for all. All trouble arises only when there is a



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 107

want. They also raise a big cry as an indication of their
joy, whenever they get plenty to eat.” 4

Bhagavan’s devotee G.V. Subbaramayya records that on
Mother’s Aradhana day i.e. Mahapuja day, the monkeys were
given a big feast supervised personally by Bhagavan. A vast
troop of monkeys sat in a row. They came in turns to receive
food from Bhagavan’s hands in a most orderly manner. After
all of them were fed, they screeched in chorus with joy as if
to say thanks to mother Alagammal.5

30. KINDNESS TO MONKEYS
One afternoon in 1946, at 2 pm. some savouries

prepared in the Ashram were distributed amongst the
devotees. A few of them were given to Bhagavan also.
Bhagavan ate them, drank some water, went out and came
back, when some monkeys came to the window near his
sofa. Seeing them, Bhagavan asked his attendants to go and
bring some of the savoury preparations, saying that the
monkeys would relish them very much. The attendants
returned saying that the people in the kitchen refused saying
that they had not prepared enough savouries to feed the
monkeys also.

“Oho! How did we get them then?” said Bhagavan.
“This is ration time,” said a devotee.

“What if it is ration time? When we have rations,
why should they (monkeys) not have rations as well?
The problem will be solved if a ration card is obtained
for the monkeys as well. They will eat these things with
greater relish than we. If they do not have it, why should we



108 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

have it either? When we are eating, see how those children
(i.e., the monkeys) are looking at us,” said Bhagavan.

Thereupon, they also got their share.
From that time onwards, Bhagavan used to accept things

only after the monkeys’ share was given to them. It seems,
earlier it was a routine to set aside a share of food and
prasadam for the monkeys and other animals before
distribution to human devotees.1

31. REMINISCENCES
Bhagavan’s devotee Devaraja Mudaliar writes in his book

Day by Day with Bhagavan  that monkeys would get their
share of plantains everyday after parayana in the evening as
a parting gift of the day:

“After parayana when the monkeys were getting their
usual parting gifts of fruit of the day, Bhagavan recounted
his old experiences with monkeys at Skandasramam, and
how his favourite ‘The Hobbler’ (the lame one) hurt him
twice. After the monkeys were given plantain, one was
left, and Bhagavan said ‘Give it to these children’ pointing
to four of them who were there, adding, ‘These are tail-
less monkeys.’”1

Dr. K Subrahmanian (Dr. K.S.) founder of Ramana
Kendra, Hyderabad recalls how Bhagavan treated animals
and birds with great concern and affection:

“Sometimes, a couple of monkeys would walk into
the meditation hall. Some devotees would get agitated.
Bhagavan would gently call the monkeys and give them
cashew nuts or groundnuts. They would go away
screeching with delight.”2



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 109

Col. A.N.S. Murthi, who met Bhagavan as a teen-aged
boy, recollects the following incident:

“On one occasion, Bhagavan was speaking about
monkeys sitting very near him as though they were
human beings. He would refer to one as the ‘leader’ and
eulogise his qualities of head and heart, while the monkey
would grin and make faces at us as though he was not
pleased with our manner of receiving Bhagavan’s
remarks. It was amazing to see Bhagavan offering food
in his hand and the grimacing monkey come and take it
from his hand as from a parent’s hand! How well behaved
they were with him. The next moment they hopped off
and went away bouncing over the rocks for their usual,
wild, carefree life!”3

Once Suri Nagamma wrote four verses under the
title Prarthana and placed them before Bhagavan. He
read them and began to laugh. When a devotee asked
what exactly had been written, with a smile Bhagavan
said:

“These are four verses written as prayer. The
second one is amusing. It seems after I left the hill and
settled down here, I have no monkeys to serve me. So,
Nagamma says, ‘Why not accept  “mind” which is a
monkey for service? This monkey is after material
things. Tie it down and chastise it; but you will see
that it does service to you.’ That is the idea.” Bhagavan
continued, “Sankara in Sivanandalahari wrote a
sloka more or less conveying the same idea. ‘O Sankara!
You are bhikshu (a beggar). Why not tie down my



110 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

mind, known as monkey, to your stick and go about
begging? You will get alms in abundance.” 4

Eleanor Pauline Noye, a staunch devotee from the
United States, relates the following about her departure from
the Ashram.

“The day of departing from the Ashram came. I
could not stop crying. As I walked down the hill with
Bhagavan for the last time, he alone knew what was in my
heart. The little monkeys were all lined up on either side
of the hill path. Bhagavan had told them to come and say
good bye to me. He knew I loved them too. When we
reached the hall, Bhagavan read a few comforting passages
from psalms.” 5

One day as usual someone complained to Bhagavan
about the nuisance created by the monkeys and added that
at this rate nobody would be able to remain in the Ashram.
Bhagavan laughingly remarked, “In that case the monkeys
would have Bhagavan all to themselves.”6

Thus the companionship between Bhagavan and the
monkeys continued with love and respect for each other.
But the days of the monkeys in the Ashram were over even
a few years before Bhagavan’s Nirvana. The palm-leaf roofs
outside the hall were extended, making access for monkeys
more difficult. Most of them were taken back to the jungle
or captured by the municipality. However, fifty five years
later, monkeys still receive Bhagavan’s blessings in
abundance and come for regular visits to Sri
Ramanasramam. There, even after so many years, and
despite his physical absence, Bhagavan’s teachings to



Bhagavan’s Companions – The Monkeys 111

respect animals are still cherished and the monkeys and
other animals are welcomed and provided for in the
Ashram, much as they were in days of old.
