CHAPTER – SIX

BHAGAVAN’S CHILDREN - THE DOGS

Ever since Bhagavan came to the hill of Arunachala, i.e.,
from the days of his stay at Virupaksha cave, dogs have been
his companions. They were part of the life at the Ashram. A
family of devotees and animals always surrounded Bhagavan.
The dogs were his children and grand children. Bhagavan
had great concern, love, and solicitude regarding their comfort,
cleanliness, bath, bed, food etc. He would say, “They cannot
speak the language of human beings. Therefore, even when they
are half starved, they cannot complain. They keep quiet.”

Bhagavan talked to his canine companions as if he were
talking to human beings. He would give them a few simple
directions, which they understood and obeyed. He never
treated dogs as contemptible or polluting creatures but as
fellow ascetics, who came to work out their remaining karma
in his proximity.

In fact, eminent Pandits and the Ashram dogs sat alike
on the same floor before Bhagavan and were equally attended
to in his presence. In his satsang the dogs would sit quietly.
They would behave as if they understood the happenings
and the dialogues of Bhagavan and his devotees.

Bhagavan called his dog companions, ‘boys’ or ‘children’;
just as children are served food first, these boys were also served
with meals first before the devotees were served in the dining
hall. When devotees came and offered any sweets or foodstuffs



116 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

to Bhagavan, the ‘children’ were given their share first. When
Prasad was given in the hall, these dogs would not touch the
Prasad, unless and until Bhagavan had taken his portion.
Professor Venkataramaiah has said in his diary that once a pandit
wanted to test this and spread some food before the dogs. But
they did not touch it, nor even look at it. After a while,
Bhagavan put a morsel of the offered food into his mouth and
immediately the dogs fell upon their food and devoured it.2

Bhagavan had great concern and care for the lives of
the dogs specially, when pups were born to a bitch.
Sometimes, he would act as a mid-wife. When ill, they were
looked after well with nursing care and medicines, and at
the time of death of these animal companions, Bhagavan
would generally be by their side attending to their last
moments of life. When they breathed their last, they were
given a solemn and proper burial in or near the Ashram.

When thieves came to rob the Ashram, Bhagavan’s
concern was more for the dogs – Karuppan who was sick
and for Jackie, than for the human devotees. When the thieves
beat up the dogs, Bhagavan was distressed and asked the
devotees to take the dogs outside the hall first.

Bhagavan knew their pedigree. “This Kamala is the
ancient dame. Her children are that Neela and this Jackie.
Rose and these little imps are her grand children.” He would
introduce them to a newcomer. Addressing the little pups,
when Kamala lay dying, he said, “You little fellows, why do
you not go and see your granny. She is going to leave you soon.”
After Kamala’s death, he addressed Rose just as he would
address and sympathise with any human disciple who came



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 117

to him in his bereavement, “Rose, you have lost your granny
Kamala”3

The lineage of the Ashram dogs was named like the
lineage of the Monarchs of England e.g. Jack I, Jack II, Jack
III etc. The Ashram had several dogs manifesting great
intelligence and serving strangers as guides like St. Bernard
dogs. They would take the newcomers round the Arunachala
hill showing every Lingam, Thirtha, temples and caves and
bring them back to the Ashram.

The canines were Bhagavan’s devotees, companions and
members of the Ashram family.

1. CHINNA KARUPPAN
One of the most remarkable of all the dogs was Chinna

Karuppan (Little Blackie).
Bhagavan himself has given an account of Karuppan:

“Chinna Karuppan was black all over, hence his
name. He was of high principles. When we were in the
Virupaksha cave a black object used to pass us sometimes
from a distance. We were able sometimes to see his head
peeping over the bushes, which showed his vairagya (non-
attachment) to be very strong. He kept company with
none; he seemed in fact to avoid company. We respected
his independence and vairagya and used to leave food for
him near his place.

One day, as we were going up, Karuppan suddenly
jumped across the path and romped upon me wagging his
tail in glee. How he singled me out of the group for the
display of his affection was surprising. Thereafter he
remained with us in the Ashram as one of the inmates. A



118 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

very intelligent and serviceable fellow he was; and how
high-souled! He lost all his former aloofness and proved
very affectionate. Universal brotherhood was his motto.
He would hobnob with every visitor and inmate, get up
on his lap and nestle close to him. His overtures would be
mostly well received. A few, however, tried to avoid him.
But he was indefatigable in his efforts and would take no
refusal as final. If, however, he was ordered off, he obeyed
like a monk observing the vow of obedience.

“Once he went near an orthodox Brahmin who was
engaged in repetition of some mantram at the foot of a
bilwa tree close to our cave. The Brahmin considered dogs
to be impure and scrupulously avoided their contact and
even proximity. Yet Chinna Karuppan insisted on going
near him. An inmate of the Ashram, out of consideration
for the Brahmin’s feelings, raised his stick and beat him
but not hard, and he wailed and ran away. Karuppan,
who then left the Ashram, was never found again. He
would not care to approach a place where he was ill
treated even once, so sensitive was he! In fact that inmate
had made this mistake despite previous warning, evidently
because he did not rate this dog’s principles and
sensitiveness at their proper worth.

 “The warning given to one and all was this: Once
before Palaniswami had been rude in chastising Chinna
Karuppan. It was on a cold rainy night; all the same
Chinna Karuppan left the precincts and lay outside under
a bag of charcoal some distance from the Ashram, whence
he was recalled only next morning.



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 119

“Another warning had been given from the
behaviour of a little dog. One swami, some years back,
chided the little dog who was with us in Virupaksha cave.
He at once ran down into the Sankhatirtam tank, in
which his carcass was seen floating after some time. That
swami and all others were at once told that dogs and other
animal inmates of the Ashram have intelligence and
principles of their own and should not be roughly dealt
with. We do not know what souls may be tenanting these
bodies and for finishing what portion of their unfinished
Karma they seek our company.”1

Bhagavan thus remembered Chinna Karuppan, the earliest
of the Ashram dogs. Bhagavan never ill-treated nor looked down
on any animal. He had Samabhavatvam for all beings.

2. JACK - I
Jack I came to Bhagavan, when Bhagavan was staying

in Virupaksha cave. He was a great Tapasvi, self-restrained
and a lover of peace. He was very quiet and never quarrelled
with other dogs.

His daily routine was as follows:
He would take his breakfast from a dancing girl’s house,

the food which she had offered to Lord Arunachala, in the
morning. After eating it, he would accompany the priest to
Guhainamasivayar temple on the hill, and then he would
climb up higher to Virupaksha cave to meet Bhagavan and
would squat in the adjoining cave and remain there in silence
and peace. He would not associate with other dogs or be
party to their quarrels and squabbles. At 9.00 or 9.30 am.,



120 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

he would go down and be just in time to take the naiveidya,
the food offered to God at Guhai Namasivayar temple and
return to the cave. He would take what was given to him (if
any) at the Ashram and not hanker after things to eat at other
times or run about with other dogs. In the evening, he would
go to the dancing girl’s house for the night meal and sleep at
Seniyarmatam with the priest. His life was just the life of a
quiet hermit, Satvic Vairagi seeking to end his days at the
feet of Arunachala. Jack I, combined strict obedience with
his intelligence and sensitiveness.

Once, Bhagavan and others started from Virupaksha
cave for giripradakshina, walking directly across the hill and
into the forest for a good distance. When starting, Bhagavan
said to Jack, “Now we are going for giripradakshina, you better
go down to the town.”  They started walking southwards across
the hill and had gone over a mile when they saw Jack running
down the hill to the town, i.e., towards the east, in obedience
to the order given him. After some distance he was lost to
sight. After they had walked over a mile in their own direction
into the forest, they noted that Jack had gone down and re-
ascended the hill and waited for them in the track they had
chosen. Bhagavan told him then: “You better go back to the
Ashram and not come with us.” And away went the dog,
probably sore at heart to part company from his master,
prizing obedience as paramount duty.

One morning, a Cheetah attacked and mauled Jack’s
neck. Jack set up a howl that was heard at Guhai Namasivayar
temple. The priest there raised a cry and the cheetah left
Jack and ran away. In 1905–06, when plague raged in



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 121

Tiruvannamalai for five or six months, the houses were all
deserted. Jack had stayed with the priest on the hill at Guhai
Namasivayar temple, somehow failing to accompany
Bhagavan to Pachiamman koil. During those months cheetahs
were openly visiting the deserted streets even by day from
adjoining forests. Jack, who had earlier escaped from the jaws
of the cheetah, seems to have fallen an easy prey to these
cheetahs.1

3. KAMALA
The ancestry of most of the dogs of the Ashram can be

traced to Kamala. The female pup came to Skandasramam
from some unknown place. The devotees tried to drive her
away, fearing that she would litter the Ashram with pups
year after year. She put up with all the rudeness from
devotees, but she refused to go. The Ashram devotees called
her Kamala. A large canine family indeed grew up around
her. Bhagavan treated them all with equal consideration.

On the occasion of her first delivery, Kamala was given
a bath; her forehead was smeared with turmeric and
decorated with vermillion on the centre of her forehead. She
was given a clean place in the Ashram (Skandasramam) where
she remained for ten days with her pups. On the tenth day,
her purification was celebrated with regular feasting.

Kamala was an intelligent and serviceable dog. Bhagavan
would often ask her to take a newcomer round the hill,
“Kamala take this stranger round the hill! ” She would guide
the new visitor to every deity, tank, and shrine around the hill.

Jack II, and Neela were her children and Rose was her
grand daughter.1



122 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

4. NEELA
Kavyakanta Ganapathi Muni who was affectionately

called as Nayana (father) used to say that dogs cannot survive
long, if they have contact with a person like Bhagavan who is
a Jnani. Therefore, he used to drive away the dogs if they
came near Bhagavan. In this connection, Bhagavan said, “Yes,
he used to drive the dogs away. But there was an exception. A
dog we called Neela (daughter of Kamala), used to come and
always sit on my lap. But she would not allow any other dog,
even her own mother, sisters, or brothers, to pass beyond the gate
of Skandasramam. Nayana used to say ‘This one has been born
a dog by mistake.’”1

5. THE RED DOG
When Bhagavan was living on the hill, a male pup

came to the Skandasramam. They named him Segappan
because he was almost red in colour. He was a very quiet,
peaceful and loving dog. He would never go and fight with
any other dog. “He is a Jnani”, Bhagavan used to say. He
always used to be near Bhagavan during daytime. After
evening he would go back to his house in the town.
Bhagavan would sometimes ask him to take the new devotees
round the Arunachala Hill and he would guide them
showing them the important places round the hill and bring
them back to the Ashram.

He used to take his food only twice a day, never eating
between the meals. If anybody gave him food he would not
touch it. In the evening exactly at 5 O’ clock, he would come to
Bhagavan and touch his feet. Then Bhagavan would tell the
ashramites, “It is 5 O’ clock. Segappan has to go back to his home



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 123

in the town. Will you give him his food? ” The ashramites would
give Segappan his meal. After eating, Segappan would go back
to his house, spend the night there, and again would be present
in the Ashram early next morning to be with Bhagavan.1

6. GURU’S COMMAND IS UPADESA
Kunjuswami came to Bhagavan, in the year 1920. At

that time Bhagavan was staying in Skandasramam.
Kunjuswami, in his reminiscences, has written about the days
he spent with Bhagavan and his experiences.

Kunjuswami recalls the first meeting he had with Bhagavan.
Kunjuswami decided that he would take whatever words come
out of Bhagavan’s divine mouth as Veda vakya (The Gospel
Truth). The Guru’s upadesa was command to him.

This is what Kunjuswami shared with other devotees:
“That day after the midday meal, I was alone with

Bhagavan. Silence prevailed between us. Then to my great
virtue (luck), Bhagavan showed me a practical
demonstration of universal love that remained imprinted
in my mind forever.

“There was no one in the place except Bhagavan and
me. Bhagavan who had not spoken a single word to me
till then, took some flour from the small tin, put it in a
pot, poured some water from the Kamandalu over it,
mixed it with a spoon and then heated the mixture on the
charcoal stove, that was kept there to heat the room.

“Thinking that Bhagavan is making some ‘Elixir’
(Magical concoction), I sat quietly hoping to get some
myself. After the contents were cooked and turned into
gruel, Bhagavan took the vessel off the stove, poured a



124 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

portion of it on a plate. Then he got up and lifted an
upturned basket under which there were four pups. The
four pups rushed out and raced towards the gruel on the
plate. Thinking that it might be too hot for them,
Bhagavan tried to ward them off but was unable to do so.

“Bhagavan, who had not spoken to me till then, called
out to me and said ‘catch hold of the four!’

“I immediately caught hold of them.
“As I had resolved to take Bhagavan’s words as upadesa

to me I took the first command to mean, ‘catch hold of the
four Mahavakyas’ (Tattvamasi – Prajnanam Brahma-
Aham Brahmasmi – Ayamatma Brahma). I decided that I
have to understand them and follow them. After the gruel
had cooled, Bhagavan said, ‘Release them one by one.’ I
released the pups one by one. I took this utterance as the
second upadesa of Bhagavan, meaning that I should give
up one by one all my worldly attachments and desires.

“The pups with their stomach full tottered along. One
of them passed urine. Bhagavan got up, washed the spot
with water and wiped it with an old gunny bag. I had an
urge to help him. I refrained from doing so, as I thought
that I should not do anything without his permission.
Bhagavan came back after cleaning the place. Then another
pup fouled up the place, and seeing my restlessness he said,
‘Wipe it’ I wiped and cleaned the place. I took this
utterance ‘wipe it’ as Bhagavan’s third upadesa. ‘Wipe the
mind and keep it clean.’ That is clean the mind of six
impurities. [Kama- Desire, Krodha – anger, Lobha –
greed, Moha – ignorance, Mada- arrogance, Matsara -



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 125

jealousy]. These are six types of impurities or the negativities
of the mind. Having got the upadesa I desired, I experienced
great happiness and peace.”1

7. ADVANCED SOULS WHO CAME TO BHAGAVAN
Bhagavan could recognise spiritual greatness or maturity

in the people around him. He could also discern it in some
of the animals that came to visit him or live with him.

When Bhagavan was in Skandasramam, there was a dog,
which would be always on Bhagavan’s lap or near him.
Bhagavan’s mother, Alagammal one day asked him, “Why
does that dog like to stay always on your lap?”

Rangan, who was Bhagavan’s childhood friend and who
became his devotee after, was sitting near Bhagavan.
Bhagavan turned to Rangan and said, “This dog is always in
unwavering ‘Samadhi’. A great soul has come in the form of a
dog. Mother does not know this.”

Hearing this Rangan thought, “If a dog can learn to go
into ‘Samadhi’, it should not be too difficult a feat for me.”

Rangan asked Bhagavan, “Why don’t you teach me that
Nirvikalpa samadhi?’”

“What good is it to you?” replied Bhagavan. “You must be
in Sahaja Nirvikalpa Samadhi. While living in the world, you
must be unattached to the world. While seeing everything, you
must be in a state of unseeing.”

A few years later, Bhagavan told Rangan a little about
another dog.

“There used to be a dog in Skandasramam, she
always slept in front of me. Even when she was awake,
she would be immersed in ‘Samadhi’. When she got



126 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

pregnant, the ashramites chased her away because she may
deliver unwanted pups in the Ashram. She went some
distance up the hill and delivered the pups there. She used
to suckle them on the Hill, leave them there and then
come and lie down in front of me.”

Around that time, a friend of Rangan from Madurai,
who was very orthodox, came to see Bhagavan. As he was
sitting down to his meal, one of the pups came and sniffed
Rangan’s friend’s plate. Rangan’s friend refused to eat the
meal, because he felt that the pup had contaminated the food.
Bhagavan smiled at his refusal and told him the story of
Dattatreya, the famous Guru of ancient India:

“Once, Dattatreya took his disciple to see his Guru.
The Guru gave Dattatreya a piece of bread dipped in
fresh milk of a dog. Dattatreya shared it with his disciple,
who felt disgusted towards dog’s milk. He threw the piece
of bread away without eating it. But, Dattatreya who
came to know about this took the piece of bread which his
Guru was eating and gave it to his disciple to eat.”

Bhagavan told this story to Rangan’s friend to make him
understand that pollution, contamination, etc. are all for this
body and not for the Atma. After hearing the story Rangan’s
friend ate the food.

 Rangan used to say that dogs, monkeys, crows, squirrels,
sheep and many other animals would come to see Bhagavan
and many of them instinctively recognised his benevolence
and greatness. It is only man who uses his mind is slow to
recognise Bhagavan’s greatness.1



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 127

THE CANINES IN SRI RAMANASRAMAM
In 1922, December, Bhagavan left Skandasramam and

came and stayed near his mother’s Samadhi. Not only the
human devotees but also his canine companions followed
him and stayed near his mother’s Samadhi. Some of them
were descendants of Kamala.

8. JACKIE - II
Among Bhagavan’s canine companions, Jackie II had an

honoured place. Jackie, the son of Kamala was born in Sri
Ramanasramam. When he was a pup he never mixed with other
dogs, nor did he play much. Instead he lived the life of a sadhu.
He would sit in front of Bhagavan on an orange cloth that had
been provided by a devotee and stare intently at Bhagavan’s
eyes. He was always looked after well as he behaved in such an
exemplary manner and as also Bhagavan had a lot of love for
him. Ramaswami Pillai in particular, took good care of him.
Every day he would wash Jackie with soap and water and remove
insects from Jackie’s body, if any. Whatever prasad was
distributed, Jackie would not eat until Bhagavan began to eat
his own portion. On such occasions, he would watch Bhagavan’s
face intently. As soon as Bhagavan put a morsel in his mouth,
Jackie would start to eat his own portion.

Annamalai Swami, a devotee recalls an incident concerning
Jackie that occurred while Bhagavan was sitting by the well,
surrounded by devotees. Jackie was also sitting with the devotees,
looking intently at Bhagavan. A stray dog entered the Ashram
through the back gate and Jackie distracted by the new comer,
began to bark. Bhagavan gently chided him by saying, “You just



128 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

close your eyes. You just close your eyes. If you do this you will not be
able to see the dog.” Jackie obeyed at once, but some of the devotees
continued to look at the stray dog.

When Annamalai Swami saw what was happening, he
laughed and remarked, “This is a good teaching. It is not
only for Jackie, the dog, but for all of us.”1

9. JACKIE’S ENDURANCE
One day a ferocious pig and Jackie II had a fight during

which Jackie was mauled badly and his stomach was torn
and the intestines had come out. Chinna Swami, Bhagavan’s
brother and the manager of the Ashram, put Jackie in a
basket and carried him over his head to a veterinary doctor.
A major operation with many stitches on the stomach had to
be done. After the operation, Jackie was laid on a soft bed in
the Mantapam, in front of the Ashram.

At the same time, Kunjuswami an ashramite, who had
a severe abscess on his foot, was suffering from severe pain.
He was also lying in the same mantapam. Both Kunjuswami
and Jackie II were well attended to by an Ashram devotee,
who also stayed in that mantapam with them.

Bhagavan came to the mantapam to enquire after the
health of the patients. Kunjuswami, who could not bear the
pain, cried aloud and fell at Bhagavan’s feet. He begged
Bhagavan to relieve him of his pain. Bhagavan, full of
compassion, said, “See how Jackie is silently bearing his pain
after such a major operation, without as much as a whimper! ”

Looking at Jackie’s endurance, Kunjuswamy felt
ashamed and did not complain any more. Bhagavan stayed
a little longer, enquired about their food, medicine etc, from



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 129

the attender. After giving solace to Kunjuswami, and patting
and petting Jackie, Bhagavan returned to the Ashram. After
getting solace from nectar-like words of Bhagavan,
Kunjuswami, slept peacefully.1

This incident shows Bhagavan’s equal love and solicitude
for human beings and animals alike. The care and compassion
he had for living beings was unsurpassed.

10. JACKIE’S LAST DAYS
The manner of Jackie’s death is interesting:
It was a few weeks before the Pongal festival of 1933. Jackie

fell ill. He was very sick. Bhagavan arranged a soft bed for him,
attending to his wants. Jackie refused to take any solid food and
he was fed on milk. Jackie did not leave the presence of Bhagavan
ever since he fell sick, Bhagavan attended on Jackie very
affectionately. After a few days Jackie grew weaker and he was
emitting a very bad smell. But it made no difference to Bhagavan.
He would take Jackie in his arms and caress Jackie lovingly. Jackie
died peacefully in Bhagavan’s arms. Although Jackie’s death could
have come any time, he survived till the festival of Pongal was
over. The parallel between Jackie’s death and that of Bhishma,
the grand old warrior of Mahabharata was not missed by
Bhagavan. With the death of Jackie, Kamala’s lineage came to
an end. Jackie was buried in the Ashram precincts near the
samadhis of Valli the deer and the blessed crow. A small
monument was built over Jackie’s Samadhi.1

11. BHAGAVAN AND THE PUP
There was a pup in the Ashram. It loved to sleep on

Bhagavan’s lap and would sleep as Bhagavan never said ‘no’.



130 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

But the attendants felt that it was an inconvenience for
Bhagavan. Therefore the attendants made a pillow of coconut
twine and placed it on a table near Bhagavan’s couch. They
tried to coax the pup to sleep on that pillow. When the pup
refused, he was forced to sleep on it. As soon as the attendants’
back was turned, the pup would invariably end up on
Bhagavan’s lap and sleep there. When attendants returned
to the hall, they would put the pup on the coconut pillow
again. But the pup would be back on Bhagavan’s lap. This
became a routine. In due course, the little fellow got used to
the table near Bhagavan. He would sit on the table
majestically and look at the attendants teasingly.

Meanwhile, Bhagavan watching the tiff between the
attendants and the pup, questioned the attendants, “Why do
you hurt the pup? What will happen if he sits on my lap? Why
such discrimination? It is pure illusion. You treat the pup this
way because he is in the skin of a dog. If you are born in the
same skin imagine how you would feel? Let the pup enjoy its
freedom and sit wherever he wants”.

The pup, still sitting on the table near Bhagavan, appeared
to be looking at the attendant with derision. From that day
onwards, the attendants did not bother the pup anymore. They
were ashamed of their attitude towards the pup.

Acharya Vinoba Bhave once said about Bhagavan “No
Rishi born on this earth ever showed such equal vision.” How
true it was!1

12. SPEECHLESS APPEAL
Jagadeeswara Shastri and his wife were devotees of

Bhagavan. They lived in Ramana Nagar near the Ashram.



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 131

They had a dog, which would accompany them to the
Ashram every day. He would come and sit quietly in front of
Bhagavan with Jagadeeswara Shastri. He was a particularly
intelligent dog and a very well behaved one as well. When
his master Jagadeeswara Shastri left the hall, the dog would
also leave the hall with him. He was very keen to come to the
hall and sit in front of Bhagavan. People tried to prevent the
dog from coming to the hall but it was no use. Bhagavan also
said, “Let him come. He is not harming anybody. He sits in
front of me and goes back.”

Once Jagadeeswara Shastri and his wife entrusted the
dog to somebody and went to Madras (Chennai) for fifteen
days. During the first four or five days, the dog used to
search for them, go round the hall, and then go about all
the places which Jagadeeswara Shastri and his wife used to
frequent. He got tired and even perhaps disgusted with
those fruitless efforts. Finally, one morning at about 10
O’clock, the dog came to the hall. He stood near Bhagavan’s
sofa, staring fixedly at Bhagavan. At that time Suri
Nagamma was sitting in the first row. Bhagavan was reading
the newspaper. Krishnaswami and others tried to send the
dog out by threats but in vain. Nagamma also asked the
dog to go out. But the dog did not move. Bhagavan’s
attention was diverted by this noise. Bhagavan observed for
a while the look of the dog, which was staring at him.
Bhagavan put the paper aside and, understood the silent
language of the dog. He waved his hand towards him and
said, “Why, what is the matter? You are asking where your
people have gone. Oh, I see, I understand. They have gone to



132 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Madras. They will be back in a week. Don’t be afraid. Don’t
be worried. Be calm. Is it all right? Now, go.”

Hardly had Bhagavan completed his instructions, the
dog turned and left. Soon after that Bhagavan remarked to
Nagamma, “Do you see that? The dog is asking me where his
people have gone and when they are returning. However much
the people here tried to send him away, he would not move until
I answered his questions.”1

Once, it seems, the lady of the house, Mrs. Jagadeeswara
Shastri punished the dog with a cane for something he had
done and locked him up in a room for half a day. After he was
let out, he came straight to Bhagavan as if to complain against
her and stayed in the Ashram for four or five days without
going to their house. Bhagavan arranged food for the dog
and when that lady came to the Ashram, he admonished her,
“What have you done to the dog? Why is he angry with you? He
came and complained to me. Why? What have you done? ” She
admitted her fault in Bhagavan’s presence and, with a good
deal of cajoling, got the dog to go home with her.1

13. TEACHING THROUGH DOGS
Most of the time Bhagavan used to be in maunam

(silence). He did not talk much. He was only a witness to the
happenings in his presence, which was his natural state. There
was a live vibrancy in his silence, which the devotees
experienced. Bhagavan had a way of correcting his sincere
devotees; sometimes by telling them directly, but more often
by subtle methods and uplift them spiritually.

Siva Prakasam Pillai had Bhagavan’s darshan (audience)
in 1902. Siva Prakasam is best remembered for the role he



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 133

played in getting Bhagavan, who was in maunam at that time to
write down his teachings on “Self enquiry.” The answers are
compiled by Siva Prakasam and published under the title “Nan
Yar?” (Who am I?)

Once, Siva Prakasam was meditating in the hall sitting in
front of Bhagavan. Suddenly there were negative thoughts which
polluted his mind. Pillai himself talks about it in the poems
written by him praying to Bhagavan to bestow his Grace and
correct him.

Sri Ramana Deva Malai (Verse: 16):
“When you knew that I was committing a sinful act, in

order to correct me, you looked at a dog that came to you
with human excreta in its mouth and warned it either to give
up the habit or to stay away from your presence. Ramana
Deva through that dog you implanted that command in me.”

In one of his other poems, Siva Prakasam Pillai revealed
that his ‘sinful act’ was his continuing desire for women.

“‘Like the dog that eats human excreta, do not wander
seeking women. If you associate with them, do not come, and
stand near me,’ Sri Ramana said this (indirectly to me).
‘Oh mind, did you act according to this injunction? You
yourself think deeply over this”’.

His guilt appeared to stem from a conviction, expressed
in one of the stray verses, “Among the many crores of sins,
the worst is to disobey the command of the Guru.”1

Only the person concerned would know, that such a
remark was meant for him as guidance or admonition. This
was well known to all intimate devotees, who had experienced
Bhagavan’s Grace and kindness in various matters.



134 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Bhagavan’s devotee Devaraja Mudaliar also had the same
experience. He recollects:

“I once visited Bhagavan in 1923 or 1924. I was
worried about finding a suitable match for my daughter.
As I was sitting in front of Bhagavan, an old devotee
brought an offering of food stuff. Bhagavan touched and
took a little for himself. The offering was distributed
among the devotees.

“There were two dogs sitting in the hall. One of them
followed the tray begging for some but the other remained
where he was, quiet and patient. Bhagavan, referring to
the latter, said this fellow knows he will get his allotted
share. And when Bhagavan said this I felt as if he was
saying that to me. ‘Why are you so anxious about this
marriage? What has been already destined for your daughter,
you will get’.”2

Thus, Bhagavan had different methods to convey what
he wanted to tell his devotees.

14. DOG PREFERS BHAGAVAN’S COMPANY
Sri K. Swaminathan in his reminiscences in Face to Face

with Bhagavan writes:
“Bhagavan succeeded in being a friend of everyone

– saint or sinner, prince or peasant, learned or ignorant,
cow, dog or monkey. J.C. Molony (a district collector)
who had gone to the hill to have darshan of Bhagavan has
recorded how his dog preferred the company of Bhagavan
to his own. ‘After visiting the sage on the hill, when I reached
my camp, one of my dogs was missing. In the evening arrived
the holy man leading the truant on a string. The sage said,



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 135

‘He came back to me, and I should have liked to keep him.
But why should I steal him from you?’”

All were welcomed to his charmed circle. Bhagavan gave
freedom to all to enjoy his Saameepya (nearness) and his
Soulabhya (easy accessibility).

15. TERRITORIAL RIGHTS
Usually the dogs have their own territory with the

boundaries marked. A dog from one territory cannot trespass
another dog’s territory.

Once, when an Ashram dog saw a new dog from another
territory trespass into the Ashram, he was very angry and
barked at the intruder. He was trying to fight with the new
dog to drive him away from the Ashram. According to him,
the Ashram belonged only to him. The ashramites were
trying to pacify the Ashram dog and prevent him from
fighting with the new dog.

When Bhagavan came out from the hall, and saw the
situation he felt amused and said, “This dog is also like human
beings. Is it not natural for those people, who come earlier and
get established, to feel that the place belongs to them and try to
boss over the new comer?” Then he turned towards the dog
and said, “Why do you bark at the new comer and boss over it?
Let the new dog also stay. Leave this place and go and sit in your
place.” Hearing this, the Ashram dog left the place obediently
and quietly.1

16. PUPS ARE LIKE SMALL KIDS
Bhagavan had the same concern for all beings – be it a

human or an animal. He demonstrated it also.



136 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

a) There was a small pup in the Ashram. He was always
near Bhagavan and often used to sit on his couch also. One
day, the pup urinated on Bhagavan’s couch. The attendants
were very angry and were about to beat the pup. But
compassionate Bhagavan came to the rescue of the pup and
defended the pup, “The pups of the dogs should be treated as
small children. Do you get angry if a small human child had
done it?” So saying, Bhagavan got up to clean the place. The
attendants felt ashamed of themselves for treating the pup
badly. They cleaned the place themselves.

b) There was another puppy, which would always relieve
herself on a spot near the office. Chinna Swami would get
furious and try to drive her out of the Ashram, but Bhagavan
would come to the rescue of the puppy, saying that if some
child did the same thing nobody would be angry, and the
puppy was only a child and knew no better.1

17. PERMISSION TO EAT
Bhagavan was concerned about dogs and took care of

their meals. He would know if a dog has taken the food
properly or not. There was a white dog in the Ashram. He
would eat his food only after Bhagavan asked him to.

One day that dog was missing. Bhagavan knew that
the dog had not had his breakfast. He asked his attendants
to look for the white dog. The attendants found the white
dog sitting in a corner of the Ashram. They brought the
dog to Bhagavan. He was happy to see him, and asked the
attendants to bring iddlies from the kitchen. When they
were brought, Bhagavan put them in front of the white
dog and asked him to eat. At once, the dog ate the food.



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 137

The white dog would not accept food from anybody except
from Bhagavan.

Jackie II too would not eat unless Bhagavan gave food.
One day a devotee gave a piece of bread to Jackie in the hall.
He refused to eat it. Bhagavan told the devotee not to get
upset because Jackie was not accepting the food offered to
him, as he was a Tapasvi. 1

18. TREATMENT FOR RICKETS
There was a puppy born in the Ashram. It was very

weak, down with rickets. Bhagavan was very solicitous and
looked after it very well. Under Bhagavan’s direction
Bhagavan’s devotee, Sadhu Arunachala (A.W. Chadwick) was
asked to help Bhagavan in nursing the dog back to health.
Chadwick gave biochemical medicines and nursed the dog
back to health. Eventually when the dog was cured, strangely
enough, he ran away. Bhagavan was concerned about the
dog, thinking some predator might have killed it. But, months
later the dog returned with a new master. It was strange and
inexplicable as he showed no interest in anybody, even in
Bhagavan. That was dog’s Prarabdha. He was not ripe enough
to be near Bhagavan.1

19. DUTY BOUND CANINE
There was a dog in the Ashram, which was a good

watchman. If some intruder or a new face came to the
Ashram, he would bark trying to drive him out. People
got angry at the dog for making such a noise; Bhagavan
would defend him saying he was only doing his duty by
the Ashram.1



138 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

20. THE SAINT WHO CAME IN THE NIGHT
One night, a dog was barking loudly in the rear of the

Ashram. Bhagavan, who was sleeping in the hall, got up and
told his attendants, “I think a saint has come to the Ashram in
the form of a dog. He must be hungry. Please go to the kitchen
and see if any food is there. Bring water and food.” The
attendants went to the kitchen and got some food and water.
Bhagavan took the food and water to the place where the
dog was, placed the food and water in front of him and came
back to the hall. The saint who had come in the dog’s form
ate the food and happily went away. Nobody saw him again.

Bhagavan, later explained, “It was some ‘Siddha’
(saint) who had taken the form of a dog and had come
here to have a meal. There are many siddhas around,
and they do not wish to make themselves known. That is
why they come like this”.1

21. GIVER OF THE BOON
“A giver of the boons to the devotees; the guru even of
the great Ganapathi, Master of Mantras; like the
celestial tree, he assuages the anguish of those who
seek the shadow of his feet”

(Sri Ramana Gita, Ch.18, V.15)
In whatever form devotees came to Bhagavan and

prayed with devotion and faith, his Grace would grant them
whatever they prayed for. He never failed. He was like
Kalpavruksha, the wish fulfilling celestial tree.

Bhagavan rarely used to go out before midnight, for
answering nature’s call. One night he had to go out due to a
stomach disorder. The attendant, who was standing some



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 139

distance away, noticed that Bhagavan took an unusually longer
time to return. So in the dark, he went nearer Bhagavan. He
heard a peculiar sound, like ‘slop, slop, slop’ - the vigorously
licking sound of an animal. Bhagavan was talking to someone,
“Enough, enough! Are you not yet content?” The attendant saw
Bhagavan uncomfortably seated, in an odd position and a dog
most excitedly and vigorously licking Bhagavan from top to
bottom! The dog being thin and bony was stinking with sores,
was full of disease and very ugly to look at. The attendant felt
that he should drive the dog away immediately. Bhagavan
sensing this, pleadingly said, “He has been very keen in showering
his affection on me all these days. Poor fellow! He was always
driven away before he could do so. Today he caught me in the
dark. He has conveyed to me his intense love by licking me to his
heart’s content!” Bhagavan got up, with tears in his eyes, took
leave of the dog, saying, “Porumda. Isn’t it enough? May I go
now?” The next morning the dog was dead.1

Was he ‘dead’? Or ‘Liberated’!

22. BHAGAVAN’S COMPASSION
Sri R. Sivaraman was the brother of Vishwanatha Swami.

Sivaraman was nine years old when in 1923 he visited the
Ashram for the first time and had the darshan of Bhagavan.

One morning, Sivaraman was sitting in the kitchen
in front of Bhagavan who was cutting vegetables, when
Chinnaswami came from the town with a basket on his
head. Sivaraman asked Bhagavan what he was carrying
and he replied, “Last night one of the dogs was badly mauled
by an angry pig. He had taken the wounded dog to the
veterinary hospital.”



140 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Then Bhagavan and Sivaraman went to the hall. In the
hall along with other devotees were the three dogs – Kamala,
Karuppan II, and another dog. There was a conch that was
lying there. Sivaraman took it and blew on it. The dogs got
scared and started howling. Bhagavan was concerned and
advised Sivaraman to stop blowing the conch, as it irritated
the dogs. The concern and compassion, he had for animals
got imprinted in Sivaraman’s heart.1

23. BHAGAVAN’S SOLICITUDE
Bhagavan would visit the goshala once a day. While

returning from the goshala, he would stop by a dog, which
had littered. He would talk to the mother, pet the pups, clear
their eyes and face with his towel. Chinnaswami once scolded
a puppy for eating human excreta. Bhagavan took the pup in
his arms, cleaned its mouth; told the pup that it should not eat
the excreta. He got iddlies from the kitchen and fed the pup.1

24. NO COMPLAINTS
Bhagavan showed disapproval when devotees brought

complaints to him. Even mild complaints would provoke a
critical rebuke.

Once, a visitor came to Bhagavan early one morning
and remarked, “Throughout the night a dog was barking. It
was making so much noise, I was unable to meditate.”

Bhagavan responded immediately by saying, “No dog
barked. It was your mind that barked,” implying that external
disturbances would not affect the mind, if attention is not
given to it.1



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 141

25. IDDLY SWAMI
There was a lame dog in the Ashram that was sickly too.

Ashramites thought that the dog would die any moment. But
under Bhagavan’s constant care, the dog recovered its health
and began to eat iddlies, which he liked very much. He ate with
so much relish that Bhagavan nicknamed him ‘Iddly Swami’.

Relating to this incident, G.V. Subbaramayya on June
15, 1945 wrote a Telugu verse meaning:

“O Sri Ramana, you once (as Lord Krishna)
straightened and beautified the hunch back woman
out of your abundant Grace: so now you have reared
this lame puppy to a fine Iddly Swami. How
wonderful.”1

26. LAKSHMANA SHARMA’S DOG
Bhagavan was once cutting vegetables for the Ashram

kitchen in the early morning before sunrise, together with
two devotees. One of them, Lakshmana Sharma, had
brought his dog with him – a handsome, pure white dog –
and it was dashing about in high spirits and refused the
food offered to it.

Bhagavan said, “You see what joy he shows? He is a highly
advanced soul who has taken this canine form.”

In those days, before Bhagavan took his meals, he would
walk round the Ashram to see if all the animals such as dogs
and birds were properly fed.1

In the later years, when the Ashram buildings had been
put up and especially when Bhagavan began to grow less
active physically, the human beings had it more their own
way and animal devotees had little access to Bhagavan.



142 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

27. THE LAME CAT
In those days, the ashramites were discouraged from

rearing a cat, as they would catch birds and squirrels and eat
them. But one day, a lame cat came to the Ashram limping.
The ashramites tried to drive her away. But she did not go.
Then Bhagavan said, “Poor thing, a Nondi (Lame) cat, let her
stay in the Ashram.” Looking at the cat, Bhagavan’s
compassion for the cat had overflowed. So the lame cat
became one of the ashramites. They named her ‘Nondi’.

But this lame cat in the Ashram annoyed everybody by
giving birth to kittens every six months. Therefore one day
without informing even Bhagavan, the lame cat along with
her kittens were taken to a village 20 miles (32 km) away and
left there. Bhagavan missed the cat but did not say anything.
After a few days, suddenly the lame cat reappeared in the
Ashram, in front of Bhagavan. He was very happy to see the
cat again. He said, “You have defeated all their efforts! Good!
Come, come,” He fondly cared for the cat. The ashramites
also got used to the cat and her kittens.1

The more one hears about the incidents portraying the
extraordinary human side of Bhagavan, the more one craves
to hear about him, “The Perfect Man; Purushottama, the
immaculate Ramana Bhagavan”.



Bhagavan’s Children – The Dogs 143

CHAPTER – SEVEN

BLESSED SOUL - VALLI THE DEER
A devotee of Bhagavan, who was a manufacturer of

match boxes, brought a little deer and offered her to
Bhagavan. Initially Bhagavan was reluctant to accept the deer
in the Ashram. “Why do we want a deer in the Ashram?”
Bhagavan asked, “Who will look after her?” Bhagavan’s
attendant Madhava Swami came forward and volunteered
to look after the deer. She was then allowed to stay in the
Ashram. The deer was named ‘Valli’. She grew up as an
Ashram pet. Bhagavan regularly fed her with rice, dal and
cashew nuts, a mixture which she greatly liked. Some
devotees also occasionally fed her with puffed rice and dal.
Valli had no interest in puffed rice; she would eat only the
dal from the bowl without sprinkling a grain outside, leaving
out the puffed rice.1

Valli often came to the hall and put her forehead on
the soles of Bhagavan’s feet. Sometimes when she did this,
Bhagavan would play with her by pushing his foot strongly
against her head. Valli would respond playfully by butting
Bhagavan’s foot. When Valli danced on her hind legs,
Bhagavan would stand alongside her, imitating her action
by dancing with his feet and waving his arms. Those who
have witnessed this dance must be very lucky indeed.

After some time Valli the deer started going to the forest
to graze with the goat-herds. People who knew that she



146 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

belonged to the Ashram brought her back. The Ashram people
also let her go to graze. She would come back on her own.

One day Valli went off to graze with some goats. Some
Panchanamas attacked her and broke her leg, hoping to kill
her and eat her flesh. This attack took place near the Easanya
Math about 2 miles (3.2 k.m.) away from the Ashram. In the
attack Valli’s leg was broken so badly that she could not return
to the Ashram. She was lying there unattended for over a day.

When Valli failed to return to the Ashram that night,
Bhagavan sent Annamalai Swami and Rangaswami to look
for her. Someone had given false information that she was
seen on one of the Muslim streets in the town. They went
there fearing that she might be dead by then. But no one
could recollect seeing her there.

However, next day Valli was found by a group of devotees
who were walking near Easanya Mutt. They bandaged her
leg and brought her back to the Ashram. A local veterinary
doctor, who was also a devotee of Bhagavan, examined her
and found her leg had broken. He bandaged it and gave a
few instructions as to how to look after her. She was put in a
corner of the dining hall on a soft bed. Bhagavan would come
every day to see her. But she did not recover from her injury.

A month later, Bhagavan sensing that she was about to
die, went to her. It was 4 o’clock early in the morning. He sat
next to her, took her on his lap. He put one hand on her head
and the other on her ‘heart centre’. Bhagavan occasionally did
this to devotees who were dying. If the devotee was a ripe soul,
then the mind would resolve into the heart and die there. When
this technique was successfully applied, the fortunate devotee



Blessed Soul – Valli the Deer 147

would attain liberation. This had been done with mother
Alagammal and Cow Lakshmi, while they were dying.

Bhagavan kept his hand on Valli’s heart for an hour.
Once, during this period, Valli urinated on Bhagavan, but
he paid no attention. He remained by her side, touching
her head and heart centre, until she finally passed away.

Later that morning Bhagavan asked Annamalai Swami
to construct a small Samadhi, near the back of the gate of
the Ashram. “We should build a Samadhi for Valli in the
Ashram itself ” Bhagavan said, “We don’t need any masons.
The two of us can build it together.”

Annamalai Swami did the masonry work. Bhagavan assisted
him by handing him the materials. When the main structure of
the Samadhi was constructed Bhagavan asked Annamalai Swami
to install a Lingam and do pooja to it. Annamalai Swami
accomplished both the tasks with Bhagavan by his side.

All this took several hours. While the Samadhi was being
built, the ceremonies were being performed. That day Bhagavan
did not go to the hall. The devotees and visitors, who had come
for his darshan, had to go where he was working.2

Valli the deer must have been a very ripe and virtuous
soul which took the body of a deer to get salvation in the
holy hands of Bhagavan.

by Suri Nagamma, Page 188.

2 Living by the Words of Bhagavan
by David Godman, Page 79 & 80.



CHAPTER – EIGHT

MONGOOSE – THE GREAT SOULS
Mongoose is a small animal which lives in the wild. It

normally does not come near humans. But a Mongoose used
to come to Bhagavan to have his darshan. Bhagavan would
say, “It must be a Siddha Purusha who has come in this form.”

1. “MAHATMA” - MONGOOSE
Bhagavan was staying at Skandasramam at that time.

He was sitting with his devotees and visitors in the veranda.
A Mongoose came and ran up to Bhagavan, sat for a while
on his lap. It was not scared of anybody nor did it hurt
anyone. It sat on Bhagavan’s lap for some time, got down
and went away on its own accord.

“Who knows why he came?” Bhagavan said, “He is not
an ordinary Mongoose. He is probably a Mahatma who had
come in Mongoose form”1

2. THE GOLDEN HUED MONGOOSE
There is another case of an extraordinary Mongoose

told by Professor Venkataramaiah in his diary Talks with Sri
Ramana Maharshi, (Talk 84):

Mr. Grant Duff asked the Master if any mongoose
had had anything to do with him. The Master said:
“Yes. It was the occasion of Ardra and Jayanti; I was living
up the hill in Skandasramam. Streams of visitors were
climbing up the hill from the town a mongoose, larger
than the ordinary size, of golden hue (not grey as a
mongoose is), with no black spot on his tail as is usual



Mongoose – TheGreat Souls 149

with the wild mongoose, passed these crowds fearlessly.
People took him to be a tame one belonging to someone
in the crowd. The animal went straight to Palaniswami,
who was having a bath in the spring by the Virupaksha
Cave. He stroked the creature and patted him. The
mongoose followed Palaniswami into the cave, inspected
every nook and corner and left the place and joined the
crowd to pass up to Skandasramam. I noticed him.
Everyone was struck by his attractive appearance and his
fearless movements. He came up to me, got on my lap and
rested there some time. Then he raised himself up, looked
about and moved down; he went round the whole place
and I followed it lest he should be harmed by the unwary
visitors or by the peacocks. Two peacocks of the place looked
at him inquisitively, whereas the mongoose moved
nonchalantly from place to place and finally disappeared
into the rocks on the south-east of the Asramam.”1

3. MASTAN OF DESUR – ACCOUNT
Bhagavan’s devotee Mastan was from Desur. Mastan was

in Skandasramam when the gold hued mongoose came on Ardra
Darshan day. It was Bhagavan’s Jayanti. Mastan was afraid that
the mongoose would hurt the two peacocks which were in
Skandasramam. But the mongoose did not hurt anybody. He
told this to Perumal, another devotee of Bhagavan. Perumal told
Mastan. “You should have caught it. If you had managed to
catch it, we could have kept it as a pet.”

Bhagavan, who was listening to this conversation, said
to Mastan, “Whom do you think he was? Do you think you
could have caught him, and do you think that this other man



150 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

could have domesticated him? That mongoose was a sage of
Arunachala who took on this form to come and visit me. He
wanted to pay his respects to me. The sages come in various
forms.”1

4. SURI NAGAMMA’S ACCOUNT
Suri Nagamma asked Bhagavan “Is it true, that, Siddha

Purushas come in various forms?” Bhagavan replied, “Yes, it
is true.” Another devotee asked Bhagavan, “Arunagiri Yogi
came in the form of a mongoose. Is it true?” Bhagavan replied,
“Yes, that is also true.”1

CHAPTER – NINE

BHAGAVAN AND THE WILD CATS
(CHEETAHS – TIGER – LEOPARDS)

In the early 20th century, there was a thick forest on
and around Arunachala Hill. There were many wild cats like
panthers, leopards, and tigers etc. inhabiting the forest. As
the town grew both in area and population, the people
encroached on the forest and the wild cats had to migrate to
other areas. Now the forest is all but destroyed. But now,
there is a growing environmental awareness in people, and
organisations like Annamalai Reforestation Society and the
State Forest Department are trying to reforest Arunachala
and the surrounding area.

In those days the cowherds or people who went to the
forest to gather wood for fuel, would bring the motherless
cubs of wild animals that they came across to Bhagavan, who
would take care of them like a natural mother. Once the
cubs grew and became independent, they were left back in
the forests. Once, even a tiger cub was brought to Bhagavan,
who was very compassionate and mother-like.

1. “PODA” (GO AWAY)
Bhagavan had no fear of wild animals nor did they have

any fear of him. Upto 1930 wild animals like leopards used to
roam on Arunachala. Annamalai Swami was an attendant to
Bhagavan in the beginning. Bhagavan never used to sleep much.



152 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

He would wake up in the middle of the night around 1:00 am
and go behind the Ashram for the calls of nature. One of the
duties of the attendant was to go with him with a lamp. Although
leopards did not come into the Ashram, they used to roam about
in the area where Bhagavan would go at night.

Annamalai recalls that on one of the nights a leopard
came near them. Bhagavan just looked at the leopard and
said, “Poda!” (go away), and the leopard simply walked away.1

2. BRUTE AND MAN ALIKE
There is a graphic account of how Bhagavan treated

wild animals and humans alike:
Sri Rangaswamy Iyengar was a businessman from Madras

(Chennai). He was a staunch devotee of Bhagavan, and was
often coming from Madras to be with Bhagavan. One day
Rangaswamy Iyengar arrived at Bhagavan’s place by one o’clock
train, in the blazing sun. Bhagavan at that time (ie. during the
days of the great plague in Tiruvannamalai in 1906), was
staying in the Pachaiamman temple. Bhagavan welcomed him
with his usual beaming face of smiles and kindness. As it was
very hot, Iyengar was advised by the devotees to have his bath
in the nearby pond. He left Bhagavan’s presence to bathe in
the pond in front of the temple.

The spot was very lonely. Rangaswamy Iyengar was
bathing in the eastern ghat. All of a sudden, Bhagavan, who
was seated inside the temple, left his seat. Devotees sitting
there thought that Bhagavan was walking out to attend the
nature call. Bhagavan, when he came near the pond saw a
leopard coming to the tank to quench his thirst at the
northern edge.



Bhagavan and the Wild Cats 153

Bhagavan quietly told the animal, “Go now, and come
later; he would be afraid”, referring to the man (Iyengar)
bathing nearby. Hearing this, the leopard went away.

Bhagavan went to Rangaswamy, who had by then
finished his bath, and said to him, “We should not come here
at this part of the day. Wild animals come at these hours to
quench their thirst.” He did not add that the wild animal
had actually come there, lest the man be frightened.

Thus did Bhagavan show his equality in treating both
the brute and the man alike. A few days later, Bhagavan
himself told this incident to the devotees.1

3. RANGASWAMY AND THE TIGER
One day Bhagavan’s devotee Suri Nagamma asked

Bhagavan, “Is it a fact that when you were in Pachaiamman
temple, somebody got frightened and ran away from an
approaching tiger there?”

Bhagavan said:
Yes, yes! When I was there, Rangaswamy Iyengar

used to come on and off. One day, when he went to
answer the calls of nature it seems he saw a tiger in a
bush. When he tried to drive her away by shouting she
replied by a mild roar. Rangaswamy Iyengar’s body
shook with terror and getting up involuntarily from
where he sat, he began running towards me, gasping for
breath, and shouting at the top of his voice ‘Oh!
Bhagavan! Ramana! Ramana!’ I happened to come out
for some work so I met him. When I asked him what all
his fright was about, he said imploringly, ‘Ayyo, tiger,
tiger! Come Swami, we must go into the temple and



154 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

close all the doors, otherwise she will come in. Why don’t
you come?’ I said, laughingly, ‘Let us wait and see. Where
is the tiger? It is nowhere.’ Pointing towards the bush he
said, ‘There it is in the bush!’ I said ‘you wait here. I
will go and see’. When I went there and saw, there was
no tiger. Still, he could not shed his fear. I assured him
that she was a harmless animal and there was no need
to be afraid but he would not believe me.

Another day, while I was sitting on the edge of the
tank opposite the temple, that tiger came to drink water
and without any fear, roamed about for a while looking
at me, and went his way. Rangaswamy Iyengar however,
observed all this, hiding himself in the temple. He was
afraid what might happen to me. After the tiger left, I
went into the temple and relieved him of his fear saying,
‘Look! What a mild animal he is! If we threaten him, he
will attack us. Not otherwise. I thus dispelled his fear. We
were not in Pachaiamman temple for long after that,”
Bhagavan concluded.1

4. CHEETAH AND TIGER NEAR VIRUPAKSHA CAVE
Vasudeva Sastry used to look after the routine work at

Virupaksha cave. He was the one who embraced Bhagavan
and wept when Bhagavan’s heart stopped beating near the
Tortoise rock. It was the second death experience of
Bhagavan.

Many years later in 1947, Vasudeva Sastry came to Sri
Ramanasramam and sat down in the presence of Bhagavan.
Bhagavan then told the devotees that it was this Sastry who
started celebrating the Jayanthi.



Bhagavan and the Wild Cats 155

A devotee asked, “Is he the person who got
frightened, and hid himself when a tiger appeared near
Virupaksha cave?”

Bhagavan replied:
“Yes, it is him, Vasudeva Sastry. During our stay in

Virupaksha cave, we were all seated one night, in front of
the veranda when a tiger appeared in the Valley below.
We put a lantern outside the railings of the veranda as we
thought the tiger would not approach us because of the
light. Vasudeva Sastry however was very scared. He
therefore crept into the cave and asked us also to do
likewise; but we refused. After entering the cave, he bolted
the iron-barred door and from there tried to frighten the
tiger, like a great warrior, saying ‘Look! if you come this
way, take care. Take care of what I will do. Yes What do
you think! Bhagavan is here! Take care.’ All these heroics
were from inside the cave and were like those of
Uttarakumara of Mahabharata story. [The story is
Uttarakumara, son of the ruling king, started out with
Arjuna for war, boasting about his prowess but took to
his heel when he faced the enemy. Arjuna finally won
the battle for Uttarakumara]. The tiger loitered about
for a while and then went his own way. Sastry then
ventured to come out – a very brave man indeed”

Vasudeva Sastry took up the thread of the
conversation and said, “That was not the only occasion.
Another time, in broad day light Bhagavan and I were
seated on a rock outside the cave. In the valley below, a
tiger and a leopard were playing with each other and



156 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Bhagavan was smiling as he watched the friendly movement
of the two animals. I was however in a terrible fright and
requested Bhagavan to come into the cave. He was adamant
and sat there motionless. As for myself, I sought the shelter of
the cave. The two animals played for a while, looked at
Bhagavan, in the same way as the pets do, and without any
fear or expression of anger, went their own way, one going
up the hill and the other down. When I came out of the cave
and asked ‘Bhagavan, were you not afraid, when the two
animals played about so close to you?’ Bhagavan said with a
smile, ‘Why have fears? I knew as I saw them that, after a
while, one of them would go up the hill and the other down.
And they did. If we get frightened and say; Oh! A tiger! They
will also get frightened and say; oh man! And will rush to kill
us. If we do not have any fear, they too will not have any fear,
and will move about freely and peacefully’. In spite of all that
Bhagavan said my fear never left me.’”1

5. LEOPARDS NEAR VIRUPAKSHA CAVE
One day, when Bhagavan was living in Virupaksha cave,

the roar of a leopard was heard from the nearby drinking
water place. By the time the scared devotees had gathered
some plates and drums in order to make noise and drive
the leopard away, she had drunk the water she required
and gone away with one more roar. Bhagavan looked at
these frightened devotees and said to them in an
admonishing tone:

 “Why do you worry so much? The leopard
intimated to me by the first roar that she was coming here. After
drinking water, she told me by another roar that she



Bhagavan and the Wild Cats 157

was going. She went her own way. She never meddled
with your affairs. Why are you so scared? This mountain
is home of wild animals and we are their guests. That
being so is it right on your part to drive them away?”
Bhagavan added, “A number of  Siddha Purushas live
on this mountain. It is perhaps with a desire to see me
that they come and go, assuming various shapes. Hence
you see it is not right for you to disturb them.”

From that time onwards, whenever the leopard came
to drink the water and the roar was heard, Bhagavan used to
say, “There you are! The leopard is announcing her arrival.”
Then again when the second roar was heard he used to say,
“The leopard announces her departure.” In this manner he
used to be quite at ease with all the wild animals.1

6. CHEETAHS NEAR SKANDASRAMAM
One day, Bhagavan was reminiscing how, while he was

living in Skandasramam, cheetahs used to visit him. He
mentioned two instances to describe their characteristics:

“In one, the cheetah made a terrific roar which
clearly appeared aggressive, but she did nothing to us and
went chasing monkeys. On the other occasion, the cheetah
walked up slowly and majestically and on nearing the
Ashram gave a loud roar, but one in which only peace
and no aggression could be detected, just as if she wanted
to announce her arrival and no more. She came very near,
where the hill stream is now flowing in Skandasramam,
and after a time, slowly walked away, giving another roar,
as if to announce her leave-taking.” 1



158 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

7. LEOPARD AND VEDA PARAYANA
Bhagavan occasionally surprised devotees by saying

in all seriousness, that Jnanis’ and Siddhas occasionally took
the form of animals that come to visit him in the Ashram.

Annamalai Swami told about an incident in which
one of these strange animals appeared to some of
Bhagavan’s devotees:

On a moonlight night some devotees were going round
Arunachala (giripradakshina), chanting the Vedas. Suddenly
they saw a leopard standing in the middle of the road, looking
at them. The chanters were understandably paralysed with
fear. They could neither chant Veda nor walk ahead nor run
away. The leopard looked at them quietly for a long time
before slowly crossing the road and disappearing into the
jungle. The devotees thanked their stars, completed their
circuit of the Hill and returned to the Ashram. Bhagavan
listened carefully while they recounted the story of their
meeting with the leopard.

“Then Bhagavan surprised them by remarking,
‘There was no reason for fear. The leopard was a ‘Jnani’
who came down the hill to listen to your chanting of ‘Vedas’.
He went away deeply disappointed because, out of fright,
you broke off your chanting. Why are you afraid?’”1

8. CHANTING – BHAGAVAN’S NAME
South Indian leopards are called cheetahs were quite

common on the Arunachala hill in those days as there was a
thick forest on and around Arunachala. Once, Krishna
Bhikshu who is the author of Sri Ramana Leela, (a biography
of Bhagavan in Telugu) went around the hill with a visiting



Bhagavan and the Wild Cats 159

family from Ananthapur. On the way they met a truck and
the driver told them that he saw two cheetahs crossing the
road and he advised them to turn back. Krishna Bhikshu
and his friends discussed among themselves and decided to
continue the pradakshina chanting Bhagavan’s name as the
best protection against all danger.

After they returned to the Ashram, a college student,
one of the party, told them that being last in the file, he saw
two cheetahs, one on each side of the road. He was too
frightened to utter a word. It did not occur to him that the
cheetahs too enjoyed hearing the chanting of Bhagavan’s
name.

Many devotees have confirmed from their own
experience the protective power of Bhagavan’s name.1

9. THE ABODE OF CHEETAHS
One late night a devotee left the Ashram by the back

gate. At the very steps of the Ashram he saw a cheetah. He
started shouting loudly. When people came running, he
could not talk but only pointed towards the hill. All started
running hither and thither with lanterns and sticks.
Somebody started shouting into the darkness, “Get away!
Cheetah get away! This is Bhagavan’s place. You have no business
here. How dare you come to a holy place?” Bhagavan overheard
it and started grumbling, “Are we such extraordinary people
that cheetahs should obey our commands? Are we the rightful
masters of this place? Was not this jungle always the abode of
monkeys and cheetahs? Because we came and occupied this place,
they have migrated to some other place.”

Bhagavan was disgusted with the whole affair.1



160 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

10. THE TIGER CUB
If a new animal was brought to the Ashram and offered

to Bhagavan, he would normally decline it saying that there
was nobody in the Ashram to take care of it. But if an
ashramite came forward to take care of the animal, the animal
was allowed to stay in the Ashram, after due consideration.
Those animals which were accepted were the lucky ones as
they would be looked after very well by the Ashram people
and of course, Bhagavan had equal consideration for all
beings that came under his care.

Once a north Indian devotee brought a tiger cub to
the Ashram and offered him to Bhagavan. As the devotee
was to stay in the Ashram for a week, Bhagavan also kept
quiet and did not say anything about the cub. The tiger cub
was very aggressive and behaved ferociously with people. But
as soon as the cub saw Bhagavan, he would calm down and
behave like a lamb. He would go and sit on the lap of
Bhagavan to whom he was very obedient. A photograph of
the cub sitting on the lap of Bhagavan was taken by a devotee.

After a week the devotee who brought the tiger cub
was to return to his place. Bhagavan asked the owner to take
back the cub as he could not adjust himself to the ashramites
and to the life of the Ashram.1

To stay with Bhagavan one should have accumulated a
lot of Punyam (virtue). Is it not?

11. EXPERIENCE OF A HUNGARIAN WOMAN
Bhagavan used to go every day a little distance up the hill,

followed by his attendant. Once, a Hungarian lady devotee
accompanied Bhagavan, walking behind him. Suddenly a cheetah



Bhagavan and the Wild Cats 161

jumped from the bush and stood in front of Bhagavan who was
walking. The lady panicked seeing the cheetah. Bhagavan assured
her that the cheetah would not hurt anyone, as he had come to
see him. The cheetah and Bhagavan looked at each other for a
long time. When satisfied the cheetah jumped back into the bush
and went the way he had come. Seeing all this, the lady was
astonished at Bhagavan’s serenity and presence of mind. She felt
blessed to have such an experience by Bhagavan’s Grace1

12. BABY CHEETAHS
Once, a person brought two baby cheetahs to the Ashram.

Bhagavan fondled them and the ashramites gave them milk.
They moved about freely amongst the people in the hall and
also got on to Bhagavan’s sofa and slept soundly on it. One of
the Ashram devotees took a photo of that unusual group. From
about 1 pm. to 3 pm, Bhagavan confined himself to one end of
the sofa keeping the cubs on the sofa in the same position all the
time. They woke up afterwards, and were there till about 4 pm.
moving about freely in the hall. Once again, before Bhagavan
went up the Hill at the usual time, photos were taken with the
cheetah cubs on the sofa and also on the table in front of the
sofa. They were later published in the ‘Sunday Times’.

The wonder of it was that, even as the cheetah cubs
were sleeping happily on the sofa, soothed by the touch of
Bhagavan’s hands, the squirrels came and ate nuts and the
sparrows came and ate broken rice, as usual. In olden days,
when animals and birds of all sorts moved together without
enmity in any place, people used to think that it was perhaps
a Rishi Ashram. These are the stories related in the puranas.
But here the same thing was witnessed by the devotees with



162 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

their own eyes. When this was pointed out to Bhagavan, he
said, “Many similar things often happened here previously. But
who was there to record them at that time?”1

CHAPTER – TEN

THE ELEPHANT

BHAGAVAN WITH THE TEMPLE ELEPHANT
Some of Bhagavan’s devotees arranged to bring an

elephant belonging to Arunachaleswara temple to the
Ashram. He was kept in the open space next to the Jubilee
Hall. Bhagavan, on his return from the bath room, stood by
the side of the elephant, and gave him some food. At that
time a young American took some photographs of Bhagavan
with the elephant.

After some days, the photograph of Bhagavan with
the elephant was shown to Bhagavan. On the back of the
photo was written ‘A big self which does not know the
body and the big body which does not know the self are
at one place.’

A devotee asked Bhagavan, “What does that mean?
What exactly could be the photographer’s idea in writing
thus?”

Bhagavan replied: “That is easy. Though that elephant
has such a big body he does not know the Self. For that reason,
whatever food is given to him, he stands there dissatisfied
trumpeting unceasingly. Perhaps because of that or for some other
reason, the elephant is stated to be a big body without knowing
the Self. I stood there somehow with a shaky body; so again,
perhaps because of that or for any other reason, it is stated that



164 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

I am the Big Self not knowing the body; that might be the
photographers idea.”

A devotee said, “That is true, Bhagavan always appears
unconcerned about the body, does he not?” 1

Bhagavan’s seventy-first birthday was celebrated on
January 5, 1950. Compassionate Bhagavan even though in
frail health sat through for hours, morning and evening, to
give darshan to his devotees. When Bhagavan was in the
midst of his devotees, the temple elephant came and stood
before him for a while. After bowing to Bhagavan, the
elephant took leave of him by touching his feet with his trunk.
Rani, a devotee took a motion picture of this scene –
‘Bhagavan with the Elephant’. The atmosphere was full of
joy, festivities and devotion. This incident was reported by
Bhagavan’s devotee Viswanatha Swami.2

When the elephant from the Arunachaleswara temple
was brought to the Ashram, Bhagavan said “The elephant
and I stayed together in the thousand pillared mantapam. Out
of compassion for me, his friend, he has come here.” 3

Kunjuswami was serving Bhagavan as an attendant. He
found Bhagavan’s body and head were shaking and faltering.
One day when there was no one else but close devotees,
Kunjuswami asked, “Bhagavan, although you are only in
middle age, strangely enough, your head shakes and the body
needs the aid of the stick for walking. What could be the
reason for it?”

Bhagavan replied, with a smile, “What is there so strange
in it? If an elephant is tied in a small hut, what else will happen
to that hut except troubles of all sorts? This is the same.”4



The Elephant 165

What profound meaning was there in those words!
Rangan, Bhagavan’s classmate, childhood friend and

devotee later used to say that Bhagavan had the strength of
thousand elephants. How else could he make all his devotees’
minds quiet and keep them quiet for so long.5
