CHAPTER – ELEVEN

THE SHEEP

1. LOST SHEEP FOUND
Poovan, the shepherd, was Bhagavan’s devotee from the

days of Virupaksha cave. He sometimes supplied milk to the
visitors in those days.

Sometime in 1930, Poovan lost one of his sheep which
had gone grazing. The sheep was pregnant. He was searching
for her for three days. He had lost all hopes of recovering
her and thought that she might have been killed by some
wild animal.

Poovan was one day passing by the Ashram when
Bhagavan saw him and enquired how he was. He replied
that he was looking out for his lost sheep. Bhagavan kept
quiet for some time. After a while he asked the shepherd
to help him in lifting some stone. Poovan did it with great
pleasure. After the work was finished, Bhagavan told
Poovan, “Go this way” pointing the foot path towards
the town. “You will find the stray sheep on the way.” So
he did and found the lost sheep with her two little lambs.

Poovan would say, “What a Mahatma Bhagavan is! Look
at the force of his words! He is great! He never forgets even
a poor man like me. He remembers my son Manikkam also
with kindness. Such are the great ones! I am happy when I
do any little work for him.”1



The Sheep 167

2. RETRIEVED SHEEP
Mudaliar Swami, son of the old devotee Mudaliar Patti

who brought bhiksha every day to Bhagavan, had the
following interesting incident to tell:

“During the time Bhagavan was staying in
Virupaksha cave, Bhagavan and Mudaliar Swami were
once walking together behind the Skandasramam site.
There was a huge rock about 15 feet high. It was cleft; a
girl (a shepherdess) was standing there crying. Bhagavan
asked for the reason for her sorrow. She said ‘A sheep of
mine has slipped into this cleft, so I am crying.’ Bhagavan
immediately descended into the cleft, took the sheep on
his shoulders, climbed up to the surface and delivered
the sheep to her. Mudaliar Swami said that it was a very
remarkable feat for any human being!’”1

SOURCE
Content Reference

1. Lost Sheep Found 1 Talks with Ramana Maharshi by
Munaga Venkataramiah - Talk – 296.

2. Retrieved Sheep 1 Talks with Ramana Maharshi-
Talk – 361.



CHAPTER – TWELVE

THE FAVOURED ONES – THE PEACOCKS
If one enters Sri Ramanasramam, the abode of Bhagavan

Ramana Maharshi, which is at the base of ArunachalaHill,
makes one wonder whether they have entered an ancient
Rishi Ashram. Immediate attention is drawn to the number
of peacocks walking fearlessly among human devotees and
to the Monkeys who behave as if they owned the place. It is
not only the children but also the grownups that enjoy the
scene of peacocks dancing with their feather spread.

The peacocks were the favoured ones of Bhagavan. They
strutted as if they owned the place. They communicated
perfectly with Bhagavan. In the Jubilee Hall, the peacocks
were very happy because they could come and go as they
pleased. They had unhindered access to their beloved
Bhagavan all the time.

Bhagavan’s devotee Kanakammal who was witness to the
interaction between Bhagavan and the peacocks recollects:

“The way the peacocks craned their necks to gaze at
Bhagavan’s sweet face, the way they ‘asked’ him for snacks,
and the concern with which Bhagavan fed them the tasty
titbits like cashew nuts, ground nuts and dried fruits; all
these cannot be described in words. At times Bhagavan,
who never asked anything for himself although he owned
the whole creation, would send someone to the store room
with an urgent demand for different kinds of nuts, just



The Favoured Ones – The Peacocks 169

for his feathered friends! Mere words are inadequate to
express the sweetness of Bhagavan’s voice and the loving
concern in his compassionate eyes. Those who were
fortunate enough to witness Bhagavan’s interaction with
birds and animals can never forget these heart-warming
incidents.”

The peacocks were his companions, right from the days
of Virupaksha cave. A devotee had gifted a pair of peacocks
to Bhagavan. The peacocks knew very well Bhagavan’s daily
routine in the Ashram. When he went to the goshala one or
two peacocks would be waiting for him near the goshala.
Bhagavan too would stand near them for a few minutes. Both
would gaze at each other as if meaningful conversation was
taking place between them. Then the peacocks would leave
the place contented. Even when Bhagavan went up the Hill
for a little distance or he walked to Palakottu usually one or
two peacocks would walk with him. The friendship and
affection between Bhagavan and the peacocks was divine.

1. BHAGAVAN’S FIRST PAIR OF PEACOCKS
One day, Bhagavan looking at Madhava, the white

peacock, told Suri Nagamma. “Look, their ears are not visible.
In the usual place for ears there are three holes. They are covered
by feathers in the shape of fan.” Suri Nagamma said, “Is that so?
I never knew it before.”Bhagavan then remarked:

“I observed this while I was on the hill. We then had
two peacocks with us. The peahen always used to sleep on
my lap. I observed this when she was thus sleeping. The
peacock never came close to me. He used to wander about
in all sorts of places. The peacock would call the peahen to



170 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

accompany him wherever he went, but like a child, she
would never leave me and go. She would sit by my side or
sleep on my lap. The peahen was very attached to me.”

Devotee Suri Nagamma said, “Little girls are always
more attached to Bhagavan and Bhagavan also shows greater
affection towards them.”

Bhagavan who was reminded of those peacocks, told
Suri Nagamma about them. “One day a fellow took the peahen
away by force. She never returned. What happened I don’t know!
But the peacock never left me. However, he breathed his last on
my lap. He was buried and we built a tomb for him there.”

“How very fortunate the peacock was!” Suri Nagamma said.
“Yes, yes” said Bhagavan and was silent again.1

2. ATTACHMENT TO BHAGAVAN
Bhagavan, when he was in a reminiscent mood would

talk about life on the hill and his experience about the
peacocks. One day he talked about a particular peacock
which was with him in Skandasramam:

“When I was in Melasramam (Skandasramam), a
peacock would come to me daily from the town and stay
with me for prolonged periods. The owner used to come and
take him home. The peacock persisted in coming often and
staying with me. One day he refused to go back with the
owner. The owner, perhaps, lost his patience and suddenly
burst out rather humorously, ‘Swami! Give me money and
take this peacock. Of what use is it to me when it does not
stay with me but is always found in your presence?’

“I told him, ‘Why should I do so? Where am I to go
for the money? I am a penniless samiar’with a piece of



The Favoured Ones – The Peacocks 171

Kaupeenam. You can take your peacock away. I did not
call him here’.

“But the peacock continued to come as usual and
none succeeded in stopping him from coming to me.”1

3. THE BODYGUARD
The above peacock developed a unique relationship

with Bhagavan. He finally settled in Skandasramam as the
owner also lost hopes of the peacock returning to the town.
Bhagavan himself has recounted the story of this peacock:

“Even after I went up to the newly built
Skandasramam, Palani Swami had continued to stay in
the Virupaksha cave itself for its solitude. He was old and
had become weaker. It was not possible for him to go up
and down the hill frequently. Every evening I would visit
him just to make sure that he was quite well. Every time,
I set out for the Virupaksha cave, the peacock would come
all the way with me to the cave and wait for me for an
hour. At the end of one hour, however, the peacock would
become quite restless and peck at my feet, take a few steps
in the direction of Skandasramam. He would check to
see whether I was following. If I was still in the cave when
he looked back, he would return to the cave and peck my
feet again. He would not leave me alone until I started
back to the Skandasramam. One day I just tried to play
with the peacock by simply proceeding some distance
towards Skandasramam from the Virupaksha cave and
returning again slowly. Knowing this clearly, the peacock
also followed me hurriedly, came in front of me and
actually waylaid me, beckoning me to return without



172 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

proceeding further. He did not leave me till I reached
Skandasramam. He used to give some strokes on my head
at times, which would sound a ‘pottu-pottu’ if I did not
yield to his wishes.

Once I went to Virupaksha cave to see Palani
Swami. As usual the peacock followed me. But he was
afraid that I might decide to spend the night with Palani
Swami as he was ill. I was reluctant to leave him alone
and of course, Palani Swami would have preferred that,
but the peacock was determined to take me back to the
Skandasramam. The peacock refused to leave my side until
I was back in Skandasramam.

Palani Swami was very ill and was unable to move out.
Bhagavan would go daily and help him in whatever way he
could.

One day Bhagavan saw the peacock, which used to
follow him daily to Virupaksha cave, flying up from
Virupaksha cave to Skandasramam in great excitement and
it struck Bhagavan that Palani Swami might be in a critical
condition. At once Bhagavan went down to the cave and
found his intuition correct. Palani Swami was in the throes
of death, gasping for breath. Bhagavan sat with him till his
great devotee breathed his last.1

4. DANCING OF PEACOCKS AND SNAKE
When Bhagavan was in Skandasramam there were

peacocks. They would spread their feathers like a spangled
fan and dance. There was a cobra too which would also take
part in the pastime and raise its hood and moved about in
their midst. Actually the peacocks and the snakes are enemies,



The Favoured Ones – The Peacocks 173

they fight until one of them die. But in Bhagavan’s Ashram,
it was a beautiful sight for the devotees who were fortunate
to witness the group dancing performance in awe. This can
happen only in a Rishi’s Ashram.1

5. THE PEACOCK AND THE KOLAM
It was the Jayanti day of Bhagavan. The Ashram had been

beautifully decorated with attractive coloured kolams
(decorative patterns drawn with rice flour and coloured flour,
usually done in front of main doors, in temples and houses).
Kolam that was done in front of the guest house was particularly
beautiful. It was a drawing of a peacock. It was so realistic that
everyone remarked on its beauty. Bhagavan admired the kolam
on his way to the cowshed. On his way back, Bhagavan noticed
one of the Ashram peacocks standing near the guest room.
The peacock was gazing at the kolam with apparent fascination.
In fact, the bird was so engrossed in the kolam that he even
ignored the dry fruits that were offered by a devotee. Bhagavan
watched the peacock for a few minutes, and then said to him,
in tones of affectionate amuseme. “What is it dear? Are you
afraid that a new bird has come to take your place? No, no! That
is no rival!” Hearing this, the bird seemed to gain confidence.
He was reassured by Bhagavan’s tone of voice, and soon started
pecking the fruits and nuts that had been offered to it. Then,
he walked away with its usual proud confident air.

Bhagavan told the devotees:
“What a wonderfully gifted artist! If this kolam could

create so much confusion in a real peacock, how realistic
it must be! Only an extraordinarily subtle intellect could
have created such a realistic piece of art. For someone so



174 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

gifted, self-enquiry and realization would be very easy to
attain. But unfortunately, such people do not show much
interest in the Quest for Truth. Having drawn a peacock
that could arrest the attention of a live peacock, this artist
will feel inspired to draw a peacock that would induce a
live peacock to spread its fan and dance! The urge will be
always to attain perfection in the artistic ability and not
to seek the perfection within!”

Even after coming into the hall and seating himself
on the sofa, Bhagavan continued in the same vein. He
said,

“Only an artist can appreciate the skill of another
artist. Take the case of a musician. No amount of praise
from mere music lovers can please him so much as the
appreciation of a fellow musician. For such exceedingly
gifted artists ‘Brahma Vidya’ (highest spiritual
attainment) is very easy. But they will not seek spiritual
perfection. Their mind will go only after further fame!

These words of Bhagavan apply to all talented people.

6. UNLUCKY ONES
Once a man brought two peacocks with their eyes

covered. When let loose in Bhagavan’s presence they flew
away. They were brought back but again they flew away.
Bhagavan then said, “It is no use trying to keep them here.
They are not ripe in their mind as these dogs.” However much
the man and the ashramites tried to keep the two peacocks
in the Ashram, they would not remain there even a minute.1

Sadhu Arunachala (Major Chadwick) in his book
A Sadhu’s Reminiscences of Ramana Maharshi  writes:



The Favoured Ones – The Peacocks 175

“This is most interesting and shows that all were not
fit to remain in his presence, and even animals came to
Bhagavan with a purpose. Bhagavan also told us how, at
times, people would reincarnate in the body of animals
just for a chance to be near him. There is of course, the
famous example of Lakshmi, the cow.”2

7. THE WHITE PEACOCK
On the 12th April, 1947, someone brought a white peacock,

sent by the Rani of Baroda as a gift to the Ashram. On seeing
him, Bhagavan said, “Isn’t it enough that ten or twelve peacocks are
already in the Ashram? They may come and fight with this one as he
is of different variety. Besides he has to be protected against the attack
of cats. Why this? It is better to send it back to its own place.” But the
person who had brought the white peacock went away leaving
him in the Ashram. It was thereupon decided that Krishnaswami
would look after his welfare and others would help him. The white
peacock also got well adjusted to the ashramites and to the Ashram.
Bhagavan also took particular care of the white peacock. He
became very attached to Bhagavan.1

Arthur Osborne in his book Ramana Maharshi and the
Path of Self Knowledge writes:

“I was surprised once to see him tasting a mango
between meals, and then I saw the reason – the mango
season was just beginning and he wanted to see whether
it was ripe enough to give to the white peacock that had
been sent from the Maharani of Baroda and had become
his ward.”1

Sadhu Arunachala in his book of reminiscences has
noted that Bhagavan would nurse this peacock and pick the



176 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

lice from its body. He also writes that Bhagavan also taught
the white peacock not to eat caterpillars and other insects
but to be a strict vegetarian.2

One afternoon when Suri Nagamma went to the Ashram,
Bhagavan was telling the devotees about the white peacock.
As he was talking about him, the peacock which had run
away somewhere was brought back by Krishnaswami.
Bhagavan, placing his hand on the peacock’s neck and
stroking him up to the heart with the other hand, said, “You
naughty chap, where did you go? How can we manage to look
after you, if you run away like this? Please don’t! There will be
cruel animals. Why not stay on here?” Thus he cajoled him.

For a long time after that, the peacock did not go out of
the Ashram but learnt to go to the various cottages within
the Ashram compound. Seeing that, Bhagavan would say,
“He is now like the Sarvadhikari.”

One afternoon when Suri Nagamma went to the Ashram
the radio was playing and the fan was revolving. The peacock
was sitting by the side of the radio, with closed eyes as if
immersed in Dhyana. Seeing that, a devotee remarked “See
how absorbed the peacock is in listening to the music.”
Bhagavan said, “Yes, the peacocks are very fond of music,
especially if it is from flute.”

Once referring to this white peacock someone said,
“Though this peacock is white, the other peacocks, which
are coloured are more beautiful.”

Pointing to the white peacock, Bhagavan said:
 “The white peacock has a beauty of his own.

The other peacocks have many beautiful colours. This



The Favoured Ones – The Peacocks 177

is pure white without the mixture of any other colours.
That means it is shuddha satva (pure self ) without the
mixture of other gunas (attributes). Peacock can be taken
as an example to explain Vedanta. The other peacocks
also do not have so many colours at birth. They have only
one colour. As they grow up, they get many colours. When
their tail feathers grow, they have any number of eyes. See
how many colours and how many eyes! Our mind is also
like that. At birth, there are no perversities. Subsequently,
there will be many activities and ideas. Like the colours
of the peacock.” 3

8. UNIVERSAL BROTHERHOOD
In early days (prior to 1946), the evening Veda parayana,

the chanting of the scriptures, was being held in the old hall.
Due to lack of space, many people were forced to sit outside.
Then the Golden Jubilee Hall (a thatched veranda abutting
on to the old hall facing the hill) was constructed in 1946 for
Golden Jubilee Celebrations of 50 years of Bhagavan’s Advent
to Arunachala. The Veda parayana was then shifted to the
new Golden Jubilee Hall. Bhagavan used to sit on stone couch
at its western end. It was only after the construction of the
Golden Jubilee Hall, that the white peacock which the
Maharani of Baroda had sent came to the Ashram.

During the early days of the white peacock’s arrival,
Bhagavan watched over him with great care, as he was very
young. He slept at night in the old hall. Since, it was summer
time Bhagavan was sleeping out in the Jubilee Hall at night.
The morning Veda parayana was also held there. But at the
setting of winter, the attendants moved Bhagavan’s bed back



178 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

to the old hall at night. Consequently, Veda parayana was
also shifted to the old hall. The peacock at night rested on a
beam in the old hall. The ground below became very dirty
by the morning with his droppings. It caused much
inconvenience for the morning parayana. Some people were
even obliged to sit outside. This distressed the attendants,
and on expressing their disgust, Bhagavan said, “Why are
you distressed? Why not get a cage made and keep the white
peacock in it in the Jubilee Hall?” Taking the hint, a devotee
immediately had a cage made.

The cage was placed in the Jubilee Hall next to the stone
couch of Bhagavan. The day the peacock was moved out to
the Jubilee Hall, Bhagavan, who was sleeping in the old hall
until then, insisted that his bed also be shifted to the Jubilee
Hall. But, as it was cold at night, it was feared that sleeping
in the open hall would adversely affect Bhagavan’s health.
When the devotees expressed their fears, Bhagavan laughed
and said, “The white peacock came to us from somewhere. What
respect is it to the guest if we make him sleep outside while we
sleep inside? If a relative comes to our house, is it proper to make
him sleep on the veranda while you sleep in the room? If possible,
we should take him inside, or else, we too should sleep outside on
the veranda”. Turning to the attendants, Bhagavan said, “If
you are afraid of the cold outside, you can sleep inside.”

The attendants said, “Sleeping outside will be bad for
Bhagavan’s health. If necessary, one of us will sleep there to
keep the peacock company.”

“Enough, enough of this! ” said Bhagavan. “Will that not
affect your health? If you want you can sleep inside.” However



The Favoured Ones – The Peacocks 179

much they pleaded, Bhagavan was adamant and slept that
night out in the Jubilee Hall.

Next afternoon, as soon as Bhagavan went out for walk
at about 4.45pm, his attendant Krishnaswami removed
Bhagavan’s things from the Jubilee Hall back into the old
hall and arranged for the Veda parayana to be held there.
Seeing this, on return Bhagavan said, “This is what I do not
like, keeping the peacock a prisoner in its cage outside in the
Jubilee Hall, while we are all inside in the old hall. And also,
we shifted outside to the Jubilee Hall because we found this old
hall was small for Veda parayana.The peacock feels lonely there
and we have ample space here. From tomorrow arrangements
should be made accordingly. If you shift my seat to this place, I
will not sit here; so take care!” Bhagavan had his bed shifted
to the Jubilee hall and slept there keeping company with the
peacock.

The next day, Bhagavan went back to sit in the old hall.
While going out in the afternoon for his usual walk,
Bhagavan looked at the peacock’s cage and again said, “It is
alright, by the time I return, you should arrange for Veda
parayana in the Jubilee hall; otherwise I will spread my towel
and sit there alone. If you find it difficult to change my seat
from one hall to the other, I will stay all day long in this Jubilee
Hall only. It is just as you please. After all, what do I need? This
towel is enough for me.”

By the time Bhagavan returned from the walk, his seat
had been shifted to the Jubilee Hall. It was after this incident
that the old hall was extended and Bhagavan continued to
stay there all the time.1



180 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Treating all living beings that come to ones presence as
near and dear ones was possible for Bhagavan alone.

9. REMEMBRANCE – FORGETFULNESS
One afternoon, at 3 O’clock, the white peacock came to

the hall and started moving amidst the devotees and visitors. A
devotee, noticing how tame it was, remarked, “This bird
appears to have the knowledge of this place from its previous
births. Otherwise, would it have moved so freely in the midst
of all these people?” Bhagavan said, “That is why so many people
here say that he is Madhava (an old attendant of Bhagavan who
had recently passed away) who has come in this form.”

The devotee asked, “If so, will the peacock know that it
was so in its last birth?”

Bhagavan said: “How could he? No one knows about
his previous birth. People forget, and that forgetfulness is good.
In this life alone, we are sometimes terribly worried over what
had happened in the past; could we bear such worries if we
had known all about our previous births too? Knowing the
facts of previous births means knowing one’s own self. If that
is known, then this birth and the previous births will be seen
to be of the mind and its desires (sankalpa) only.”1

In the book Living by the words of Bhagavan,  Annamalai
Swami is quoted as saying, “Many devotees believed that the
white peacock who lived with Bhagavan in the later part of
1940s was a reincarnation of Madhava Swami, the attendant
who had served Bhagavan for many years. In this case
Bhagavan seemed a little more willing to admit that one of
his devotees had reincarnated in the animal body’ (Bhagavan



The Favoured Ones – The Peacocks 181

never confirmed or denied that Keeraipatti, who served
Bhagavan on the hill was born as cow Lakshmi).

Annamalai Swami has also been quoted to say that he
was present in the hall when someone asked Bhagavan, “How
is it that Madhava Swami came back as a white peacock?”

Bhagavan, neither confirming nor denying the
assumption in question, replied, “It happened in the same
way as the new bodies that are created in dream.”

It was the conclusion of many devotees that Madhava
Swami’s devotion to Bhagavan enabled him to take birth
again as Bhagavan’s white peacock. There were a few
circumstantial indications which convinced many people
of this.

The white peacock, whenever he came to the hall would
make it a point to inspect the book shelves. Madhava Swami’s
daily chores included looking after the library. He also
repaired and rebound any books that were damaged. When
the peacock came on his inspection tour, he would often
peck the books repaired and rebound by Madhava Swami,
but would not touch any other book.

Madhava Swami never liked women and would not talk
to them. The peacock retained the same trait by refusing to
have anything to do with any of the peahens, which lived in
the Ashram.

Whenever Madhava Swami came to Annamalai Swami’s
house, he used to sit on a particular place on a concrete bench
in his house. Even the white peacock was sitting at the same
place where Madhava Swami used to sit, whenever he went
to Annamalai Swami’s house.



182 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Madhava Swami loved Veda parayana and music and
would get absorbed in them while listening. The white
peacock too would get absorbed in music and Veda parayana.2

10. THE WHITE PEACOCK AND THE CROW
S.S. Cohen, one of the ardent devotees of Bhagavan,

was privileged to move closely with the Master. He has
recorded the following incident in his book. Guru Ramana:

“24 June 1948, 9.00 am. Maharshi is reading and
talking in a cheerful mood. The white peacock strolls in,
pecks a few of the grains placed before him by the
attendant and walks off confidently to a short distance. A
crow drops in and sets to pecking hastily at the grains.
Bhagavan draws our attention to the scene. The peacock
stands aghast at the scandalous encroachment of the crow.
Horrified, he spreads his feathers round his back, cranes
his neck and looks fiercely at the crow, as if ready to spring.
Now – he peacock steps forward with an extremely bellicose
gait. We all thought that a terrific battle was going to be
waged before our eyes. To our astonishment the crow
proves brazen faced, as he remains unmoved. He watches
the peacock mockingly with one eye and with the other
continues to peck greedily – obviously he knows his man.

“Still we are apprehensive of the fate of the crow.
But alas, the peacock, instead of advancing, suddenly falls
two hurried steps back and stops meditatively – planning,
we thought, a violent attack. We waited, but when at last
the assault did come, it was a single resolute step forward
followed by a dead halt. By now all the grains have been
peacefully reposed into the crow’s belly who then hops to



The Favoured Ones – The Peacocks 183

the water in the cement basin nearby, drinks his fill, wipes
his beak on the hard ground, bows in deep salute to the
proud peacock and flies away contentedly. The Maharshi
and all of us had a hearty laugh at the cowardice of the
peacock, who now cools down, lowers his feathers and
struts away with a feeble show of bravery. We were
thoroughly amused.

“Sri Bhagavan has all along been very fond of
watching the behaviour of animals. He has thus become
an expert in anticipating their reactions in given
circumstances and in knowing how to deal with them to
help them. His sympathy and consideration for them seem
to exceed even those for humans.”1

11. BHAGAVAN AND THE PEACOCKS
One day Bhagavan was in his usual reclining posture. It

was about 10 am. There was quite a large gathering that day.
Nobody spoke. There was a vibrant silence in the hall.

Two peacocks walked into the hall at that time. One
was white and the other was multicoloured. Catching
Bhagavan’s silent wish, the attendant put out a handful of
grains for each of them. The coloured peacock began to peck
away at his Prasadam. The white one, without touching his
share, stood watching the other feed with relish. Suddenly
the white one flew at the other and drove him away. The
one, that was driven away, looked up at Bhagavan. One could
easily feel the appeal and the silent query in the bird’s look.

Bhagavan smiled and spoke to the mischief maker, the
white peacock, as a loving mother might to a mischievous
little son, “Come here! You have your share. Why do you trouble



184 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

him?” Everyone in the hall was watching. The white peacock
took a few dignified steps towards Bhagavan. He gazed at
him with shining eyes. Bhagavan, extending his hands, said
softly, “Yes, eat your share, leave him alone. Go, go!”
Obediently, the white peacock retraced his steps to his
portion of the grains. It seemed as if a highly tensed spring
had been gently yet firmly released. Nobody spoke. Silence
had become more silent. Everyone, including the peacocks
understood.1

This demonstration of pure love and understanding
moved every one’s heart. This incident is noteworthy because
one saw the unity of the trainer and the trainee as the union
of the river with the ocean!

12. SVETA – MAYURA ASHTAKAM
Sri G V Subbaramayya, a staunch and long time devotee

of Bhagavan, was one of the few privileged devotees who
moved with Bhagavan with child like familiarity and had his
Grace in full measure. He has noted:

The white peacock presented by Rani of Baroda,
became a darling of Bhagavan and the ashramites. Though
very young, he was very active and intelligent. He would
visit daily the work of temple construction that was going on
in the Ashram. So Bhagavan designated him ‘the building –
supervisor’. Occasionally he would also enter the dining-hall
and walk between the rows of people seated, and so he earned
from Bhagavan the title of ‘Assistant Sarvadhikari’!

One noon when G.V. Subbaramayya entered the hall,
Bhagavan was saying that some people believe that the white
peacock was the reincarnation of Madhava Swami.



The Favoured Ones – The Peacocks 185

Henceforth, Bhagavan started addressing the white peacock
as ‘Madhava’.

On June 20, 1947, Sri G.V. Subbaramayya composed
eight Telugu verses on the white peacock in Mayura Vrittam
(peacock meter) and presented them to Bhagavan in the Jubilee
pandal. Bhagavan appeared to be greatly pleased with them.
He gave them to Smt. Lalitha Venkataraman, and suggested
that she might sing them with her veena. She composed the
music and within half an hour she brought her veena and got
ready to sing. At that time the white peacock was not there.
Bhagavan said, “But the hero should be there to hear his praises
sung! Where are you Madhava? Come.”

At once the white peacock jumped down from the roof
of the pandal. While Lalitha Venkataraman sang, Madhava
the white peacock spread his tail feathers like a fan and started
dancing. Bhagavan sat and watched his dancing with
beaming eyes. When the melodious singing was concluded,
the peacock walked to the veena, and pecked its strings with
his beak. There-upon Bhagavan told the singer, “Madhava
wants you to repeat the song.” So she sang once more and the
peacock danced again. It was a sight for the gods to see,
reminisced G.V. Subbaramayya.1 Smt. Kanakammal, who was
present there said, “To see the peacock dancing through the
music performance, was really a thrilling experience for all
of us, who were present there in the Jubilee Hall.” 2

G.V. Subbaramayya who composed Mayura Ashtakam
translated the verses as desired by Mrs. Talyarkhan as follows:

1. O White peacock, speak quick, art thou
Ayyaswami who, saying I will never leave you whatever



186 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

might happen to serve his own Guru, assumed this
shape on earth?

2. (Or) say O White peacock, art thou Madhavan
so sweet-natured who while enjoying the bliss of serving
the feet of Sri Ramana was snatched away by dull Fate,
and has returned thus to Sri Ramana on this earth?

3. Without any diffidence or hesitancy for being
new thou cheerfully exercisest supervision everywhere
in the Ashram. Thou also seekest to set books aright.
Say, O White peacock, how hast thou acquired this
devotion?

4. Seeing thy lustre, thy beauty, thy Graceful gait,
thy tremulous gestures, thy gleam and glitter. O White
peacock, our eyes thrill as though Heaven had
descended upon earth to dance.

5. Bhagavan incessantly gives thee His look
overflowing with Grace, love, and great compassion;
and He always talks of thee with exceeding zeal. Thou
art verily Goddess Lakshmi of Sri Ramanashram, O
peacock!

6. To run as fast to hear the veena-music and
dance, so finely and wonderfully, to get imprinted thus
in the pure mind of Sri Ramana of Arunachala, thou
mayst be the Goddess Saraswati incarnate, O peacock!

7. Perhaps the radiance of Satvaguna (purity and
light) has thus manifested in shape. Perhaps the Vedic
utterance That Thou art, Bhagavan’s exclusive
possession complete perfection art thou alone, O White
peacock!



The Favoured Ones – The Peacocks 187

8. Where is this Arunachala! Where is that
Baroda! Having been born there, to have thus joined
Maharshi here, art thou the peacock of God Skanda.
O thou of moon-like body? Hail to thee, O beloved
Son of Bhagavan.

The white peacock’s attachment to Bhagavan was
certainly remarkable. During the last hours of Bhagavan in
the body, the peacock sat on the roof of the Nirvana room
and rent the air with his plaintive cries. The bird’s mournful
cries were an expression of his anguish and sorrow, so full of
feeling as to melt the hardest of hearts. 3

The white peacock did not live long after the Nirvana
of Bhagavan. Sometime after, he breathed his last.

A devotee presented two white peahens and a white
peacock to the Ashram. The white peacock was driven away
by the coloured peacocks. Females were accepted and so were
their descendants. The white peacocks can be seen even now
in the Ashram.

The intimate relationship that began between Bhagavan
and his favoured peacocks has thus continued unhindered.



188 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

CHAPTER – THIRTEEN

THE WELL-WISHER OF BIRDS

Birds were as much attracted to Bhagavan as humans
or animals. Even in the early days when Bhagavan was in
Gurumurtham, the birds used to build their nests around
him and were not scared of him. He was keenly observant
and knew their needs. When birds complained in their
language, Bhagavan understood and his soft merciful heart
would melt and make him plead on their behalf whenever
he noticed or felt any kind of discrimination or harm done
to them.

Thus many birds were privileged to come and sit on his
lap because of their good deeds in previous lives. If the birds
were hurt or ill, Bhagavan would nurse them with care. Many
times he had acted as a foster mother also, when the young
ones were abandoned by the mother-bird or mothers were
killed by predators. These are some of the incidents of lucky
birds which came to Bhagavan, the well wisher of creation.

THE DOVES (PIGEONS)

1. A PAIR OF PIGEONS
One morning in September or October 1945, a devotee

from Bangalore, by name Venkataswami Naidu, brought a
pair of pigeons and gave them to the Ashram as an offering.
Seeing that, Bhagavan said,“We have to protect them from cats,
etc. is it not? Who will look after them? A cage is required; food



190 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

must be given. Who will do all these here? It is better if the one
who has brought these pigeons takes them back.”

The devotee Sri Naidu said that he would make all the
required arrangements and requested that they should be kept
in the Ashram. He placed the pair of pigeons in Bhagavan’s
lap. With overflowing affection and love, Bhagavan drew them
near him, saying. “Come dears Come! You won’t go back? You
wish to stay on here? All right; stay on; a cage will come.” As he
thus petted them with affection, they became absolutely quiet,
closed their eyes as if they were in samadhi and stayed on his
lap without moving this way or that. Bhagavan thereupon
stopped petting them, and with his gracious eyes fixed on them,
sat in silence, deeply immersed in Samadhi.

It took nearly an hour for the devotees in the Ashram to
find and bring a cage for them. The wonder of it was, all through
that one hour, the pigeons sat in Bhagavan’s lap without moving
one way or the other as if they were a pair of Yogis in Samadhi.
What could one say about their good fortune? Is it not the result
of their Punya in previous births that this great sage should seat
them on his lap, cajole them, by patting them from head down
to the feet with his hand, bless them and thereby bestow on
them divine bliss? Not only that, when the cage was brought in,
Bhagavan patted them cajolingly and put them in the cage,
saying, “Please go in. Be safe in the cage”. Then Bhagavan said,
“In Bhagavatham, pigeons also are said to be in the hierarchy of
Gurus in the chapter relating to Yadu Samvadam. I remember
having read that story long ago.”

While the pigeons were in his lap, a devotee came and
asked, “What is this?” Bhagavan said, without attachment



The Well -Wisher of Birds 191

but assuming responsibility, “Who knows? They come, and
decline to go back. They say they will stay here only. Another
family has come up for me, as if what I already have is not
enough.”

Suri Nagamma writes in her book:
“When I looked at those pigeons, it occurred to me

that they may be great saints who had fallen from their
austerity in meditation. Otherwise how could they get into
the lap of Bhagavan, a privilege which is impossible for
ordinary people? In canto 5 of Bhagavatham there is a
verse which says that people born in Bharatavarsha are
blessed, since Hari has come there as an avatar and blesses
living beings by His precepts, help and guidance. The
above incident is an illustration of this, is it not?

“It was the divinity which one practically experienced
with Bhagavan when he dealt with animals, birds or plants.
No ordinary human being can be so compassionate,
concerned, caring and loving and become one among
them when interacting with them. As Bhagavan said many
times, ‘Any living being that comes to me does so only to work
out the balance of karma. So don’t prevent anyone from
coming to me.’”1

2. THE INJURED PIGEON
This was told by Annamalai Swami:

“Bhagavan was always concerned if any of the animals
in the vicinity were injured or discomfited in any way.

“Once Bhagavan was walking on the hill followed
by Annamalai Swami, in the early morning, when a pigeon
fell in front of them. It had been attacked by a bigger



192 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

bird and its head was severely injured. Bhagavan asked
Annamalai Swami to pick the injured pigeon and bring it
back to the Ashram. When they got back to the hall,
Bhagavan put the injured pigeon on his lap and massaged
the injury with castor oil. From time to time he would
gently blow on the wound. The bird made no objection
to this because it was in a state of shock or was unconscious.
After Bhagavan’s treatment the pigeon made a speedy
recovery. The next day, Bhagavan and Annamalai Swami
took the bird to the hill and released it. It flew away without
exhibiting any signs of the serious injury it had recently
suffered.”1

3. THE WOUNDED DOVE
One day a devotee brought a wounded dove and

gave it to Bhagavan. He held it in the hand and asked
the devotees gathered in the hall “Who will take good
care of this bird, until it gets quite well?” No offer came.
Some time back the Maharani of Baroda had offered a
white peacock to the Ashram and everybody was eager
to take charge of it. Bhagavan looked around and started
talking to the bird, “What a pity you are not a peacock
sent by Maharani. You are a mere dove, a useless little thing.
Not a costly bird. Who wants you? Who will take care of
you? Don’t worry; I will take care of you.” The dove was
kept in a cage and was looked after. It was released from
the cage after it became well. The concern Bhagavan
had for the well being of all jeevas is evident from this
incident.1



The Well -Wisher of Birds 193

4. THE DOCTOR BHAGAVAN
Smt. Saroja Krishnan is a long time ardent devotee of

Bhagavan. She is the niece of Bhagavan’s attendant Rajagopala
Iyer. Her reminiscences:

“One day my uncle Rajagopala Iyer came home in a
great haste, hurriedly took a two anna piece (about 13
paise in modern coin) and ran back without saying
anything. People at home were intrigued by this behaviour
until he came home in the evening and explained the
matter. It was an illuminating incident.

“Bhagavan was coming down the hill after his usual
morning stroll. Suddenly a dove fell down at his feet. He
bent down and picked it up, cradled the dove in his arm
and gently soothed it by passing his arms lightly over its
back. Then he turned around to see how the bird
happened to fall down. A young hunter was hesitantly
standing a little distance away with a catapult in his hand.
Bhagavan remarked, ‘This is the poor boy’s food, but two
annas would do to satisfy his hunger.’ Uncle Rajagopala
Iyer and an attendant had accompanied Bhagavan and
were standing by his side. One word from Bhagavan, any
one of them would have run down to office and brought
the money in a minute. But did Bhagavan ever consider
the Ashram as his own? In his eagerness to carry out
Bhagavan’s remark and desire to pay it from his pocket,
uncle ran down to the office and without mentioning
anything, borrowed the two annas for himself, went up
and paid the hunter boy. He immediately returned the
money to the office, running to his home to fetch it. His



194 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

deepest desire was to carry out the wishes and words of
Bhagavan.

Bhagavan did not send word to the office for this
paltry sum. Perhaps, he to whom the whole creation
belonged wanted to show his devotees that he considered
that nothing belonged to him; that he owned nothing.

“The dove was lying still and dazed in Bhagavan’s
arms. It must have been seriously injured. It did not move
even after Bhagavan came back to the hall. ‘A few drops
of green grape juice, squeezed on its head, would cure this
dove.’ As Bhagavan was saying this, an out-station devotee
entered the hall with a few bunches of green-grapes, as
an offering to Bhagavan. ‘Hey look, we are just talking
about green grapes and here they are,’ exclaimed
Bhagavan. A few drops of grape juice were applied to
the dove’s head. In a short while the bird stirred, raised
its head and looked around. After a few tentative steps,
it fluttered its wings as if to show Bhagavan that it had
recovered and took to its wings.1

Where, when and how did Bhagavan learn this
treatment for birds and who was the veterinary doctor
who taught him?

Is he not a Sarvajna !

THE SPARROWS

1. PERFECTED BEINGS
There is a firm belief in Tiruvannamalai that there are

a number of perfected beings, called Siddhas with invisible
bodies living on Arunachala. There are several other



The Well -Wisher of Birds 195

reported instances of Bhagavan saying of one or more of these
beings taking an animal form and visiting him to have
his darshan.

One day two sparrows came and perched on top of the
double doors which were on the Southern side of the old
hall (now meditation hall). Each sparrow sat on its own door
and looked intently at Bhagavan for a whole day. Neither of
them showed any fear, when visiting devotees walked in and
out of the door way. Usually doors to the hall are closed at
night, but when the sparrows refused to leave, even after
dark, Bhagavan told the attendants to leave the door open.
They stayed throughout the night and departed early next
morning. After they flew away, Bhagavan told us that two
Siddha Purushas (perfected beings) had come in the form of
sparrows to have Bhagavan’s darshan.

2. VISWANATHA SWAMI’S ACCOUNT
Sri Viswanatha Swami, who was a permanent resident

of Sri Ramanasramam from 1922 till his last day, narrates an
incident which he witnessed for many days:

“A pair of sparrows, a male and female, used to visit
Bhagavan regularly. The sparrows would put in their
appearance every evening and perch on the doors. Bhagavan
would forbid people to close the doors of the hall even during
the nights; although it was winter, saying they would come
now and that they should not be shut out.”1

3. COMPLAINT AGAINST MADHAVA SWAMI
There were others, less exalted sparrows in the

neighbourhood. One of them tried to build a nest over



196 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Bhagavan’s sofa. It never got very far because Madhava
Swami kept destroying them with a long stick. After
several attempts, the sparrow flew to the top of the
entrance door, looked at Bhagavan, and repeatedly
chirped at him. To the people in the hall it sounded like
ordinary bird noise, but Bhagavan understood that she
was complaining.

He turned to Madhava Swami and asked, ‘Who has
destroyed her nest? She is complaining to me about it.”

“I did”, replied Madhava Swami, “If it builds its nest
on any other beam in the hall, there will be no problem. But
there will always be trouble if it constructs its nest over the
sofa. Grass will always be falling on Bhagavan’s head.”

Bhagavan agreed and arranged for two wooden boards
to be nailed to the beams in a different corner of the hall.
The sparrow was somehow persuaded to rebuild its nest on
these new boards. In the absence of any disturbances, the
sparrow laid some eggs and raised a family there.

One day one of the baby sparrows fell down from the
nest. Bhagavan picked up the young one and gave it milk by
dipping cotton and squeezing the drops of milk into its mouth
and asked one of his attendants to put the young one back
in the nest. Though touched by Bhagavan, the little one was
accepted by the mother as they thought Bhagavan was one
of them (usually if a sparrow is touched by a human, the
other sparrows boycott it and even kill it).

The sparrow stayed there for a couple of months. When
her children grew, became independent, and learnt to fly,
the family took off one day and never returned.1



The Well -Wisher of Birds 197

4. PROTECTION FROM COLD
It was a very cold winter season. One night there was

no electricity. Petromax lights were lighted. Then the devotees
noticed that two sparrows were sitting in front of petromax
light and warming themselves. Bhagavan’s attendant wanted
to close the windows of the hall to ward off cold draft which
was blowing. Bhagavan stopped the attendant by saying, “No,
don’t close the windows! These sparrows have come to protect
themselves from the cold. For them there are no blankets as we
have. Let them stay here. Whenever they want to fly out, they
can go out through the windows. Do not trouble them”. So
saying, Bhagavan showed his solicitude for the sparrows.
Bhagavan had Samabhavatvam for all creatures high or low.
He would say “The sparrows also have the same consciousness,
‘Atma’, like human beings. Only the forms are different”.1

5. VICTORY FOR SPARROWS
Sparrows would collect dry grass, tiny sticks, cotton,

thread, dried leaves etc. and build their nests on the beams
in the old hall. Bhagavan’s attendant, Madhava Swami, with
a long bamboo pole would poke the nest and remove them.
When sparrows came and saw, they were angry. They went
straight and chirped in front of Bhagavan, complaining about
their misfortune. Bhagavan called Madhava Swami and asked
him who had disturbed their nests. Madhava Swami replied
that as they were dirtying the hall with garbage, he had to
remove the nest.

Bhagavan challenged Madhava Swami saying, “Poor
creatures. They wanted to build a nest to lay eggs in it and
hatch them to raise family. It was a very natural thing for them.



198 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Let it be. They will again build their nest. Let us wait and see
who will win this war of grit between you and the sparrow.”

Those sparrows with grit and determination again and
again built their nest collecting dry grass, cotton, tiny sticks,
thread etc. Finally they succeeded, laid eggs and hatched
them. The young ones grew and became independent. They
all flew away winning the challenge.

So it was Madhava Swami who lost the battle.1

6. BHAGAVAN’S ADVICE
One day a sparrow refused to go out of the hall. She sat

adamantly and chirped her heart out until she had an
audience with Bhagavan. Bhagavan who had utmost concern
for all species listened to her complaint with patience and
compassion, and said, “What can I do? They have disturbed
your nest. They will not allow you to build the nest here in the
hall. So, please go and build your nest elsewhere.” After listening
to Bhagavan, the sparrow flew out merrily!1

Bhagavan’s identity with everyone, including animals
and birds, was so full and total that He was treated by every
species as their own!1

Sri K. Swaminathan writes in his book ‘Ramana
Maharshi’ (National Biography):

“There is a reason to believe that Maharshi understood
the language of birds and animals. Sri Anantanarayanan,
former Chief Justice of Madras, writes:

“Bhagavan, who was busy writing, puts away the
materials and his spectacles and calls the attendant. ‘ A
pair of sparrows just came here and complained to me that
their nest had been removed. I look up and find their nest



The Well -Wisher of Birds 199

missing …. Madhava, did anyone remove the sparrows’ nest?’
The attendant said that he did so, as their persistent

nest building above the couch of Bhagavan had become a
major nuisance. Bhagavan replies, ‘That’s it. That is why the
sparrows complained. The poor little ones…’

All things lie open to him who is in love and all animals
and birds speak to him, the knower, as to each other.2

7. CAN A CRACKED EGG BE HATCHED?
It was early hours of the morning. Bhagavan after

finishing his bath went to the farther end of the hall to take
his towel that hung from a horizontally suspended bamboo.
A sparrow had built a nest at one end of it and had laid therein
three or four eggs. While taking out his towel, Bhagavan’s
hand came against the nest, which shook violently and one
of the eggs dropped down and cracked. Bhagavan was taken
aback, aghast. He cried out to Madhava Swami, the personal
attendant “Look, look what I have done today!” So saying, he
took the cracked egg in his hand looked at it with his tender
eyes, and exclaimed, “Oh! the poor mother will be so sorrow
stricken, perhaps angry with me also, at my causing the
destruction of her expected little one! Can the cracked egg shell
be pieced together again? Let us try!” So saying, Bhagavan
took a piece of cloth, wetted it, wrapped it around the
broken egg and put it back in the mother’s nest. Every
three hours he would take out the cracked egg, remove
the cloth, place the egg on his roseate palm and gaze at it
with his tender eyes for minutes together. What was Bhagavan
really doing? How can we say? Was he sending with those



200 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

wonderful looks of gentle Grace, life-giving beams into the
cracked egg, putting over new warmth and life into it? That
is a mystery none can solve. Yet he kept on saying, “Let the
crack be healed! Cannot this be hatched even now? Let the little
one come from the broken egg! ”

This anxious concern and tenderness of Bhagavan
continued from day to day for about a week. The fortunate
egg lay in the nest with its wet bandage cloth only to be
fondled by Bhagavan with divine touch and benign look.
On the seventh day, he took out the egg, and with the
astonishment of a school boy announced, “Look what a
wonder! The crack has closed, and so the mother will be happy.
She will hatch her egg after all! My God has freed me from the
sin of causing a loss of life. Let us wait patiently for the blessed
young one to come out! ”

A few more days passed and one fine morning Bhagavan
found the egg had hatched. The wonder was that the bird
understood enough to sit on the egg and hatch it, even after
being handled by man.

A little bird had come out. With a smiling face radiant
with usual light, Bhagavan took the baby sparrow in his
soft hand, caressed it with his lips, stroked it with his soft
hand, and showed it to all bystanders to admire. He was so
happy that one little germ of life had been able to evolve in
spite of the unhappy accident to it in the embryo.

Ah, what a concern for the meanest (only from human’s
point of view!) of creation! Is it not the heart of a most
compassionate Mahatma who shed his first tears of anxiety
at the crack in the eggshell and then tears of joy at the



The Well -Wisher of Birds 201

birth of a new-born baby? Could there be any milk of
kindness so sweet as this?1

8.THE BIRD IN A CHIMNEY (The Cause of Bondage)

          Bhagavan used examples from life to teach the ultimate
truth. The cause of all misery and evil, beginning with birth
experienced by Jeevas, is forgetfulness of the way one came.
This is illustrated by Bhagavan by the following incident of a
bird caught in a chimney.

There was a chimney in the hall, where Bhagavan used
to give darshan. The chimney was closed on all the sides with
a steel mesh, except at the bottom. One day a beautiful small
bird somehow entered it from the bottom and was trapped
in a condition diametrically opposed to its natural
environment (the vast space where it could fly freely). From
the moment it entered the chimney, it was frantically
struggling to escape, but all its effort proved futile. Why?
Because, forgetting the way it came, it was repeatedly trying
to escape through all the closed routes. Bhagavan took this
example to reveal a great truth:

 “This bird has given up the all - pervasive space,
its natural place of residence. It has been caught in this
limited space which is opposed to its nature. Not
knowing how to escape from this prison, it is agitated
and afraid. Like this bird, ‘Jeevas’ have also given up
their natural place of residence, the vast space of
consciousness. Through the delusion they have become
trapped in the prison of the body without knowing how
to escape, they are tormented by various afflictions. The
ceaseless efforts of this bird to reach its natural place of



202 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

residence are unsuccessful because they are directed
upwards, the way of bondage, instead of downwards,
the way it came. Similarly, the reason why the Jeevas’
ceaseless efforts to attain freedom are unsuccessful is
because they are also directed outwards, the way of
bondage, instead of inwards, the way they came. The
natural tendency of the bird to go upward asserts itself
even in their attempts for freedom. Likewise the natural
tendency of Jeevas to roam outwards asserts itself even
in their attempts at liberation. This is Jeeva’s natural
tendency. If, through true discrimination and awareness,
the Jeeva is made to turn back from outward – directed
sight to inward sight, and if it remains fixed there, it is
certain to attain liberation in an instant.”1

This one upadesa will be sufficient for those genuinely
thirsting for liberation.

CROWS

1. VIGIL OF THE CROW
In the earliest days of present Sri Ramanasramam, when

mother’s Samadhi was being built, the ashramites when
digging at a spot to collect mud for building Samadhi, found
a fresh water spring with continuous flow. At that time
Bhagavan was still living in Skandasramam. Chinna Swami
took the sample of water to Bhagavan and asked whether it
could be used for the daily needs. That evening Bhagavan
came down and saw it and said it was a perennial spring
which would not dry even in summer. Therefore a pit of
three feet width, three feet length and three feet depth was



The Well -Wisher of Birds 203

dug. The devotees stacked dried tree-stump into the ground
to hold while lifting the water from the spring. Once they
found to their utter surprise that a crow, setting aside its usual
caution, was sitting on the top of this post for three days
without stirring and even without food. On the third day
Kunjuswami told Bhagavan, when he was returning to the
old hall from his usual walk, about the crow’s undisturbed
stay on the stump near the spring. Bhagavan immediately
arose from the couch and walked outside. Those in the hall
were surprised, as Bhagavan usually did not stir outside at
this time. Going close to the crow, Bhagavan enquired, “What
is the matter?” The crow slowly opened its eyes. Bhagavan
asked his attendant to bring his Kamandalu. Then holding
the crow in one hand, he poured a few nectar drops of water
into its beak. Immediately after this, the crow breathed its
last in Bhagavan’s hand.

Bhagavan arranged and supervised the samadhi and
when someone remarked the crow must have been a great
soul waiting for the touch of Bhagavan. He replied “Yes! It
appears so.”

The pit was enlarged later into a big well. As Bhagavan
had said the well has perennial water source. Even in the
very dry summer months, the well does not dry up. The water
is used only for temple purposes like Abhishekam, cooking
Naiveidyam etc. It is the same well that is found in between
Bhagavan’s Shrine and old dining hall.1

2. CROW AND THE PARROT
In addition to the animal shrines at Ramanasramam,

Bhagavan constructed two more Samadhis for animals while



204 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

he was still living on the hill. The first one was for the pet
peacock which lived at Skandasramam, which died on
Bhagavan’s lap. The second one is for a parrot.

One day, Echammal, one of the earliest devotees of
Bhagavan, was climbing the hill after giving Bhiksha to him.
She saw a parrot being attacked by a crow. At that time
Bhagavan was living on the hill in Skandasramam. She
brought the injured parrot to Bhagavan. Bhagavan and
Echammal tended it for four or five days. But finally one day
the parrot died when it was in Bhagavan’s hand. When the
parrot was buried on the hill, Bhagavan remarked, “A
building will come up here.” The prediction came true. A
building soon appeared there to be known as ‘Kili Guha’,
the cave of the parrot1.

3. REMINISCENCES OF SAMPOORNAMMA
During the early days, Bhagavan would go to the

kitchen early in the morning and help the cooks there.
Sampoornamma was a staunch devotee who worked in the
kitchen in those days. This was what she had to say
reminiscing about the days she spent with Bhagavan in the
kitchen:

“According to Bhagavan, high souls often visited the
Ashram in animal bodies. Once, for example, a white breasted
eagle, which is considered holy in India, flew into the old
Hall, and sat on the cupboard near Bhagavan. After a while
it flew around him and disappeared. ‘He is a Siddha (a
perfected being) who came to pay me visit’ said Bhagavan
seriously.”



The Well -Wisher of Birds 205

On another occasion Bhagavan remarked: ‘When I was
on the hill, a crow used to come near me. He used to give me
company. He was a Rishi in a crow’s body. He would not eat
from anybody’s hand but mine. He died soon after.’

The respect Bhagavan showed to the animals and the
birds was most striking. They were served food first like some
respected visitors. Who knows in how many different forms,
birds, animals, human beings and divine beings visited this
embodiment of the Almighty? We common and ignorant
women knew only the bliss of his presence and could not
tear ourselves away from the beloved of all.1

Bhagavan’s sympathy gained the confidence of crows
and other birds. They would come along with their young
ones and eat the food off the palm of Bhagavan and thrust
bits into the young ones mouths. There is an instance where
a particular crow used to leave its young one in the care of
Bhagavan and go hunting for its food. Bhagavan would take
the trouble of feeding them whenever they cried for food.2

There are innumerable such incidents but most of them
have not been recorded. The lucky ones are those birds who
enjoyed Bhagavan’s compassionate attention like any other
inmate or visitor.

Salutations to Bhagavan, the well-wisher of birds!

CHAPTER – FOURTEEN

 THE PROTECTOR

SNAKES – SCORPIONS
Snakes were Bhagavan’s companions since the days on

the hill. Bhagavan and snakes respected each other. They
were the fellow lodgers in the caves on the Hill. Bhagavan
used to say, “We have come to their residence and we have no
right to disturb or trouble them. They do not harm us.”

Bhagavan was completely at home with the snakes and
other reptiles. They also had absolutely no fear of Bhagavan.
It is said that Sadhus extend their protection to all beings.
This applied to Bhagavan who protected even poisonous
creatures. He was stung by deadly scorpions at least on three
occasions but nothing happened to him.

1. IT IS COOL AND SOFT
Once, a devotee wanted to know from Bhagavan

whether it was true that he was friendly with snakes while
living on the the Hill. Bhagavan replied, “Yes, it is true. One
particular snake crawled over my body. It was a very friendly
snake. At its touch my body used to feel as though it was tickled.
The snake used to come and go whenever it liked, on its own
accord.”

Dr. Sreenivas Rao, the Ashram doctor, asked Bhagavan
whether a snake had crept over his body. Bhagavan replied,
“Yes, snakes raise their hood and look into our eyes. They seem to



The Protector 209

understand that they need not be afraid of me and then pass over
me. It did not strike me either that I should do anything to it.”

When another devotee asked, how Bhagavan felt when snakes
crawled over his body, he replied laughingly, “Cool and soft.”

2. SNAKE AT SKANDASRAMAM
Once, a snake crept into Skandasramam. Bhagavan’s

mother was frightened when she saw the approaching cobra.
But Bhagavan began walking towards the snake very calmly.
As he did so, the snake began to withdraw and moved out. It
withdrew into a crevice in the rocks. Bhagavan followed the
snake to the crevice. The passage in the crevice ended against
a rock wall. Being unable to escape, the snake all of a sudden
turned towards Bhagavan and raised its hood and looked
steadily at Bhagavan who in turn returned its gaze. The game
of mutual hypnotizing went on for some time. The snake,
presumably grasping that Bhagavan was harmless uncoiled
and approached Bhagavan confidently. It almost touched his
feet, and later slithered away. The same snake would often
visit the Skandasramam for Bhagavan’s darshan even when
he was amidst visitors. The snake would occasionally try to
climb on to Bhagavan, but he would not encourage it. There
were two peacocks in the Ashram. When they danced, quite
surprisingly this snake also would join them in their dance.
The peacocks never hurt this snake.

There was another snake which would come to
Bhagavan, go round him in Pradakshina three times.
Bhagavan would say, “Go away. Visitors come here for darshan.
Seeing you they will be frightened. Please go away!,” and the
snake would slither away.1



210 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

3. OBEDIENT SNAKE
At Skandasramam a peacock would follow Bhagavan

everywhere. One day a huge black cobra appeared in the
Ashram and the peacock attacked it fiercely. The cobra spread
its hood and the two natural enemies were poised to fight to
death. When Bhagavan saw this, he understood the gravity
of the situation. Bhagavan went near the cobra and said softly,
“Why did you come here? That peacock will kill you. Better go
away at once.” The cobra immediately lowered its hood and
crawled away.1

4. DO NOT KILL THE SNAKE
One morning in December, 1945, Chinnaswami and

others saw a snake crawling near Bhagavan’s hall. People
started shouting outside, “What kind of a snake is it? Beat it!
Beat it!” When Bhagavan heard the sound of beating of the
snake he cried out, “Who is beating it?” Apparently this
remonstration of Bhagavan was lost in the noise of the crowd,
and the snake was killed. When Bhagavan came to know
this he said, “If these persons are beaten like that, then they
will know what it means to be beaten.”1

5. PROTECTOR OF SNAKES
Bhagavan was against killing of all animals including

snakes. One night a snake fell down from the roof of the
Ashram hall. Bhagavan asked the men to take a lantern to
light its path to the door and leave the snake safely outside.1

6. THE GREEN SNAKE
Bhagavan was very considerate towards wild animals.

But he was equally considerate towards beings like snakes



The Protector 211

which also inhabited the Ashram premises. Even now one
can see snakes moving about in the Ashram not harming
anybody.

Suri Nagamma records in her letter dated 23.5.1949,
the following incident about a green snake:

“The opening ceremony of the Pathala Linga
temple had been fixed. Several visitors were expected
at the Ashram including the Governor of Madras State
and his wife. A pandal was put up on the right side of
Bhagavan’s sofa in the Jubilee Hall to accommodate
them. On all the sides crotons were placed, khus-khus
thatties were tied and water was sprinkled regularly.
Hence the place remained comparatively cool. Some
four days after the pandal was erected I happened to
go there in the afternoon a little earlier than usual.
Bhagavan had just gone out and was back. There was
nobody near him. I prostrated before him and sat down
under the pandal. A big snake came through the
opening between the crotons on the side of Bhagavan’s
sofa, glided along some distance, got up on to the roof
of the pandal and settled down comfortably there. I
was not frightened in any way and kept quiet looking
at the snake and at Bhagavan. He noticed my feelings
and said with a smile, ‘He has come here because it is
cool.’ I said, ‘Since how long could he have been here?’
Bhagavan replied, ‘He came here about the same time
as I returned here after the midday meal. He has been
going around the pandal and also the crotons. He has
been coming like this for last three days and going away



212 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

around 2.30 pm.’ I said, ‘He must be a great soul. He
must have come here in this form to be with Bhagavan
when he is alone.’

“As I was saying this Krishnaswami came in.
“Krishnaswami: ‘I do not know what to do. He is

coming here everyday Bhagavan says we should not chase
him away.’

“Bhagavan: ‘What if he comes? What harm has he done
to us?’

“Krishnaswami: ‘He has not done anything to us. But
several people come to this place. Is it not risky?’

“Krishnaswami: ‘It is alright now. But during festival
days people come in at all the hours.’

“Bhagavan: ‘Oho! That is your fear! ’
“So saying Bhagavan looked at me and the snake. I too

began looking at the snake and Bhagavan and said, ‘He must
have come here to be with Bhagavan. But if he comes in this
form, there is likely to be some trouble from the people in
general to him and from him to the general public.’

   “Bhagavan: ‘It might be so.’
   “Bhagavan thereupon looked at the snake for a

while, steadfastly and graciously. Immediately after
that, the snake, which was remaining still all the time
they were discussing, got down the pandal rapidly,
went into the flower garden and disappeared. There
was no knowing what message he received when
Bhagavan gazed at him. The clock struck the half-
hour. It was 2.30 pm.



The Protector 213

“Devotees started coming in rows and prostrated
before Bhagavan. Bhagavan’s look thus got diverted and
he came back to his normal state. The snake was never
seen afterwards.”1

There were so many incidents like this to show Bhagavan’s
abode is a place of safe resort at all times, not only for the
women and the poor but also for the dumb animals.

7. THE SNAKE AND THE MEDITATOR
This incident was narrated by Swami Madhavathirtha.

He was a Gujarati devotee of Bhagavan and came to Sri
Ramanasramam in 1944 and stayed there for some time.
He has written a Biography of Bhagavan in Gujarati.

Swami Madhavathirtha writes in his reminiscences:
“There was an incident which I witnessed in

Maharshi’s hall. There was a devotee who had had a desire
to go and meditate in a cave on the holy hill of Arunachala.
Bhagavan gave him permission to go. The devotee went
to the cave, but many snakes were there. A little later,
while the devotee was sitting in meditation, a snake came
and hit its hood on his thigh. The devotee opened his
eyes and discovered that the snake had not bitten him.
The snake went away and the devotee started meditating
again. Later, the snake came back and repeated its strange
action.

“The devotee thought to himself, ‘If this keeps on
happening, it will not be possible to stay in this cave and
meditate.’

“He went to see Bhagavan who immediately asked
him what had happened.



214 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

“After the devotee had narrated the whole incident,
Bhagavan commented, ‘The snake came to you to say, ‘You
must stay there only’. If you could have caught the snake by
the mouth he would have said, ‘Stay here only’. God came to
test you in the form of a snake.’

“After listening to this answer the devotee went
back to the cave to meditate.”1

SNAKES AND SCORPIONS PROTECTED

Sometimes a snake would drop from the roof of the
hall at night. Bhagavan would ask the attendant to show the
snake out to safety by walking behind with a lamp. Thus the
protector of snakes and the protected snakes stayed together
without fear or hurting anybody.

8. SCORPIONS
The scorpions stayed with Bhagavan without fear and

Sri Vasudeva Shastri has recounted an incident, which he
had witnessed:

“One day, when we were in Skandasramam, I was
aghast to find a scorpion climbing up over Bhagavan’s
body in the front. Another, at the same time was climbing
down from his back. But Bhagavan remained calm, as if
nothing was happening. The two scorpions, after crawling
over his body as if over a wall, eventually left him. After
they left, Bhagavan explained:

“‘They crawl over you just as they would crawl on
the floor , a wall or a tree. Do they crawl over these stinging
as they go? It is only because you fear them and do
something that they fear you and do something in return.’”1



The Protector 215

Another time while Bhagavan was walking, a scorpion
stung him. He said, “It is not the mistake of the scorpion, but
mine. I did not see it and stepped on it. According to its nature,
it stung me to show its presence”.2

Bhagavan’s attendant Madhava Swami was adept in
picking up scorpions by the tail so that they could not sting,
and carry them outside where they could neither do harm
nor be harmed by humans.3

9. FROG
Bhagavan used to say:

 “A Frog is often compared to a yogi. It remains quiet
for a long time. The only sign of life is being the rhythmic
movement of the under-skin below the neck. Giant frogs
can remain for extraordinarily long periods with their
animation suspended. They are said to swallow their
tongue. Swallowing the tongue is a yogic practice. The
animation is suspended. The yogi does not die because the
tongue is drawn out by someone else before life-activity is
resumed. It is a wonder how the frog brings out the already
swallowed tongue and resumes activity.” 1

While in Skandasramam, Sri Bhagavan saw a white toad,
small and long at a distance about 10 feet from him.
Bhagavan stared at it and it stared back at him. Suddenly, it
took a long jump and lodged itself precisely on the right eye
of Bhagavan who quickly closed it. The ashramites gasped,
fearing some harm to his right eye.

Attendant asked if Bhagavan was alright. Bhagavan did
not answer, though his broad smile and slight chuckle said
something like, “No need to worry. He’s just saying ‘Hello’. ”2



216 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation
