CHAPTER – FIFTEEN

BHAGAVAN – COMPASSIONATE
FRIEND OF SQUIRRELS

Squirrels were Bhagavan’s friends from the days of
Gurumurtham. They were not scared of him. They built
their nest near him and would run over his body. Even when
Bhagavan came to Virupaksha cave and Skandasramam, the
squirrels continued to be his companions.

After Bhagavan came down the hill and started to live
near his mother’s Samadhi, he was literally surrounded by
squirrels. They would run all over his couch, on his body
and even under his pillows. Bhagavan had to be extremely
careful before he sat or leaned, lest some squirrel babies be
crushed by the weight of his body.

Squirrels used to hop through the window on to his
couch and he would always keep a tin of peanuts beside him.
Sometimes he would hand a visiting squirrel the tin and let it
help itself. Sometimes he would hold out a nut and the little
creature would take it from his hand.

Bhagavan used to feed the squirrels in the hall at about
1.00 pm every day. Ten to fifteen squirrels that lived in and
around the hall would always show up around this time
and wait for Bhagavan to feed them. They would also show
up at other times of the day especially if they heard the
sound of Bhagavan opening the tin of nuts which he always
kept next to his sofa. The squirrels showed no fear or



218 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

nervousness when they were with Bhagavan. Even though
they were undomesticated rodents they would happily run
over his legs, arms and head while they were waiting for
their food to be given.

1. PROTECTION
One early morning in April 1942, when Bhagavan was

returning from his walk on the hill after breakfast, he had a
nasty accident. One of his favourite squirrels ran across his path
as he was descending the stone steps near the Ashram dispensary.
This squirrel was chased by the Ashram dog that was in full
pursuit. Bhagavan immediately pursued forward. He put his
stick in front of the dog to stop it and to protect the squirrel.
The squirrel escaped and ran away as the dog was distracted.
But Bhagavan lost his balance, slipped and fell down the steps
which caused a lot of pain. He had broken his collar bone.

Bhagavan did not care for his own safety while
protecting his devotee. It may be just a squirrel, but for him,
the primary concern was to protect his friend, the squirrel.
His own safety was secondary.1

2. PUNISHMENT FOR THE WRONG DEED
Once, a mischievous and overactive squirrel bit Bhagavan’s

finger. This particular squirrel would not take his nuts from
the tin but would insist on Bhagavan feeding him.

One day it so happened that when he came for food,
Bhagavan was reading or otherwise occupied. So there was a
slight delay in giving him food. Perhaps because of his anger
at the delay he abruptly bit Bhagavan’s finger, but Bhagavan
still did not offer him food. Bhagavan was amused and said,



Bhagavan – The Compassionate Friend of Squirrels 219

“You are a naughty creature! You have bitten my finger! I will
no longer feed you. Go away!” Saying so, he stopped feeding
the squirrel for a few days. That was how Bhagavan punished
the squirrel for his impatience and naughtiness.

A regular tussle then followed between Bhagavan and
the squirrel. The squirrel did not stay quiet. He began begging
Bhagavan for forgiveness by crawling hither and thither.
Bhagavan put the nuts on the window sill and on sofa and
told him to help himself. But he would not even touch them.
Bhagavan pretended to be indifferent and not to notice. But
the squirrel would crawl up to Bhagavan’s leg, jump on his
body, climb on the shoulders and do ever so many things, to
attract his attention. Bhagavan turned to his devotees and
said, “Look! This fellow is begging me to forgive him for his
mischief in biting my finger and to give up my refusal to feed
him with my own hands.”

Bhagavan pushed the squirrel away for some days saying,
“Naughty creature! Why did you bite my finger? I won’t feed
you now. That is your punishment. Look, the nuts are there.
Eat them all”. But, the squirrel would not leave its obstinacy
either. Some days passed and Bhagavan who is always kind
and merciful to his devotees, had to admit his defeat, and he
fed the squirrel with his own hands.

Suri Nagamma said, “It occurred to me that it was
through perseverance that devotees attained salvation.” 1

3. ANGUISH
That naughty and mischievous squirrel, which bit

Bhagavan’s finger, did not stop at that. He gathered together
a number of his gang and began building nest in the roof



220 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

exactly above the couch on which Bhagavan sat. They began
to squeeze into the beam bits of string, coconut fibre, and
the like. Those things were falling down whenever there
was wind. So people got angry and began to drive them
away.

Seeing this Bhagavan was anguished and felt very sad at the
thought that while there was not enough room for the squirrels to
build a nest the people in the hall were driving them away.

Bhagavan’s face on such occasions would show so much
concern for such beings; it clearly showed that he had equal
solicitude for all beings.

4. SAMADHI OF BABY SQUIRRELS
Once when Nagamma was talking to Bhagavan about

squirrels, Bhagavan remarked about the baby squirrels. He
narrated:

“There is a big story about the squirrels. Sometime
back they used to have a nest near and above me. They
had children, grand children and thus members of their
family grew very large.

“The babies and the adults used to play about on
this sofa in whatever way they liked. When I went out
for my usual walk, some little squirrels used to hide under
my pillows and when on my return, I reclined on my
pillow, they used to get crushed. We could not bear the
sight of this, and so Madhava drove the squirrels out of
the nest and sealed it by nailing some wooden board
over it. There are lots of incidents about them if one
cared to write about them.

“As Bhagavan was narrating this, his voice choked.”1



Bhagavan – The Compassionate Friend of Squirrels 221

5. WE WANT CASHEW NUTS
One day a few squirrels came on to Bhagavan’s sofa for

their share of cashew nuts. The nuts in the tin, which used to
be near Bhagavan, had been exhausted. Therefore, Bhagavan
instead gave peanuts, but the squirrels would not eat them.
They refused to eat and began to express their discontent in
all possible ways. Bhagavan cajoled them. “We don’t have
cashew nuts my dears. What to do?” he said, but they could
not be appeased. The squirrels showed their displeasure by
crawling over the legs and hands of Bhagavan continuously.

Seeing their plight, Bhagavan asked his attendant
Krishnaswami to go and find out if there was any stock of
cashew nuts in the store room. Krishnaswami went to the
kitchen and returned with a few cashew nuts. “Is that all?”
asked Bhagavan looking at the cashew nuts.

Krishnaswami said, “Tonight, cooks are preparing
payasam and they said that they can spare only this much.”

Bhagavan was annoyed and said, “I see, payasam will not
be less tasty if the cashew nuts are a little less in quantity than
usual. What a pity! These squirrels do not like anything else, and
they are worrying me. The store keepers have declined to give
cashew nuts saying that they will have to put them in the payasam.
Who will be worried if there are no cashew nuts in the payasam?
See how these children are worrying themselves for want of cashew
nuts!” With that, the cashew nuts which should have gone to
payasam went into the stomachs of the squirrels and also into
the tin by the side for future feeding of the squirrels.

The same evening Dr. Anantanarayana Rao, a devotee of
Bhagavan brought from Madras 2 viss (about 2.7 kg) of cashew



222 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

nuts, saying that he had brought them for the squirrels. With a
smile, Bhagavan said to Krishnaswami, the attendant, “Look at
this! They are earning whatever they want. There is no need to beg
anybody. These cashew nuts are squirrel’s property. Keep them
carefully, Note that they should not be given to the store room”.1

6. MOTHER TO THE SQUIRREL BABIES
The following incident highlights the care and concern

Bhagavan showed to baby squirrels, whose mother had
deserted them:

In the roof of the old hall, squirrels had built nests. Once,
some new-born squirrels dropped on Bhagavan’s sofa from
their nest. Their eyes had not yet opened and the size of each
baby might not have been more than an inch. They were
very red in colour with fresh flesh, absolutely tender to touch.
The mother squirrel ignored them. Now what to do? How
to feed and attend to such tender ones?

The baby squirrels were on the palm of Bhagavan.
Bhagavan’s face glowed with love and affection for them. While
there was a question mark in the face of those who surrounded
Bhagavan, he himself was happy and cheerful. He asked for
some cotton to be brought. He made a soft bed for them. He
also took a bit of cotton and squeezed it to a tiny end. The end
portion looked like a sharp pin; he dipped it in milk and
squeezed milk into the tiny mouths. At regular intervals,
Bhagavan repeated this act of compassion. He tended them with
great care and loves till they grew up and ran around. They did
not run away, but only ran around their ‘mother’, far kinder
than the one who gave them birth!  How lucky squirrels were!1



Bhagavan – The Compassionate Friend of Squirrels 223

7. TURNING INWARDS
There was a nest near Bhagavan’s couch. The young

squirrels became orphans as their mother was eaten by a cat.
Therefore, Bhagavan, who had equal concern for all living
beings without any discrimination, took care of the young
ones.

“The little ones do not know that the wisdom lies in remaining
inside the nest, because all troubles are outside. But they cannot
remain inside, i.e. within” said Bhagavan and using this example
Bhagavan taught his devotees how to turn the mind inwards
and how beneficial it is for spiritual Sadhana. He told, “If the
mind is not externalized but remains sunk in the Heart, then there
would only be happiness, but mind keeps moving out.”

Rangaswamy was an attendant to Bhagavan for a
number of years and served Bhagavan with diligent devotion.
Suri Nagamma referred to him as ‘Nandi!’ He was to
Bhagavan what Nandi was to lord Shiva!

Once he asked Bhagavan, “What is the path for keeping
the mind inwards?”

Bhagavan replied, “It is exactly the same as what I am
doing now. Each time a young squirrel comes out, I keep putting
it back into the nest. When I go on doing it, the young squirrel
learns the happiness of staying in the nest.”1

8. ARE THE SQUIRRELS DUMB?
One day a squirrel came to Bhagavan and he was feeding

it with cashew nuts as usual. Bhagavan turned to the devotee
Devaraja Mudaliar, the author of Day by Day with Bhagavan
and said, “Shroff sent some cashew nuts yesterday and said that
they are intended for my dumb friends.”



224 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Devaraja Mudaliar said, “Probably Bhagavan would
object to our calling these squirrels dumb.”

Bhagavan said, “They communicate with me.
Sometimes, if I am having a nap they draw my attention
to them by gently biting my finger tips, they have their
language of their own. There is one great thing about
squirrels. You may place any amount of food before them,
they will just eat what they need and leave the rest behind.
Not so the rat, for instance. It will take everything it finds
and stock it in its hole.”

Devaraja Mudaliar remarked, “Possibly it would be said
that the squirrel is less intelligent than the rat, because it does
not plan or provide for the future but lives in the present”.

Bhagavan said, “Yes, yes, we consider it intelligent
to plan and live wretchedly like this. See how many
animals and birds live in this world without planning
and stocking. Are they all dying?” Bhagavan then said
about monkeys, “They too don’t build nest or stock things.
They eat what they find, go and perch on trees when night
falls. They are quite happy.”1

9. THE FEEDING OF SQUIRRELS
It was 24 June 1948, at about 10.30 am, Bhagavan

had closed his eyes. A female squirrel leapt on his couch and
bit his thumb tenderly probably to draw his attention; he
quickly pulled back his thumb and stroked it, remarking,
“I’ll not feed her!” Other squirrels crowded on his couch and
for half an hour he continued to feed them with cashew nuts,
one nut at a time to each. Then he turned to his devotees
and pointing to one of them said, “This she-squirrel has been



Bhagavan – The Compassionate Friend of Squirrels 225

trying to fool me, thinking I don’t recognise her and so shall feed
her. Once she comes from this side, once from the other and once
from under the couch and once from above it. But I recognise
her very well. She shall not have anything” and laughed.

As Bhagavan was feeding the squirrels, a squirrel jumped
from the window to the couch. Bhagavan looked at it intently.
He gave it a nut, then another and addressed it, “Now go.
Have you come to bite me again?”

Mr. Cohen, who was sitting in front of Bhagavan
immediately, guessed that, it was the same squirrel which had
bitten Bhagavan, four days ago. He wondered how Bhagavan
recognised and relented. Nevertheless, Cohen asked Bhagavan
if his guess was correct and Bhagavan confirmed it.

After a while, the same squirrel came back for more nuts.
But to this one, Bhagavan refused to give again. Usually Bhagavan
would feed the animals till of their own accord they ceased to
eat. But when he saw the naughty squirrel’s persistence he lifted
his fan in threat, which made the naughty squirrel disappear at
once. Then Bhagavan sat with a pensive look and a faint smile
on his face. After a while, he turned in Cohen’s direction,
broadened his smile and softly spoke, “Even animals understand
a rebuke and, if it is repeated sufficient number of times, they learn
to behave. Some animals are more sensible than some others…..”

Mr. Cohen who was sitting there wondered how
Bhagavan could recognise each one of the squirrels which
looked alike for most of the people!1

10. THE LUCKY ONES
This was an incident witnessed by Chhaganlal V. Yogi

in Bhagavan’s Hall:



226 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

“For most of the day Bhagavan used to sit on his sofa, which
was adjacent to a window. Squirrels would occasionally come in
through the window and run around near him. Bhagavan would
respond to them by feeding them cashew and other foodstuffs
with his own hands, lovingly.

“One day when Bhagavan was feeding the squirrels, a
Muslim devotee, who had been watching him, gave him a note
in which was written, ‘The squirrels are very fortunate because
they are getting the food from your own hands. Your Grace is so
much on them. We feel jealous of the squirrels and feel that we
also should have been born as squirrels. Then it would have been
very good for us.’

“Bhagavan could not help laughing when he read this
note.

“He told the man, ‘How do you know that the Grace is
not there on you also.’

“And then, to illustrate his point, Bhagavan told a story:
“‘ thief went to a saint to get his blessings so that

his attempt in the robbery that night would be successful.
But that night the thief was unsuccessful in robbery.
Because of his failure, the thief was very angry with the
saint for giving him false blessings. The Saint said, ‘To
be unsuccessful in bad work means that the blessings
have indeed borne fruit. There are so many honest
ways of feeding the stomach; you should accept any
one of them. To come to this conclusion it was
necessary that you be unsuccessful in thieving job’.
The thief understood and became an honest and
good man.’



Bhagavan – The Compassionate Friend of Squirrels 227

“Having narrated the story, Bhagavan asked the
devotee, ‘Do you mean to say that only if everything goes
according to your desire it is possible to say that the Grace
of a saint has worked?’

“‘I don’t understand’, replied the devotee.
“Bhagavan explained in more detail, ‘The blessings of a

saint perform the purificatory work of life. These blessings cannot
increase impurity. One whose understanding is limited, will ask
for blessings so that he can fulfil certain desires, but if the desires
are such that their fulfilment will make the seeker more impure
rather than purer, the saint’s blessing will not enable him to fulfil
the desires. In this way the seeker is saved from further impurities.
In that case, are not a saint’s blessings a gift of compassion?’

“The devotee finally understood and was satisfied
by Bhagavan’s words.”1

11. REMINISCENCES
1) Mr. N. Ponniah, who lived in Malaysia, visited

Bhagavan in 1948. He has written his reminiscences in the
book Face to Face with Ramana Maharshi:

“In Bhagavan’s hall, the darshan was not the monopoly
of humans. At certain hours squirrels from a large tree by the
side of the hall came down to claim their fair share. The
beautiful peacock followed. Bhagavan looked at them most
graciously. ‘Oh, you are hungry!’ he would say, and give them
some nuts and grains. They would then move away happily
like a child after being fed by its mother!”1

2) Mr. K Subramanian, founder of Hyderabad Ramana
Kendra reminisces that when he was in college he used to write
letters to Bhagavan and used to go and have Bhagavan’s darshan:



228 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

“Sometimes a squirrel would scramble up the couch.
Bhagavan would fondle it and give it whatever is available
and the squirrels would leave without disturbing anybody.
Similarly, a peacock would come and get some puffed rice
from Bhagavan’s hands.”2

3) This incident is narrated by Narayana Iyer:
“It was 8.30 one night when I came to the Ashram.

When Chinna Swamy saw me enter, he said, ‘ Narayana
Iyer, don’t go near Bhagavan’s couch. He had a fracture of
his collar bone. A plaster has been put on it. He should not
be disturbed. Prostrate at a distance and come away.’

“I was shocked to hear the news, but was eager to see
Bhagavan. I went on tiptoe and prostrated quietly. Bhagavan
saw me and said, ‘Narayana Iyer, come. Sit by my side on the
couch. Only then I can talk to you’. Implicitly I obeyed
Bhagavan in spite of Chinnaswami’s warning.

Bhagavan said, ‘I was going up the steps. A dog was
chasing a squirrel. I barred its way by placing my walking
stick in front of it. The stick slipped and I fell down and got hurt
on the collar bone. They say it is a fracture and native bone
setter of the village, an old devotee was sent for. He has put this
bandage with some green leaves and black gram paste and I am
enjoined not to move lest it be disturbed.’ Bhagavan narrated
the incident as though it was someone else’s body that was
injured and was suffering.”3

4) In Talks with Ramana Maharshi, Munagala
Venkataramaiah writes (Talk: 229, 8-8-1936):

“At 8 am. the pet squirrel was watching for an
opportunity to run out. Bhagavan remarked, ‘All wish to



Bhagavan – The Compassionate Friend of Squirrels 229

rush out. There is no limit to going out. Happiness lies within
not without”4

Thus the relationship between Bhagavan and his squirrel
friends continued from the Gurumurtham days to Virupaksha
cave, Skandasramam and finally to Ramanasramam. But
Bhagavan was very sad and anguished when he had to move
to the new hall in front of Mathrubhuteswara temple, to
accommodate the growing number of devotees. It was hard
for Bhagavan to accept the change. He felt that it practically
meant denial of entry to these animal friends especially the
squirrels as they had no access to his couch. Bhagavan would
send his attendants to feed them at meal times and would
enquire about their well being.

CHAPTER – SIXTEEN

BHAGAVAN AND INSECTS

Bhagavan showed his concern even for insects. He
discouraged devotees from killing insects unless the latter were
causing or about to cause, injury or suffering to people or animals.

When a visitor maintained that one should not kill any
kind of insect life, Bhagavan replied, “When you cut vegetables
and cook, you cannot avoid killing a few insects. If you think
killing worms is a sin, then you cannot eat vegetables.”

If Bhagavan saw people deliberately killing harmless
insects, he would usually show signs of disapproval. One day
for example, a small boy came to the hall and began to catch
flies and kill them by clapping hands together or squash the
flies between his palms, just to amuse himself.

Bhagavan told him, “Don’t kill flies like this. It is a sin.”
Bhagavan strongly opposed the killing of all forms of

life including even snakes and scorpions. All higher forms of
life, including dangerous and poisonous animals, were
sacrosanct.

1. BHAGAVAN AND HORNETS
Once, Suri Nagamma asked Bhagavan whether he had

gone up the hill only after seeing the Banyan leaf and was it
then that the hornets had stung him.

Bhagavan narrated the incident of the Banyan tree and
hornets:



232 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

“Yes, yes. One morning unintentionally I came down
the hill from Virupaksha cave and was going round the
hill, when it occurred to me that I should go up the hill by
a short cut between Panchamukha temple and
Pachaiamman temple. It was all a big forest. While I was
feeling my way, a big banyan leaf drifted across my path.
That one leaf was as big as the leaf we stitch together with
several Banyan leaves to eat food on. When I saw that
leaf I was reminded of the sloka in the Arunachala
Puranam where there was a description of the Banyan
tree under which Arunagiri Yogi was living.”

“What is that sloka?” asked a devotee. Bhagavan
thereupon recited that sloka which when translated means
as follows:

“On the northern slope of Arunachala hill is the big
banyan tree whose shade is large and circular in shape.
The tree which catches the eye of gods and human beings
alike is gazed at in wonder by them. Lord Siva is reputed
to sit eternally under this banyan tree in the form of a
‘Siddha’ called Arunagiri Yogi.”

Bhagavan then continued:
“As soon as I was reminded of that sloka, I thought

that this leaf must be from that banyan tree and so felt that
I could see the tree if I went along the direction from which
the leaf came. I started climbing up further and saw a tree
on an elevated spot. As I was going along to it, my thigh hit
against a bush. On account of the disturbance, the hornets
in the bush came out and began stinging me. I thereupon
thought that I had committed an offence (offence of



Bhagavan and Insects 233

disturbing the hornets) and that was the punishment. So
thinking, I stood still. The bees did not sting me at any
other place than the thigh that had touched the bush. They
bit me to their fullest satisfaction. After they left me, I began
walking. Curiously enough I forgot all about the banyan
tree and wanted to reach the place of the seven springs. But
there were three big streams in between which were very
deep. The thigh too had swollen and was paining. I somehow
crossed the three streams, and reached the seven springs.
From there I began to descend the hill and reached the
cave of the Jataswami by evening. Till then I had no food,
nothing whatsoever. There they gave me a tumbler full of
fruit mixed with buttermilk and jaggery which was like
Amritha. I drank it and took some rest. After some time I
went to the Virupaksha cave and stayed there for that night.
The leg got more swollen. Neither Jataswami nor the others
noticed it, but Palani Swami saw it and asked, ‘What is
it?’ And I told him all that had happened. Next day, as he
applied some gingili oil to it, he found that there was a
spike as strong as a wire nail in every place I was stung.
With great effort he took out every one of them and gave
some treatment. The swelling subsided after two or three
days.”

Suri Nagamma asked Bhagavan, “Did not Bhagavan make
any efforts afterwards to trace the place of the Banyan tree?”

Bhagavan replied, “No, the thought never came to my
mind again.”

In later years when Bhagavan narrated this incident
several times, some of his devotees like Kunjuswami,



234 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Venkataramaiah, Muruganar and others went in search of the
big banyan tree and had to return disappointed. They had to
endure many difficulties as they had lost their way. Due to
Bhagavan’s Grace, they returned to the Ashram safely.1

Years later, when Muruganar heard about the incident
from Bhagavan, he wrote a question in the form of a verse:

Sighting an overgrown, green leaved bush, and
When stepping on it stung by hornets to have legs

swollen,
Venkata, in truth, why was an accidental intrusion
Treated without mercy, just as a wanton transgression?

Bhagavan responded likewise in the form of a verse:
When I was stung by hornets in revenge
Upon the leg until it was inflamed,
Although it was by chance I stepped upon
Their nest, constructed in a leafy bush;
What kind of mind is his if he does not
At least repent for doing such a wrong?2

(This is taken from The Collected Works of Ramana
Maharshi. The original work is in Tamil. It is translated into
English by Arthur Osborne.)

2. AN INSECT IS RESCUED
This incident was narrated by Bhagavan’s attendant

Sivananda Swami to Sivaraman, Sri Vishwanatha Swami’s
brother. It shows how keenly observant and profoundly
compassionate Bhagavan was. Sivananda Swami narrated:

“It might have been in the morning at about 10
o’clock. Bhagavan was returning from the goshala. I was
with him. When he neared the well which was next to the
hall, he noticed some peculiar sound coming out of the



Bhagavan and Insects 235

well, similar to the flapping of wings by a bird or a large
insect struggling in the water. Bhagavan stood still and
looked into the well and found that it was a large insect
with wings. Bhagavan told Sivananda Swami, ‘Please see
whether it is in a playful mood fluttering its wings on the
water or struggling to escape drowning. If the former, don’t
disturb it. If it is the latter, please quickly release it from the
water.’

“Seeing that the insect was struggling, I reached the
insect with a long bamboo pole. The insect sat on it, came
out and was rescued. It flew out joyously!”1

3. MOSQUITOES
Mosquitoes were a perpetual problem for devotees.

Bhagavan did not criticise devotees if they swatted
mosquitoes which were biting them. In the 1940s he even
permitted the cowshed to be sprayed with pesticides, so
that the cows would not be troubled by the biting
mosquitoes or insects. However, if he was questioned about
the moral aspect of killing mosquitoes, he would usually
answer by saying that one should not identify with the body
that is being bitten.

A devotee who asked him about this got the reply:
“If you were to take your complaint against

mosquitoes to the court of law, the mosquitoes would win
the case. Their Dharma i.e. the rule they must live by is
to bite and sting. They live on the blood sucked after
biting. They are teaching you that you are not the body.
You object to their stings only because you identify yourself
with the body.”1



236 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

In Day by Day with Bhagavan (10.5.46) Sri Devaraja
Mudaliar writes about an incident that happened in
Bhagavan’s hall:

“One afternoon Krishna Jivrajani asked Bhagavan:
‘Suppose there is some disturbance during meditation like
mosquito bites, should one persist in meditation and try to
bear the bites and ignore the interruption or drive the
mosquitoes away and then continue meditation?’

“Bhagavan answered, ‘You must do as you find most
convenient. You will not attain Mukti simply because
you refrain from driving the mosquitoes away. The thing
is to attain one pointedness and then to attain
Manonasa. Whether you do this by putting up with the
mosquito bites or driving the mosquitoes away is left to
you. If you are completely absorbed in your meditation
you will not know that the mosquitoes are biting you.
Till you attain that stage why should you not drive
mosquitoes away?’” What a lesson it is to all seekers! 2

From the book Letters and Recollection of Sri
Ramanasramam,

One day when Bhagavan was in a reminiscent mood,
he talked about the time when he was in Virupaksha cave.
The devotees arranged to take Bhagavan to Tirupathi, as
mosquito menace that year was unusually serious. They fixed
an auspicious day for the journey, packed everything and
asked Bhagavan, “Bhagavan shall we start?” Bhagavan
answered, “Yes, do go and come back.” Then they asked
Bhagavan, “What about Bhagavan?” Bhagavan replied,
“Where can Bhagavan go? Where is he staying?”



Bhagavan and Insects 237

Then the devotees said, “We are unable to bear this
mosquito problem.”

Bhagavan said, “If you are unable to bear it you may go
and come back. Is it for my sake that you are going? Did I say
that I was not able to bear the mosquito nuisance?”

As there was no use in arguing, the journey was cancelled.
In those days Bhagavan was not covering his body except for
the Kaupeenam. So the devotees begged him that at least to
cover his body with a shawl. Bhagavan agreed only when he
was given an old rug of Pichandi, the son of Jayaraman, a
devotee and Pichandi was given a new shawl in exchange.
Unwillingly the shawl was exchanged with an old rug.

Bhagavan covered himself with the old rug. That was
the beginning of his covering the body with anything.
Bhagavan said, “When I began to cover myself with that prickly
type of rug, the mosquitoes did not dare to come anywhere near
me and it used to be warm too.”

ANTS

4. KOLAM WITH LIME POWDER
On a festival day in 1944, some ladies were decorating the

floor of the Ashram with rice paste prepared by grinding the
soaked rice in water. While returning from goshala, Bhagavan
remarked to the people following him, “Look at those people. See
what they are doing? They are concentrating their mind on this sort
of work. What to do? Let them carry on. Be careful not to step on
the drawing. Why should we step on them when they are drawing
the kolam with such great devotion?” So saying he walked carefully
without disturbing the designs and sat in the hall.



238 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Immediately after that, Bhagavan noticed that an old lady
of the Ashram was carefully drawing design with dry lime
powder on the floor below the steps opposite the hall. Bhagavan
called her by her familiar name. The granny came with great
expectation. Bhagavan said, “Look here granny, you are taking so
much trouble in decorating the floor with that powder. Is it rice
powder?” When she replied that it was lime powder, Bhagavan
said, “What a pity! It will not be useful even for the ants. The ladies
there also are doing the same thing. Their work is also of no use. It
is mere waste of time. The paste they are using is made of rice dough
and so ants can eat it. Decorating the floor really means feeding the
ants. If that Dharma is given up and powdered lime is used not
only the ants cannot eat it, but if by mistake, they come anywhere
near it, they may die because of the strong pungent smell. Why all
that? Please add at least some rice flour.”

An Andhra gentleman enquired “Is it for feeding the
ants that in the Dhanurmasa (i.e. the period between middle
of December to middle of January), that the floors are
decorated with rice powder?”

“Yes, of course,” replied Bhagavan, “Out of their feelings
of happiness and joy at the receipt of the fresh crop of rice, they
decorate the floor with rice powder thus feeding the ants. Practices
laid down by the elders are always based on kindness to living
creatures. But who cares for those traditions now? They do most
things just for the sake of beauty only.”1

5. PRASAD FROM TERMITES
During the Pathalalingam days, Bhagavan’s thighs were

eaten away by insects and termites (white ants). The poison
of termites stayed on in his body.



Bhagavan and Insects 239

One evening, Kunjuswami, Bhagavan’s attendant,
accompanied Bhagavan up the hill. He noticed some
secretion, like puss, oozing out from the upper portion of his
thigh. Bhagavan wiped it out with his small towel.
Kunjuswami was nonplussed. Bhagavan explained with a
smile, “Yes! It is the white ant’s Prasad for Swami!”

Bhagavan told Kunjuswami that the oozing used to occur
twice a year, but it was not noticed by others. This oozing
stopped completely in 1940s.

6. AHIMSA
This incident was told by the sisters Shantabai and

Sakhubai:
“The sisters Shantabai Narayanaswami and Sakhubai

Sreenivasan came to Bhagavan as young children. Their
father Gopala Iyer and his wife were Bhagavan’s devotees.
Once, during the holidays Gopalan’s family came to
Ramanasramam. While the children were playing in front
of the Darshan hall, Shantabai playfully threw a stone at a
wasp. The stone hit the wasp. The enraged wasp came
back and stung Shantabai on her hand. She had a burning
sensation and it was very painful too. She started crying
loudly.

“Bhagavan, who was in the hall, could hear the child’s
painful cry. His heart melted for that child’s suffering.
He asked the attendant to bring the crying Shantabai to
the hall. When the child was brought to Bhagavan, he
asked the child how and what had happened. The child
narrated to Bhagavan that, while playing she had hit a
wasp with a stone and it suddenly came back and stung



240 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

her. Bhagavan asked Shantabai if it was a red wasp or a black
one. The child retorted, ‘Don’t you know that black wasps
are found in bamboo plants? Here there are only mango
trees and only red wasps are found.’ The dialogue between
Bhagavan and the child took a few minutes. Then
Bhagavan took the child’s hand and started massaging the
hurting hand. Immediately the burning sensation and also
the pain subsided. The child asked Bhagavan, ‘Why did
you take so long to take my hand and massage it? If you had
massaged my hand earlier the burning sensation and pain
would have subsided then by itself!’ Bhagavan told her
consolingly, ‘Child, I did not massage your hand earlier
because I wanted you to experience how it had hurt the wasp
when the stone you had thrown had hit it.’”

Shantabai now living with her son in Surat says, “I have
not forgotten what Bhagavan taught me that day. Since then
I have not knowingly hurt any living being.” 1



Bhagavan and Insects 241

CHAPTER – SEVENTEEN

ABHAYAM SARVA BHUTHEBHYAHA

(SECURITY AND COMPASSION FOR ALL BEINGS)
Life in whatever form was sacred to Bhagavan, since one

consciousness pervades all beings. He saw Sat-Brahman in all
beings. He would say hurting life in any form is like self-injury.
Hence he would show the same consideration whether it is
human being, animal, plant or even insect. To drive home the
point that life is one, Bhagavan once remarked:

“You may call a tree a standing man;
and a man a walking tree.”1

1. ECHAMMA’S BILVA POOJA
Echamma was one of Bhagavan’s earliest devotee who

was preparing food for Bhagavan daily. Bhagavan was finding
the food prepared by her very tasty as it was stuffed with love
and devotion. He would not start eating his meal unless the
food sent by Echamma was served to him.

When Bhagavan was in Virupaksha cave, Echamma
installed a photo of Bhagavan in her home. She decided to
do puja with a lakh (1,00,000) of tender leaves of Bilva. She
informed Bhagavan before starting the puja. By the time she
had offered fifty thousand leaves in puja, the leaves in the
tree got exhausted (as it was summer)and she could not gather
more leaves even though she wandered all over the mountain.

She got tired and went to Bhagavan to pour her
grievances.



Abhayam Sarva Bhuthebhyaha 243

Bhagavan said, “If you cannot get the leaves, why not pinch
yourself and do puja?”

Echamma said, “Oh that will be painful!” Bhagavan
said, “If it pains you to pinch your body, is it not painful to the
tree when you pinch it to pluck the leaves?” Echamma turned
pale and asked, “Why did you not tell me earlier, Bhagavan?”

Bhagavan replied, “When you know that pinching the
body is painful, how did you not know that the tree will be
equally pained if you pinch and rob the tree of its leaves? Do I
have to tell you that?”1

That tender leaves should not be cut from trees is also
stated in the Seventieth sloka in ‘Devikalottara Stotra’ in
‘Jnanachara Vicharapadalam’, the meaning of which is roots
should not be pulled out. Leaves should not be harmed.
Flowers should not be plucked.

2. TULASI LAKSHARCHANA
Another time, when a devotee wanted 1 lakh (one

hundred thousand) Tulasi leaves to perform an elaborate
puja, she did not escape Bhagavan’s wrath.

“People will not allow the leaves to remain where
they are, beautiful and alive. They pick them, pierce them,
thread and kill them. Does God need such worship? Don’t
they belong to him when they are on the plants or trees?
We do these horrible things to flowers, but we would not
offer ourselves to God.”1

3. DOES IT NOT PAIN THE COCONUT TREE
One morning Krishnaswami, Bhagavan’s attendant, was

plucking down the ripe coconuts from the trees with a stick



244 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

while Bhagavan was returning from the goshala. Bhagavan asked
Krishnaswami, what type of stick he was using to pluck the coconuts,
whether it had a bamboo bit attached to the end or an iron point.
Krishnaswami remarked that it was only an iron sickle.

Bhagavan asked; “Will not the trees be hurt by the sharp
iron? Would not a stick with bamboo bit at the end serve the
purpose? ” And Bhagavan did not wait for the reply.

Krishnaswami went on plucking the coconuts with the
iron sickle every morning.

A week later, at the same time as on previous occasion,
while Krishnaswami was plucking the coconuts from the trees,
one of them fell on his forehead and struck his nose very
painfully. This news was reported to Bhagavan.

While expressing his pity and concern for the man,
Bhagavan also remarked, now he will know what it is to be
hurt, and how much this iron sickle must have hurt the
uncomplaining trees.

This is how Bhagavan finds all nature pulsating with
life and light!1

4. TREES ALSO HAVE PAIN
Bhagavan’s kindness and solicitude even extended to the

vegetation. There used to be an almond tree to the north of
Bhagavan’s hall. Chinnaswami, asked a workman to clear it
of dead leaves, which could be used later for making leaf
plates. The man started chopping with his billhook the leaves
of the tree, indiscriminately right, left and centre.

Bhagavan who was observing all these winced and called
out to the man, “Hey! What are you doing? You are torturing
the tree too much. Don’t you know the tree is alive?”



Abhayam Sarva Bhuthebhyaha 245

The workman said he had been ordered by
Chinnaswami to cut-off dry leaves.

Bhagavan kept on admonishing the man, “You people
can do nothing without causing pain. Imagine what would
happen if I suddenly grabbed your hair and pull it. Your hair
may have no life, yet you would feel it. Better leave the poor tree
alone and go away! ”1

Once, Bhagavan saw somebody cutting a twig from a
Neem tree in the night to use it as a tooth brush the next
morning.

Bhagavan asked him, “Can’t you let the tree sleep in peace?
Surely you can take your twig in the day time. Why not have a little
sense and compassion? A tree does not howl, nor can it bite or run
away. Does it mean one can do anything to the tree?”2

5. DO NOT BEAT THE TREE
One morning, during the mango season, coolies had

been assigned to pluck the mangoes from the Ashram tree.
They were gathering the fruits by beating the tree with a
stick to knock down the mangoes. They were doing so, instead
of climbing the tree and plucking the mangoes one by one.
In the course of beating, along with the fruits, mango leaves
were also falling in heaps.

Bhagavan could hear the sound of beating even while
seated on the sofa. He sent word to the coolies, through his
attendants not to do so. He then went out as usual and saw
mango leaves in heaps. He could not bear the cruel sight.
He curtly told the coolies in a harsh tone, “Enough of this!
Now go! When you have to gather the fruits, do you have to beat
the tree so that the leaves fall off? In return for giving us fruits, is



246 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

the tree to be beaten with sticks? Who gave you this work? Instead
of beating the tree, you might as well cut it to the roots. You need
not gather fruits. Go away!”

Bhagavan’s voice which was like a thunder, reverberated
in the ears of all those who were there and made them tremble
with fear. The bamboos that were aloft were brought down
and placed on the ground. The coolies stood with folded
hands like statues. They had no words to speak.

Suri Nagamma who was a witness to this incident
observes, “When I saw the personification of kindness
towards nature in an angry mood, my heart beat violently
and my eyes were full of tears. Can one who is so moved by
the falling of leaves of a tree bear pain in the minds of human
beings? Bhagavan Ramana is indeed karunapoorna
sudhabdhi, the ocean filled with the nectar of Karuna. Can
he bear the sufferings of human beings? By the time
Bhagavan returned from the goshala side, the devotees had
gathered leaves into a heap, and begged him to forgive
the fault. Bhagavan went into the hall saying, “How cruel!
See how many beatings were showered on the tree! How big
is the heap of leaves! Oh! ’ One could feel the pain he had
in his voice.”1

6. LET THE FLOWERS BE ON THE PLANT
Tiruchuzhi Lakshmi Ammal, a childhood friend of

Bhagavan visited the Ashram often. Bhagavan treated her
with affection and informality and called her by name.

Bhagavan often went on giripradakshina. Once when
he went on giripradakshina with his party, Lakshmi Ammal



Abhayam Sarva Bhuthebhyaha 247

was also in the group. When the party reached Sona
Thirtham (next to Ganesha temple on the South West side
of the hill), they decided to rest there for some time. Lakshmi
Ammal spotted a flowering tree nearby and started plucking
flowers. After resting for a while, Bhagavan and others
resumed their giripradakshina. When Bhagavan saw Lakshmi
Ammal plucking the flowers, he approached her and asked,
“What are you doing Lakshmi?” Lakshmi Ammal replied, “I
am just plucking flowers for the puja.” She had spread the
towel on the ground and had collected the flowers in it.

Pointing to the towel, Bhagavan said, “You already have
so many flowers. Why pluck more?”

Lakshmi Ammal replied, “There are so many flowers
on this tree. I thought it is better to gather them for puja,
rather than let them all be wasted.”

Bhagavan said, “So! I suppose you cannot perform puja
without lot of flowers. You have already seen and enjoyed the
sight of the tree laden with beautiful flowers. Now you do not
care whether anybody else gets a chance to enjoy the same heart-
warming sight or maybe you have some exclusive rights over this
tree. Did you plant it and nurture it yourself, thus making it
your own personal property?”

Lakshmi Ammal was stunned by Bhagavan’s words. She
was full of remorse for her thoughtless action. This incident
made such an impression on her, that from that moment
onwards, she never plucked flowers from the plants. Every
time she saw beautiful flowers on a bush or a tree, she would
mentally dedicate it to the Lord, saying, “O, Lord! This is
yours. Accept these flowers as my humble offering to you!”



248 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

Lakshmi Ammal used to tell others that ever since that
incident, she was reminded of Bhagavan’s word every time
she saw a flowering bush.

The verse 70-71 of Devikalottara (Translation of which
is in the Collected Works of Bhagavan) says, “It is not advisable
to pluck even a flower, without a good reason.”1

Our Bhagavan’s life was an illustration of this all-
encompassing love and care which could not tolerate even
the pain inflicted upon flowers!

Some other incidents:
1) Another time somebody was stripping a tree of all

its flowers. Bhagavan was quite indignant.
“Why not leave some flowers for the tree. It likes them.

You people pluck the flowers to the last bud as if they were
something very precious rather than allow them to wither
and perish on the tree. They would live a little longer on
the tree. I really cannot understand why you are so cruel.”1

We make a lot of fuss about human beings, but we are
indifferent to insects and plants. But for Bhagavan all were
equally alive and equally important. Hence he could feel the
pain of even a blade of grass.

7. DO NOT TOUCH THOSE SEEDS
From a very young age, the devotee Kanakamma’s

cousin Radhakrishna and his father were also devotees of
Bhagavan. Radhakrishna was interested in the spiritual
path and had great devotion and love for Bhagavan. He
had a lot of liberty to converse freely with Bhagavan and
was very close to him. Even after he went to Madras for
studies, he kept regular correspondence with Bhagavan.



Abhayam Sarva Bhuthebhyaha 249

He spent all his holidays at Ramanasramam with
Bhagavan.

On one occasion, when Radhakrishna had come to the
Ashram, Bhagavan left for Palakotthu. Bhagavan always
walked barefoot. He walked slowly and steadily not caring
about the heat though the stones on the path were often
burning hot. He would urge the attendant and other people
who would walk with him to run and stand under the shade
of a tree. The devotees therefore decided to plant some shade
giving trees along the path to Palakotthu. They had dug the
rows of pits along the path and also had seeds ready to be
planted. They wanted Bhagavan to plant the seeds with his
hands and so were waiting for him to come there.
Radhakrishna was also walking with Bhagavan.

When Bhagavan came to the spot, he saw the pits and
the group of devotees gathered there. He asked, “What is going
on?” The devotees explained their plans and requested
Bhagavan to plant the seeds with his own hands. When
Bhagavan was about to pick up a seed from the tray, there was
a loud shout from Radhakrishna running towards them,
shouting, “Bhagavan! Bhagavan! Please don’t touch the seeds!”
Bhagavan exclaimed, “What is this? All of you told me to sow
these seeds. Now Radhakrishna is telling me not to touch them.
What am I to do?” Everyone turned to Radhakrishna, waiting
for an explanation for his behaviour. By this time Radhakrishna
was in tears. In between sobs, he stammered, “Bhagavan! Do
you know what these people are planning to do? They want to
grow shade giving trees along the path. But if you touch those
seeds, the whole purpose of the exercise would be defeated!



250 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

One look from your eyes is enough to set a man free from the
cycle of birth and death. When this is so, what will happen to
those seeds if your hands touch them? Will they not attain
liberation immediately? How, can they grow into trees?”

Everyone was stunned by Radhakrishna’s explanation
for his strange behaviour. Bhagavan gave him a keen look,
but did not say anything. Actually Radhakrishna’s fear was
well founded. Just a few months earlier, Radhakrishna,
Kunjuswami, and Bhagavan had planted some onion seeds
in the kitchen garden. As they were planting seeds, they were
jocularly remarking among themselves that they would watch
out as to whose seeds would grow into the healthiest plants.
A few days later, the seeds sown by Kunjuswami and
Radhakrishna had sprouted to healthy little plants. But none
of the seeds sown by Bhagavan had germinated. When
Radhakrishna narrated this incident and explained the reason
for his apparently strange behaviour, the devotees were
thrilled and moved with emotion. Bhagavan too appeared
to be pleased by Radhakrishna’s perceptive observation. But
he did not say anything. He moved on to Palakotthu.1

8. DELIVERANCE TO A THORNY BUSH
When a visitor asked Bhagavan about the possibility of

animals being liberated, Bhagavan sat quietly for some time
and then said, “It is not true that birth as a human being is
necessarily the highest or that one attains realization only from
being human. Why can’t other creatures attain liberation?”

So saying Bhagavan told the tale from Chidambara
Mahatmyam about a great soul conferring liberation to a
thorn bush:



Abhayam Sarva Bhuthebhyaha 251

“In Chidambaram, there was a Jnani by the name
of Umapathi Sivacharya. As he was in an extraordinary
state of spirituality, he did not pay any attention to the
usual Brahminical practices. Hence, the Pandits of the
place became angry with him; especially since he was a
very learned man and knew all the precepts of the Hindu
religion. They forbade him to live in the village or even
visit the temple!

“He therefore lived in a hut built on a raised ground
outside the village. A low caste man called Pethan Samban
used to supply him with all that he required and helped
him in a general way.

“One day, Pethan was carrying on his head a bundle
of firewood to the hut of Dikshitar (of the temple). Iswara
(the Lord) Himself met him on the way, in the guise of the
Dikshita in charge of the temple, wrote a verse on a Palmyra
leaf, and gave it to him saying that it was to be handed
over to Umapathi Sivacharya and then disappeared.

“Pethan gave the verse to Sivacharya, who on
opening it, found in the first line itself the words,
‘Adiyarkkadiyen Chitrambalavanan (the servant of the
devotees, the Lord of Chidambaram). Sivacharya was
overwhelmed with devotion and a thrill passed through
his body as he read the letter. The gist of the verse was a
note from Chidambaranathan – the servant of the
devotees - to the person who has set up a new establishment,
namely Sivacharya. It is your duty to give initiation to
this Pethan Samban regardless of caste and to the surprise
of all people’.



252 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

“He read the letter and was overwhelmed with joy.
In obedience to the orders of the Lord, he initiated Pethan
into the order of sannyasis, though Pethan belonged to
the lowest caste and in due course he gave ‘Nayana Diksha’
(transmission of power through eyes) to Pethan
immediately after which Pethan vanished into holy light.
Sivacharya himself was immensely surprised at this
occurrence and only then understood the wisdom of
Pethan.

“Enemies of Sivacharya noticed sacrificial offerings
and other things he was having for these initiations and
complained to the government that Sivacharya had
burnt Pethan to death for some mistake. When the king
came there with his retinue to enquire into the
complaint, Sivacharya showed the verse from Lord
Nataraja and said he gave initiation to Pethan and
that Pethan vanished thereafter in the form of divine
light (Jyoti). The king was surprised and asked
Sivacharya if he could likewise give initiation and
moksha to the thorn bush nearby. ‘Yes, what doubt is
there?’ said Sivacharya. Accordingly he gave nayana
diksha to the thorn bush and that too immediately
disappeared in pure light (Jyoti).

“The king was still more astonished at that and said,
‘This looks like black magic. You said this note had been
written by the Lord Nataraja. Let us go and ask him.’
The Guru Sivacharya said that he was forbidden to enter
the temple. The king said that it would not matter as he
himself was accompanying Sivacharya.



Abhayam Sarva Bhuthebhyaha 253

Accordingly they started for the temple. The Pandits,
common people curious about the whole thing and the
enemies of Sivacharya who were sure he would be duly
punished – flocked to the temple to see the strange sight.
As they entered the temple, and when out of regard to the
king, aarati was offered to the Lord Nataraja, it was found
that, Pethan stood on one side of the Lord and on the
other the thorn bush.

The Pandits were surprised and out of fear and
remorse fell at the feet of Sivacharya, requesting him to
pardon them for all their faults. They subsequently brought
him back to the village with due honour.

This story is found in ‘Chidambara Mahatmyam’.1



254 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

CHAPTER – EIGHTEEN

BHAGAVAN IN THE KITCHEN
After Sri Ramanasramam was established, more and more

people started coming to the Ashram as the devotees and seekers
did not have to climb the hill to have Bhagavan’s darshan.
Some spiritual seekers like Subbalakshmammal, Shantammal,
Sampoornamma, Sundaram, Natesh Iyer and others who had
come to Tiruvannamalai to pursue their spiritual practices
under Bhagavan’s guidance through force of circumstances
had to take up kitchen responsibility. Although they were quite
happy to serve Bhagavan in this way, sometimes frustration
peeped in, as they were confined to the kitchen all the time
and could not attend Bhagavan’s satsang in the hall like others.
Bhagavan understood this. Therefore after his early morning
ablution or while returning from the goshala, Bhagavan would
go to the kitchen and spend as much time as possible in helping
them with kitchen work. He would give suggestions and
instructions both in cooking and also in spiritual matters.

Bhagavan tried to ensure that the Ashram animals
received good quality food and not just leftovers. Krishna
Bhikshu was reminded of this when he spoke to Bhagavan
in the dining room. At meal time Bhagavan would ask to be
served very little. He would carefully clear the plate of the
last grain of food before getting up.

Although he never asked others to do the same, Krishna
Bhikshu once asked him, “Bhagavan, if we clear our dining



256 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

leaves so scrupulously, the dogs, the monkey, the cats, and
the rats, the ants, and other animals will starve.”

Bhagavan answered, “Well, if you are so compassionate,
why not feed the animals before taking food yourself? Do you
think they will relish your scrapings?”1

In those days, in the Ashram, three times a day the kitchen
bell rang, before the meal time and a small portion of all dishes
including iddlies were taken out to feed the cows, the monkeys,
the dogs, the peacocks and the other birds including crows.
Before taking his meals, Bhagavan would walk around the
Ashram to see if all the animals were fed well and see if the
cattle were being looked after well. They were given good
quality of food. Peacocks and squirrels were fed with groundnut
kernels. If that was not done first, Bhagavan would not come
for food. If anyone showed disinclination to serve animals in
this way, Bhagavan would not tolerate it and say, “All right, go,
if you like. Animals have come in the same way as we have all
come, and they will get their food as we all get it. You want to
serve us with respect, saying, ‘Swami, Swami,’ but you serve them
with curses. Did we purchase them and bring them here? They
have come just as we did. Why show disrespect to them?” Bhagavan
would also ensure that the beggars were fed properly.2

Once Sampoornamma narrated an incident that
happened in the kitchen:

“One day Bhagavan was cutting vegetables in the kitchen.
A visiting lady devotee was also cutting vegetables sitting in
front of him. Suddenly the lady devotee had a cut in her finger
as the knife was very sharp. The blood oozed out. She told
Bhagavan, “Bhagavan the knife is very sharp. It cut my finger.”



Bhagavan in the Kitchen 257

Bhagavan replied, “Amma, it was your mistake; do not blame
the knife. While working, you had no concentration on your
work and because of your carelessness, you gave your finger to
the sharp edge of the knife. So you got hurt. Is it not your mistake?
Before we put the blame on others, should we not check if the
mistake lies with us or not?”

Is it not a lesson to all of us!3
Another time, as usual Bhagavan started for a walk towards

Arunachala. On the way, there were some steps. While crossing
over these, his foot slipped and was injured. Some devotees
examined the steps and found them uneven. Immediately some
masons were called and by the time they started work, Bhagavan
returned to the spot and observed some portion of the steps
were being cut off. “Why?” Bhagavan questioned, “Why do you
cut off the steps? The steps did nothing. It is only this foot which is at
fault. Cut off this foot”. How different is the saintly reaction! 4

The above incidents show that it was not only for the
living beings like animals, plants, birds etc that he had concern
but also for inanimate objects like stone and knife. He saw
Brahman in everything. Do we need better examples than
these of equal vision?

SOURCE
Content Reference

1. Bhagavan in the Kitchen 1 Ramana Smrithi – Souvenir,
Published by Sri Ramanasramam,
Ch - Bhagavan I Knew.

2 Letters from Sri Ramanasramam, by
Suri Nagamma -7.2.1948.

3  As told by Sampoornamma.

CHAPTER – NINETEEN

EQUAL VISION
(Samadarsatvam)

The following incident is recorded by Suri Nagamma
in her book Letters from Sri Ramanasramam:

On the 2 May, 1949, Mandalabhishekam of
Matrubhuteswara temple was performed. 21 May was
Mahapuja day. A day before Sarvadhikari requested
Bhagavan to come and stay in the new hall for two days.
He came to the new hall on the 20th afternoon and sat there.
Just after Bhagavan’s arrival Suri Nagamma entered the hall
and saw Bhagavan seated on the sofa. She found his face
looking sad, not radiant as usual. She wondered why but
she did not ask anybody.

After Sarvadhikari’ left the hall, Suri Nagamma went
and prostrated before Bhagavan and got up. There was
Bhagavan’s attendant Sivanandam alone near him and two
or three persons sitting in front of Bhagavan at a distance.
Bhagavan was looking intently at the ceiling of the hall and
at the huge stones that were chiselled outside. Bhagavan
looked at Nagamma and with a voice full of kindness, said,
“Do you see this? They have imprisoned me within these four
walls. They have made me a prisoner by preventing other people
from coming in.” So saying Bhagavan looked up at the
ceiling, which had no outlet or inlet anywhere and said,
“How can they (squirrels) come here?” He continued to stare



Equal Vision 259

at the ceiling. Nagamma stood absolutely dumb-founded
on realisizg his way of looking at things. Sivanandam, who
was close by, said, “The Sarvadhikari and others feel that if
Bhagavan is here, he will be protected from rain or hot
sunshine outside.” Bhagavan whose look was concentrated
on the ceiling, was startled when he heard those words and
looking at Sivanandam, said, “That’s alright. If we look to
our comfort, is it not at the expense of the suffering of others?
Squirrels, monkeys, peacocks, cows and others have no chance
of coming here. Does it not mean we have deprived them of
all their privileges? People think that it is great happiness for
a swami if he is here, what is to be done?” Bhagavan’s voice
became tremulous. The attendant took up the thread of
the conversation and said, “Yes that is true. Only human
beings can come in; animals – and birds cannot come in
freely.” Bhagavan did not say anything.

After some time, some rich devotees came and sat opposite
Bhagavan. One or two poor people came after them but were
afraid to come in. Noticing this, through the windows,

 Bhagavan told his attendants:
 “There you are. Look at those people. You said there

was every convenience for men to come in. Is there scope for
all people to come in? Rich people are accustomed to see
huge buildings with lights, fans, collapsible doors and other
imposing furnishings, and so they may come unhesitatingly.
But poor people like me will hesitate to come in, for they
feel that it is a place where only rich people live. They are
afraid of what people would say if they come in, and so, go
away quietly like those people who, as you see, are peeping



260 Bhagavan Ramana – The Friend of All Creation

through the windows. Where is the place for them here? See
those poor people! What a pity!”

Unable to say anything further Bhagavan resumed silence.
As soon as it was evening, he sent away some of his

attendants saying that the evening was the time when all
of them (monkeys, peacocks, etc.) come for their food.
“They may perhaps think that ‘Swami’ has given them the
slip and gone elsewhere. Please go. What a pity! Go, give
them at least some food and come back.” As soon as the
attendants returned after feeding the animals, Bhagavan
remarked with tremulous voice, “Have you fed them all?
They will perhaps feel that Swami has deserted them and
has gone away to a better place and is sitting there so that he
alone can be happy. Perhaps they thought I had forgotten
them. There is no scope for them to come here. What to do?”
Whenever any animal or bird came to Bhagavan, he would
always say, “We do not know who they are (Mahatma might
have come in that form)?” He would never look at them
with indifference. If any of the attendants did not give
them proper attention, he would not tolerate it, but would
say, “That is bad. You merely see the skin that covers the
body but not the person within. You feel that you are great
and others are small and so you try to drive them away. They
(animals and birds) have come here just as we have come.
Why do they not have the same right as we have?” He used
to admonish the devotees who looked down on animals
and birds. It is not surprising that Bhagavan felt
compassion towards the animals and the poor who did
not venture to come into the new hall with those lights,



Equal Vision 261

fans, iron doors, guards and other paraphernalia.
Samadarsatvam i.e. looking at all beings with equality was
but natural to Bhagavan.

SOURCE
Content Reference

Equal Vision Letters from Sri Ramanasramam by
(Samadarsatvam) Suri Nagamma, 22-5-1949.



CHAPTER – TWENTY

AHIMSA MURTHY

Smt Mahalakshmi Suryanandan, daughter of
Bhagavan’s long time staunch devotee Sri. K. Swaminathan
has been coming to Bhagavan since she was a teenager. She
has written a number of devotional songs in Tamil on
Bhagavan. The following song ‘Ahimsa Murthy’ is from her
book Sri Ramana Paamalai, which tells about the Divine
relationship Bhagavan had with his animal companions. The
song is a free translation by Smt. Gita Gopalkrishnan, a
devotee of Bhagavan Ramana. Sketch: Sri Padmavasan.

Was it that more than humankind - beasts and birds – had
engaged in deep

austerities?
Or was it that the great Ramana had exercised His

enchantment?

Lakshmi, the cow, refusing to step into her stall without
Ramana Bhagavan’s holy feet entering the goshala and
her birthing a calf every year on the day of His jayanthi,

Is this not the wonder of wonders?

What good fortune had the numerous squirrels received,
that ran up and down Ramana’s body and nibbled

nuts at His palm
every day?



Ahimsa Murthy 263

Whole families of monkeys would come in, noisy and
raucous in argument,

and would leave Ramana’s hall in peaceful harmony!

Karuppan and his fellow dogs, with their tails wagging,
in Ramana’s presence would play affectionately

with the cat!

With parrots cooing, koels singing, and bees humming,
peacocks, both white and blue, would dance in all

their splendour!

Crows and sparrows would build their nests and rear
their young ones,

and in Ramana’s protecting palm a cracked egg would
stay intact!

Cobras and snakes and other creatures from the wild
would circumambulate

the compassionate Ramana and would return the way
they came!

Let us sing the praises of the noble being of Ahimsa,
Ramana,

who taught us never to harm any living being, the
smallest insects,

birds, beasts or plants.

Let His grace fall on us as we bow down at His divine
feet!
